2. Check add to cart to initiate checkout against the target of 25-50%. With 12 add-to-carts, that means at least 3 to 6 checkout starts. [L1260.014]
3. Check initiate checkout to purchase against the target of 50% or higher. If it is lower, optimize the checkout. [L1260.014]
4. Fix only the stage that fails before moving on. [L1260.014]
5. Do not change checkout or other stages at the same time as the landing page, or the cause of a change cannot be identified. [L1345.004]

### Diagnosis order

- Diagnose a low conversion rate in this order and never skip ahead [L1456.001, older advice, 2021-22]:
  1. Evaluate traffic quality: look at search terms and whether visitors are the right people at the right stage of the buying journey. [L1456.001]
  2. Run competitor analysis: list every seller of the same product and compare price, returns policy, review count, site design and similar factors in a matrix. [L1456.001]
  3. Only then optimize the website and offer based on what the competitor comparison revealed. Doing the website first wastes effort and can make the position worse. [L1456.001]
- Speed and friction issues matter most when visitors arrive but do not add to cart. Fix these first before other changes. [L1260.037]
- If the landing-page add-to-cart rate is below benchmark, fix the landing or product page first and leave later funnel stages for now. [L1345.004]

### Diagnosis: low add-to-cart

- Check the landing and product page first, because speed and friction issues matter most when visitors arrive but do not add to cart. [L1260.037, L1260.014]
- Product pages should clearly show these points, because gaps explain why competitors win sales [older advice, 2020]:
  - stock status and shipping time
  - the shipping and returns policy
  - the price match policy
  - more and better images
  - descriptions that answer buyer questions

  [L1277.079]

### Diagnosis: low checkout completion

- Check initiate checkout to purchase against the 50% target. If it is lower, check the checkout, including trust elements, shipping display, taxes, currency, and payment methods. [L1345.010, L1260.061]
- When TikTok traffic adds to cart but does not buy, check the offer, the trust signals and the checkout in that order. High add-to-cart with low purchases usually points to checkout or trust level rather than the ad. [L1377.045]
- When an ad brings many checkout starts but no sales, place a test order yourself through the full checkout to confirm payment works before changing the ad. [L1426.046]
- If the checkout works, run the original ad again to see whether the low conversion came from the ad or from a checkout fault that has since been fixed. [L1426.046]

### Diagnosis: high add-to-cart with few purchases

- High add-to-cart with few purchases can be caused by a broad audience, for example TikTok audiences under 24 years old. Exclude non-buying age groups, such as 18-24, if they convert poorly, and test a stronger offer. [L1260.038]
- Example: a store with 18+ targeting had good add-to-carts but weak conversion. After removing the 18-24 age group, add-to-carts dropped but the conversion rate improved. The conversion rate is the goal to watch, not the add-to-cart count. [L1260.039]

### Diagnosis: high CTR with no sales

- If CPC is below $1, CPM is below $10, and CTR is above 1.5% but the store converts poorly, work on the store instead of the ads. [L1375.008]
- Test new offers, a new store design, and new copywriting. Change something on the store every day until the conversion rate reaches the target. [L1375.008]
- When a product gets clicks and spend but no sales, check the parts you control in this order: landing page first, then creative, then offer, and only then the product itself. Changing the product before the page is fixed leaves the real cause unknown. [L1382.071]
- Clicks and spend may not show sales on the same day. Orders can come from people who clip an ad and keep it until a need appears, so a good ad can produce sales months after it stops running. Plan for delayed responses when measuring an ad campaign. [L1366.071]

### Diagnosis: traffic but no sales

- Assuming a traffic problem when sales are missing is a mistake. If visitors arrive and do not convert, the issue is usually the store experience or the offer compared with competitors. [L1274.009, older advice, 2018]
- Diagnose in this order [L1274.008, older advice, 2018]:
  1. Check the bounce rate on the pages that receive traffic.
  2. Check whether shoppers add products to the cart and then leave without completing the order.
  3. Check whether visitors are comparison shopping and leaving for better deals elsewhere.
  4. Re-research the top competitors, focusing on the pages already getting traffic, to see what they do differently.
  5. Only after you know what competitors offer, add incentives such as bonus gifts or expiring coupon codes to make buyers choose your store.

  [L1274.008]
- Case: a second store with over 100 products, running Google Ads and working on SEO, had been open since April, received a fair amount of traffic, and had zero conversions. The previous Black Friday produced zero revenue. The recommended diagnosis was a conversion problem, not a traffic problem: check the bounce rate and add-to-cart-then-leave behaviour, check comparison shopping, then re-research competitors on the pages that already get traffic. Set up email campaigns before peak season so non-buying visitors can be followed up. [L1274.084, older advice, 2018]

### Google Shopping troubleshooting

- The Google Shopping conversion rate should be around 2.5 percent or higher, roughly five times the 0.5 percent that signals a problem. Measure it on Google Shopping traffic only, not overall site traffic. [L1277.077, older advice, 2020]
- Conversion fix when Google Shopping traffic does not convert [L1277.078, older advice, 2020]:
  1. In Google Ads, list the products that receive clicks and where the spend goes.
  2. Search those products on Google Shopping the way a customer would, and note which competitors show up.
  3. Open the competitor ads and product pages, and compare them side by side with your own pages.
  4. Check how each competitor shows shipping timing and stock, shipping and returns policy, price match policy, number and quality of images, and product descriptions.
  5. Cut spend on products that cost money without sales and that you cannot compete on.
  6. Improve your product pages where competitors are stronger, then move budget to products that convert.

### Traffic versus store attribution

- Strong ad metrics do not prove the store works, so analyze the store separately. [L1345.001]
- Use the funnel stages to decide whether a problem sits in traffic or in the store. Ad clicks with a healthy landing-page add-to-cart rate point to later stages; a 1-2% add-to-cart rate points to the landing or product page. [L1260.061, L1260.014]
- Measure ad effect with delayed responses in mind, because a good ad can produce sales months after it stops running. [L1366.071]
- Do not treat a traffic problem as the cause of missing sales without checking the store. [L1274.009, older advice, 2018]

### When to kill a product

- Do not conclude that a product is dead after the first day or two of traffic. Low sales often come from fixable problems in the offer, images, trust elements, or checkout, so optimize each level before giving up. [L1345.005]
- Before cutting a product, check the parts you control in this order: landing page, creative, offer, then the product itself. [L1382.071]
- Cut spend on products that cost money without sales and that you cannot compete on, then improve product pages where competitors are stronger before moving budget to products that convert. [L1277.078, older advice, 2020]

#### Further details

- In Phase 5 (Days 60-75), after 48-72 hours of ad testing, diagnose whether poor performance is caused by an ad problem (weak creative or ineffective targeting) or a funnel problem (low product page appeal, unclear offer, or checkout friction); this diagnosis determines which system requires optimization effort. [L0199.021]

## Customer research, feedback and behavior analytics

### Why people buy and why they leave

- Study why customers buy before designing the store and the offer, because the psychology determines what the page must show. [L0914.008]
- Market research and emotional framework analysis improve pre-landing pages, bottom-of-funnel ads, the website and overall funnel performance. Scaling advertising works best when every message addresses the actual psychological needs of the target customer. [L0221.013]
- Customer knowledge improves conversion because the offer and messaging then match the points customers need to see before buying. It also shows which traffic channels to use, by focusing on the places where ideal customers are most often online. *Older advice (2021-2022):* [L0872.007]
- The first customers reveal the real buyer profile. A niche with a specific buyer group can justify building a page for that group, and designing the store around the first customer type can open a repeat business channel. [L0360.023]
- Monitoring visitors online works like standing in a physical store and observing customers: track how many enter, how long they stay, what they examine, and what they purchase or avoid, then improve based on observable patterns. [L0293.012]
- Improvements based on what one visitor tells you benefit all future visitors, so conversion gains compound over time. [L0208.018]
- Objections from visitors who browsed but did not buy, found in complaints, comments and feedback, show the real hesitations that stop purchases. Use them to guide page copy, FAQs and ad messaging. [L1268.008]

Example of a primary customer desires framework for a financial product (example: social security):

| Desire | Urgency | Notes |
|--------|---------|-------|
| Not to be the fool who left money on the table | 9/10 | Loss-aversion-driven; stronger than gain-framed version |
| Certainty that the irreversible decision is right | 10/10 | Peaks sharply in the 90 days before filing |
| Protect the spouse who will outlive me | 8/10 | Highest emotional intensity; lowest current awareness |
| Enough monthly income not to be afraid | 7/10 | Chronic, background, always-on desire |
| Avoid a permanent Medicare penalty | Variable | Sharp spike at age 64.5-65 |
| Understand it well enough to stop feeling stupid | High | Dignity desire; massively powerful |
| Get money owed that I didn't know about | High | Fairness-based; divorced-spouse survivor angle |
| Retire before my health goes | High | Drives early claiming against standard advice |
| Not be a burden on my children | High | Identity and family role protection |
| Beat the system that I think is unfair | High | Fairness desire; fuels 'claim early' behavior |

[L0221.021]

### Diagnosing from the traffic side and from visitor signals

- When conversion problems are hard to diagnose from the inside, ask visitors and customers directly for feedback. Owners often cannot see their site objectively because they are emotionally attached to it, so customer answers replace internal assumptions. *Older advice (2021-2022):* [L0682.001]
- Collect feedback to find recurring patterns across many answers, not to react to a single opinion. One response never justifies a site change, while a problem named by a meaningful share of respondents deserves a plan. *Older advice (2021-2022):* [L0682.002]
- Asking customers where they found the site or why they contacted support is a useful signal, but few stores do it, so it is too thin to diagnose a low conversion rate on its own. Do not wait for that feedback. Start the diagnosis on the traffic side and work through the funnel. *Older advice (2021-2022).* Both sources apply together: ask customers when you have enough volume to hear them, and start on the traffic side when feedback is thin. [L0473.031, L0682.001]
- In Google Analytics, go to Behavior and open the page as a landing page. Check bounce rate, time on site, time on page, the number of visitors who continue to other pages, and the number who exit from it. *Older advice (2021-2022):* [L0473.018]
- Average session duration above 15 seconds indicates customer interest in the product. A session under 15 seconds means the customer left early because of weak images, offer or messaging. [L0321.012]
- A high bounce rate on a Google Shopping product page can be normal, because these visitors already know what they want. If the page answers every buying question, check session recordings for frustrations rather than assuming the page is at fault. [L0804.084]

### Self-testing the store

- Take a week away from the store, then open it as a real customer and try to buy a product, for example pants. Note what prevents you from completing the purchase at each step. Note what demotivates you, such as slow loading, unclear prices, missing shipping information or too many pop-ups. Ask a good friend who will be honest to buy a product while thinking out loud, and watch where they hesitate. Do not rely on family members for honest feedback. [L0390.017]
- Make a purchase yourself on your own store. If you would not buy, note why and change it. If you could not buy, note why and fix it. [L0423.016]
- If the store looks perfect and you still see no problems, get an outside view. Ask a friend to go through the buying process and have them think out loud, so you hear their hesitations. [L0423.017]

### Choosing a feedback method

| # | Method | Channel |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Post-purchase survey | Email, sent a set time after purchase (separate from the review request) |
| 2 | Customer service survey | Phone or chat support, asked before the call ends |
| 3 | Exit-intent survey | Pop-up when a visitor is about to leave |
| 4 | On-site survey | Single question sliding onto the page, or on-site survey tool |
| 5 | Online chat survey | Automated chat that asks questions or links to a survey |
| 6 | Browse abandonment survey | Email triggered by a browse abandonment event |

[L0682.003]

- Method choice depends on order volume and traffic. At around 100 customers per day, asking existing customers about the site is practical. Without customers, the business must reach visitors who have not bought yet. *Older advice (2021-2022):* [L0682.004]
- Introduce feedback collection gradually. Start with one or two methods and add more over time, since running all six at once is unnecessary. Sites with different order volumes and traffic levels will find different methods work best. *Older advice (2021-2022):* [L0682.024]
- Post-purchase survey answers come only from people who already bought, so they cannot show why non-buyers left. Their suggestions for improvement still help make future visitors more likely to convert. *Older advice (2021-2022):* [L0682.027]
- Hotjar is a behavioral tracking tool that also offers on-site surveys that appear on the page. It is the recommended tool for on-site surveys. *Older advice (2021-2022):* [L0682.018]
- Google Forms is free for off-site surveys. Paid alternatives are Typeform, RightMessage and SurveyMonkey. Typeform can be embedded directly on a Shopify page so the visitor stays on the domain. *Older advice (2021-2022):* [L0682.020]
- Keep surveys and testing running as an ongoing process. Stopping once the first round is done wastes insight into customer friction, so conversion work should be a constant improvement process. *Older advice (2021-2022):* [L0399.017, L0399.018]

Setting up the off-site customer survey (*older advice, 2021-2022*):

1. Create emails that invite visitors, opt-ins, pre-purchase prospects and existing customers to complete the survey on your site experience.
2. Choose questions from the survey template list.
3. Build the first survey with these questions.
4. Use the survey in the emails.

[L0399.015]

Implementation steps for customer feedback (*older advice, 2021-2022*):

1. Set up one off-site survey (e.g. Google Forms or Typeform).
2. Add the survey email and a reminder into the post-purchase thank-you email sequence.
3. Choose one on-site survey method and implement it (Hotjar survey, exit-intent pop-up, on-site pop-up, or online chat survey); test variants later to improve response rate.
4. Task the customer service team to ask key questions during calls, especially pre-purchase.
5. Set up a Q&A tracking tool and give the right people access.
6. If browse abandonment tracking is used, set up a browse abandonment feedback email.

[L0682.022]

### Survey design rules

- Off-site surveys should be short, with three to five questions and a maximum of five. Three is a good number. State clearly that the survey takes only a couple of minutes. *Older advice (2021-2022):* [L0682.010]
- Collect at least 50 responses per question before drawing conclusions or hypotheses. Aim for 100 responses per question for conversion-related decisions. *Older advice (2021-2022):* [L0682.011]
- Make each survey question specific enough that every possible answer leads to a clear conclusion. Use yes/no or 1-to-10 rating questions where they are enough to decide whether a problem exists. Use open-ended questions where nuance is needed to find a solution. *Older advice (2021-2022):* [L0682.012]
- Recommended pattern: if 10 out of 50 respondents name the same problem, treat it as a pattern worth acting on and plan an improvement for that area. *Older advice (2021-2022):* [L0682.013]
- On-site surveys should ask one single question at a time, appearing as a slide-in on the page. Rotate the question over time: keep one question for a few weeks, then switch to another after it has run for a while. *Older advice (2021-2022):* [L0682.008]
- Exit-intent or timed pop-ups for feedback can be shown when a visitor leaves or after about 30 seconds to a minute on the site. Ask for a short survey of about two minutes and explain its purpose. Expect only a minority of visitors to respond, so the survey must run long enough to build useful data. *Older advice (2021-2022):* [L0682.007]
- Asking visitors why they did not buy with an exit-intent pop-up: only about 5% of visitors usually answer, but the answers can reveal issues such as unclear shipping regions or missing information. Fix these in the FAQ or on the page. [L0390.022]
- For an automated chat survey, the chat can trigger on specific pages or after a set time, then run a series of questions in the chat. Each answer triggers the next question, and the answers appear in the chat software. The alternative is a chat message that links out to an external survey, which sends the visitor away from the site. *Older advice (2021-2022):* [L0682.009]
- Survey length depends on where the survey appears. Off-site surveys: three to five questions (*older advice*, see above). Single questions fit moments of hesitation, such as the cart or checkout page, and the last abandoned checkout email. Sources differ on count, so match the count to the channel: one question at the point of hesitation, three to five in a full off-site survey. [L0682.010, L0423.019, L0605.043]

Template for a cart or checkout feedback poll (one short question, asked at the moment of hesitation):

```
Place a feedback poll on the cart or checkout pages that asks: What's stopping you from completing this purchase? Keep the question short so visitors answer it at the moment they hesitate.
```

[L0423.019]

### Post-purchase and order confirmation surveys

- Add a short survey to the Shopify order confirmation (thank-you) page, using an app or a free Google Form, and send the responses to Google Sheets. Customers are most willing to answer right after buying. [L1204.024]

Post-purchase survey for the order confirmation page (answer fields can be multiple choice or short text):

```
1) Why did you buy from us?
2) What was the most important factor for you when choosing a [niche product]?
3) Are you looking to buy any other [niche products]? If yes, which ones?
4) Which websites, podcasts and YouTube channels do you follow?
5) How did you hear about us?
Replace the bracketed parts with your niche. Use the answers to plan monthly offers, find new products to add, and find new advertising channels.
```

[L1204.025]

- Use a small prize draw on the survey, for example one monthly cash winner, to give buyers a reason to answer. State on the survey that answers are anonymous. [L1140.011]
- On the first screen of a post-purchase survey, state the reward clearly: a monthly cash prize such as $1,000 for one respondent per month, plus a note that all answers are anonymous. A chance to win costs the buyer nothing, so the prize is a reason to answer rather than a payment per answer. [L1140.031]
- Survey invitation formula: reward + time needed + number of questions + anonymity note. It works because it lowers the perceived effort and risk of answering, which raises completion rates for post-purchase surveys.
  - Example 1: Tell us why you bought and get 10% off your next order. Three quick questions, under 60 seconds, and all answers stay anonymous.
  - Example 2: Two short questions about your results, about 45 seconds, and one buyer each month gets a $50 gift card.

[L0906.008]

- Post-purchase survey rewards in the Source D example differ from the older Source A case: a 30 to 50 USD gift card was offered for about 30 minutes of time in a pre-launch interview. Choose the reward by the effort asked: short surveys fit a small prize or discount, while long interviews need a clear payment. [L0390.031]

### Abandoned checkout and exit follow-up

- Add the survey question to the last abandoned checkout email, after the other emails have addressed price, offer and trust. Ask what stopped the customer from buying and send a simple plain text email rather than an image-heavy one. Split test the survey in the last email against the first email to compare the replies. [L0605.043]
- Surveys are less useful at the start of an abandoned checkout flow, because customers stop for different reasons and each email must address them all. Once the flow already answers trust, price and offer objections, a survey in the last email shows what remains unsolved. [L0605.044]
- Call customers who abandoned checkout to ask why they did not complete the order, and record the answers as qualitative data for improving the store. Do not call on Sundays, since many customers do not welcome calls then. *Older advice (2021):* [L1090.069]

### Session recordings, heatmaps and device checks

- Start the Lucky Orange free trial only after active traffic is coming to the store, not during development. You can cancel within the first six days to avoid being charged. [L0293.009]
- Use Lucky Orange to record individual sessions and heatmaps. Recordings show where visitors click, hesitate and exit. Analyze drop-off points after 150 to 200 sessions to decide whether to improve images, offer, trust factors or product fit. [L0321.013]
- Recordings show whether people add to cart, click the bundle, or leave at the product page, which helps diagnose a funnel problem. [L0950.031]
- Lucky Orange helps find clicks on non-clickable elements or actions you do not want. Use those recordings to make optimizations to product pages. [L0835.079]
- Heatmap review, page by page:
  1. Open Lucky Orange and review heatmaps page by page to identify where visitors click most heavily (deep orange areas).
  2. Verify whether areas with high click activity are actually interactive or meant to be.
  3. For non-clickable areas receiving heavy clicks, redesign that section to be clickable or clarify its purpose.
  4. Check your Add-to-Cart button specifically: it should display as deep orange (most-clicked area).
  5. If the Add-to-Cart button is not deep orange, enlarge it, change its color, or increase its visual prominence.

  [L0293.011]
- Use session recordings to find device-specific bugs, such as a feature that works on desktop but fails on one particular Android phone. Check the recordings for that device, then fix the layout or function and test it on the same device again. [L0390.054]
- Online visitor monitoring works the same way as observing customers in a physical store: you track entries, time spent, what they examine, and what they buy or avoid. [L0293.012]

### Live chat as a signal

- Questions in live chat are a signal of conversion problems. When customers ask for variants that are not available, the variant structure may be confusing. Splitting variants into separate landing pages can clarify what is on offer. [L1315.065]

### Competitor and customer research

- Competitor product-page analysis, step by step:
  1. Find your previously best-selling product pages in your store.
  2. Search each product name on Google.
  3. Open the product pages of the top competitors that appear in the results.
  4. Check whether they changed anything compared with last year, and identify the changes that help them overcome obstacles you also face.
  5. Repeat this regularly, because competitors keep adapting and the process becomes an ongoing back-and-forth.

  [L0472.074]
- Find the most visited product pages in the store analytics, which are the products receiving the most clicks from ads. Search each product on Google and review the results as a shopper would. Compare competitors on price, product photos, product descriptions and shipping speed. Ask why a shopper would choose the competitor over your store, then adjust your product page so it beats the competitor on those points. [L1083.012]
- Analyze competitors at product-page level:
  1. Pick competitor stores in your niche.
  2. Open their product pages side by side with your own.
  3. Note what competitors do well and where they are weak.
  4. Turn each competitor weakness into a strength on your own product pages.

  [L1116.043]
- Before comparing a product across two stores, make sure both listings are the same variant, for example a 12-light model against a 12-light model. Load both product pages with the same SKU or name so the comparison of price, images and policies is fair. [L0894.042]
- Competitor research findings should directly inform conversion rate optimization work, not stay as a standalone document. Review the research before making any conversion-related change so that changes respond to the market. *Older advice (2021-2022):* [L0898.007]
- Customer research case, skincare startup selling its own sustainable cosmetics: it used user feedback tools to form hypotheses before redesigning its website. It spent about 3 months building the new design, then about 3 more months tweaking it. Before launch it asked its top 10 customers for feedback, offering a 30 to 50 USD gift card for about 30 minutes of their time. After the redesign, the store reached a 5% conversion rate for new visitors and 8% for returning customers. [L0390.031]

### AI-driven CRO audits: principles and scope

- Run an AI-assisted audit of the full customer journey before hiring an agency or buying complex CRO testing tools. This fits brands doing roughly $500K to $2 million a year that have not yet completed the basic best-practice review of every page from homepage to checkout. [L0886.025]
- The target store profile for this audit is a brand doing roughly $500K to $2 million in annual revenue that has not yet done the foundational best-practice review of its full customer journey. [L0886.010]
- ChatGPT Projects can hold the audit instructions and input metrics as sources, with each prompt run in its own chat. Claude can be used the same way. Projects are a container that keeps all three audit prompts and their outputs together. [L0886.005]
- The audit should cover five customer journey pages: homepage, product page, collection page, cart page and checkout. The criteria are clarity, trust, friction, offer, product page conversion, homepage orientation, mobile experience, objection handling and conversion prioritization. [L0886.009]
- Audit instructions should demand specific, not generic, recommendations. Prioritize customer clarity and trust over cleverness. State that the output must explain the commercial reason for each recommendation and be usable in call notes, Slack recaps or a CRO checklist. Include that the AI should assume most traffic is mobile and flag small-screen friction. [L0886.006]
- When metrics, screenshots or copy are provided, the audit assistant should first ask only for the most important missing information, then proceed with the audit using the data given. [L0886.008]
- Input metrics to supply to the audit: conversion rate, average order value (AOV), traffic by channel, returning customer rate, add-to-cart rate, checkout completion rate, Meta ROAS and MER (marketing efficiency ratio). [L0886.011]
- The example brand's checkout completion rate of 54 percent was flagged as a key leak, because the audit uses input metrics to decide which opportunities come first. [L0886.012]
- Fictional or approximated metrics can test the audit flow. For real results, the input metrics should match the actual store as closely as possible, including the URL and real copy. [L0886.013]
- Feed screenshots of the homepage or product page into the audit whenever AI output hallucinates or is inaccurate, so it can give deeper, more precise feedback. [L0886.014]
- Run the audit prompts one section at a time, rewriting these separately rather than all at once: homepage hero, above-the-fold messaging, product page headline, benefit bullets, FAQ and objection handling, offer framing, shipping and returns clarity, and trust badge placement. [L0886.015]

### AI-driven CRO audits: setup procedure and prompts

Running an AI-assisted CRO website audit with three separate prompts in one project:

1. Create an AI project named 'CRO website audit' and add two sources: the audit instructions (a system-style brief defining the auditor role, the audit criteria, and rules) and an input-metrics document.
2. Fill the input-metrics document with the homepage headline, subheadline, primary CTA, key homepage messaging themes, promotional banner examples, social proof, collections, hero product and its positioning, average price range, the metrics conversion rate, AOV, traffic by channel, returning customer rate, add-to-cart rate, checkout completion rate, Meta ROAS and MER, the top customer objections, the main promotional offer, example bundles, the primary target customer, a secondary audience and their psychographics.
3. Open a separate chat for each prompt: Prompt 1 is the customer journey diagnosis, Prompt 2 rewrites weak sections (one section at a time is recommended), Prompt 3 builds the 30-day roadmap.
4. If the AI output is inaccurate, feed it screenshots of the homepage or product page and ask for a deeper look.
5. Before making major theme or website changes, duplicate the theme so you can revert if something breaks.
6. Turn the roadmap into delegated tasks per team member if you have a team.

[L0886.001]

Input metrics document for the audit (start with text only, add screenshots later if the output is inaccurate):

```
- Store URL
- Homepage copy: hero headline, subheadline, primary call to action, key homepage messaging themes, promotional banner examples
- Social proof: ratings, testimonials, and how many customers trust the brand (historical or current)
- Collections and hero product, including how the hero product is positioned
- Average price range
- Key metrics: conversion rate, AOV, traffic by channel, returning customer rate, add-to-cart rate, checkout completion rate, Meta ROAS, MER (marketing efficiency ratio)
- Top customer objections: the reasons people hesitate or do not buy
- Main promotional offer and example bundles
- Target customer as specifically as possible, secondary audience, and their psychographics
```

[L0886.026]

Prompt 1, customer journey diagnosis:

```
Take on the role of a seasoned senior CRO specialist for ecommerce and review this store as someone landing on it for the first time. Pinpoint the largest places where visitors drop off, covering clarity, credibility, the deal itself, the product page, the mobile version, points of friction, handling of hesitations, and what pushes someone to buy. Rank these problems by how much revenue they likely cost, and justify each ranking.
```

[L0886.002]

Audit assistant instructions (the auditor brief):

```
You are a compact, AI-driven assistant for ecommerce conversion rate optimization and customer journey auditing. Your job is to examine the path a buyer takes through an online store so it becomes easier to follow, more believable, less effortful, and more likely to convert. Review these areas:
1. Clarity: does a visitor immediately grasp what the brand sells and why that matters?
2. Trust: is there enough confidence from reviews, social proof, press coverage, the founder's story and similar cues?
3. Friction: where does the shopper have to work too hard?
4. Offer: is the deal attractive and simple to understand, including bundles, discounts and free shipping?
5. Product page conversion: does the product page clearly lay out what the item is, its advantages, its use cases and its objections?
6. Homepage journey: does the homepage orient visitors quickly, build credibility and explain the product category?
7. Mobile experience: assume most visits come from phones and point out anything on a small screen that could slow people down.
8. Objection handling: what must a customer know before purchasing, and does the site answer that plainly?
9. Conversion prioritization.
The five pages in scope are the homepage, product page, collection page, cart and checkout.

Guidelines:
- Be concrete, not generic. Do not hand over a list of stock suggestions.
- Put customer clarity and trust ahead of cleverness.
- Rank recommendations by impact, effort and confidence.
- Do not suggest A/B testing by default if the brand probably lacks the traffic to reach a meaningful result.
- If a change is obvious and low-risk, label it a 'fix' rather than a 'test'.
- Explain the commercial reason behind each recommendation.
- Keep advice workable for a small, lean ecommerce team.
- Aim for a higher conversion rate, higher average order value, stronger customer confidence and more completed purchases.
- Write in plain language that can go straight into call notes, a Slack recap or a CRO checklist.
- Rewritten copy should convert while staying true to the brand voice.
- If information is missing, say what you would need to sharpen the audit.
- When screenshots, website copy or metrics are provided, first ask only for the most critical missing item if one is needed. Otherwise, carry on with what was supplied.

Output layout: open with an executive summary. Then give the customer journey diagnosis with an impression of each core area, the biggest trust gaps, the friction points, copy and messaging improvements, and finally a prioritized action plan.
```

[L0886.024]

### AI-driven CRO audits: example result

- In an example audit of a premium hair-tool store priced at $149 to $299, the biggest leak was price skepticism. First-time visitors did not see why the product was meaningfully better than well-known branded tools or marketplace tools. The highest-impact opportunities were: clarify the value proposition above the fold; add stronger science and proof for the core technology and damage reduction; add direct comparison content against branded premium tools, marketplace tools and standard hot tools; improve objection handling on the product page; make the offer easier to understand; and reduce checkout hesitation, which was flagged because checkout completion was only 54%. [L0886.030]

## Matching traffic to awareness stage and page type

### Awareness stages and the customer journey

#### The five-stage model and the pyramid

- *Older (2021-22):* Customers move through five awareness stages before buying: Unaware, Problem Aware, Solution Aware, Product Aware and Ninja Aware (also called Most Aware). Each stage is a mental step from first being in the market to deciding on a specific product. [L0904.001]
- *Older (2021-22):* A four-stage version of the same model runs problem aware, solution aware, product aware and most aware, in that order. A product page converts visitors who arrive at an earlier stage only if it walks them through all the missing stages, because a price or buy button means little to someone who has not yet accepted the problem or the solution. [L0473.032]
- *Older (2021-22):* The whole potential market fits into the awareness pyramid. The Unaware group is the largest segment at the base, and the segments get smaller toward the top, where Ninja Aware buyers are the fewest. [L0904.002]
- *Older (2021-22):* The customer awareness pyramid is also called the customer journey, and marketers use the two terms interchangeably even though they are not exactly the same. The sales funnel describes the same stages turned upside down, with top of funnel at the pyramid's base and bottom of funnel at its tip. [L0481.019]
- *Older (2021-22):* The awareness pyramid runs from completely unaware prospects at the bottom, through problem aware and product aware, to ninja aware buyers at the top, who are ready to purchase now. Traffic and landing pages should be matched to the stage each prospect is in. [L1392.015]

#### What each stage looks like

- **Unaware:** *Older (2021-22):* Unaware people match the profile of a future buyer but do not yet know they have a problem or desire that the product solves. Reaching them requires messaging that first makes them see the problem. [L0904.003]
- **Problem Aware:** *Older (2021-22):* At Problem Aware, people recognize a pain and start looking for a solution. Pain is caused by problems, and people either run away from pain or pursue pleasure, so the pain is the entry point for messaging. [L0904.004]
- **Solution Aware:** *Older (2021-22):* At Solution Aware, people know a broad type of solution to their problem and need to learn which option fits them, possibly among several solution types. This is the largest group in the market at any time, but most of them are not ready to buy because they still need education to feel safe purchasing. [L0904.005]
- **Product Aware:** *Older (2021-22):* At Product Aware, people know several specific products that solve their problem, including your product and competitor products, but have not yet chosen one. They are still comparing products before making a final buying decision. [L0904.006]
- **Ninja Aware:** *Older (2021-22):* At Ninja Aware, people have learned their options, the desire for a solution has been built, and they know the exact product they need. They are ready to buy at this stage. [L0904.007]

#### Why the stage decides the destination

- *Older (2021-22):* Roughly 10 percent of the total market is ready to buy at any given time, which is only the Ninja Aware segment. The earlier awareness stages hold much larger shares of the market. [L0904.008]
- *Older (2021-22):* Product pages that only target Ninja Aware visitors reach a small pool of people. Visitors at earlier stages land on those pages, find nothing that speaks to their situation, and leave without buying. [L0904.009]
- *Older (2021-22):* Product Aware and Ninja Aware visitors already know the solution, so for them the decision mostly comes down to price, the best offer and the best deal. Simple copy with a clear deal and a bit of urgency works only for this group. [L0904.010]
- *Older (2021-22):* Serving people with good copy and the right product information at each awareness stage increases the likelihood of purchase. This raises the store's conversion rate. [L0904.019]
- *Older (2021-22):* Buyers choose the type of solution before they compare specific products. For example, a beginner who needs a surfboard first decides between renting and buying, then compares products. Product pages and copy should answer the solution-level question before the product-level comparison. [L0904.021]
- *Older (2021-22):* The landing page a visitor reaches must match the awareness stage that visitor is in at that moment. Sending problem aware or completely unaware traffic to a hard-sell product page ignores the buyer's need for education and usually yields few sales. [L0481.009]
- *Older (2021-22):* Under the 40-40-20 principle, the first 40 percent is targeting, so the audience you target determines which awareness stage must be served on the landing page. [L0481.010]
- *Older (2021-22):* Modern version of the rule: the first 40% depends on reaching the right audience in the right place, at the right time, with the right destination. The destination must match where the visitor sits in the customer journey, so cold social media traffic should not land on a high-ticket product page with a direct buy request. [L0408.005]
- *Older (2021-22):* Sending cold, top-of-funnel social media visitors who are unaware of the solution straight to a product page selling a 3000 USD product is expected to yield an extremely low conversion rate. The traffic may match the market, but the destination does not match the buyer's awareness stage, so the visitor needs to be moved toward readiness first. [L0408.006]
- *Older (2021-22):* A product page converts cold traffic only if it educates visitors from problem awareness through solution awareness and product awareness to readiness to buy. When visitors who are still problem-aware land on a product page without that education, the mismatch is usually a bigger cause of low conversion than the page design. [L0473.009]

#### Worked example: the awareness ladder for one product

- *Older (2021-22):* Example for a home office worker with back pain from long sitting: the person is problem aware of the pain, then solution aware that a standing-capable desk could help, then product aware of the electric height-adjustable desk type. Ninja aware means they have chosen one exact model, and only then do they decide which retailer to buy it from. [L0481.018]

### Matching the headline and page to the stage

- *Older (2021-22):* Match the ad headline exactly to the primary promise on the destination landing page. If sending to a product page, use the primary promise from product copy; for blog or sales pages, match the page headline. Mismatches cause high bounce rates and wasted ad spend because users expect immediate visual confirmation of what they clicked. [L0241.010]
- Message match between the ad and the landing page matters: if the ad shows one thing and the link goes to a clearly different product or topic, visitors leave immediately. [L0691.010]
- Keep the product page visually close to the ad, so a GIF of the product in use in the ad is followed by the same GIF on the page. This one-to-one match reduces confusion for the visitor. [L1194.025]
- Match your headline to the prospect's awareness of the product. A product-aware prospect should see the product named first, a desire-aware prospect should see the desire first, and a problem-aware prospect should see the problem stated specifically. Where a prospect stands on that scale decides how the copy begins. [L0523.056]
- Relevance example: a golf-targeted ad for sports leggings should lead to a page that talks about golf performance, such as how the leggings support stance and swing, instead of generic sportswear benefits. A visitor who sees their own activity named on the page feels understood and is more likely to buy. Build this kind of audience-specific variant after the first landing page test shows a winner. [L0918.035]

#### Diagnosing a low-converting page

*Older (2021-22):* Steps to fix a product page with a low conversion rate:

1. Identify the awareness stage of the traffic arriving on that page, which can be mixed; problem aware visitors can be recognized by search traffic about their problem.
2. Check whether the page moves visitors from their current stage to the next one.
3. If it does not, either segment marketing so problem aware visitors first reach a more suitable page, or add elements to the existing page that help visitors move through the stages.

[L0481.014]

#### Sequencing problem-aware and unaware visitors

*Older (2021-22):* For a problem aware or completely unaware audience, the sequence is:

1. Send the visitor to a content page first, which explains the problem and why it exists and shows how to solve it and what solutions exist.
2. Move them through email, retargeting or another page that introduces your products as solutions.
3. Finally lead them to the product page.

[L0481.013]

- *Older (2021-22):* Problem aware visitors typically arrive from Google searches about their problem and from social media posts that promote a product solving that problem. Because of this, traffic from these sources should usually land on an educational content page, not a product page. [L0481.017]

### Traffic temperature and bridges

#### Hot, warm and cold traffic

- Hot traffic consists of people who already know you, such as email subscribers, podcast listeners and blog readers. Speak to them personally and share stories, opinions and details of your life, because they already trust you. Such personal messages can generate large revenue from hot traffic. [L0523.053]
- Warm traffic reaches you through someone else's relationship, such as affiliates and joint venture partners. A personal endorsement from a trusted source, such as a lift letter, raises response greatly. In classic direct mail, list owners and sellers often split profits 50/50. [L0523.054]
- Cold traffic consists of people who do not know you, such as Facebook or pay-per-click visitors. These prospects need the longest bridge, and most sellers fail here because they speak in insider language. Plan a separate path for cold traffic instead of sending it straight to the offer. [L0523.055]
- Bridge length scales with coldness. Hot traffic needs only a quick link or email, warm traffic needs an endorsement from a trusted person, and cold traffic needs the longest bridge that educates the visitor before the offer. Choose the bridge from the traffic temperature. [L0523.057]
- Not all clicks are equal, because the context a visitor came from determines how they respond to your page. A visitor who has just read a glowing recommendation converts far better than one who has just read a hostile warning about you. Control the frame your traffic arrives through wherever possible. [L0523.050]
- Visitors from cold traffic often need several steps before they buy, so a path through trust-building landing pages can come before the purchase page. Plan the journey so each page answers one question the visitor has. [L0524.076]
- Traffic from different sources arrives at different awareness levels, so one landing page often cannot serve all of it. Plan up to three landing pages, one each for hot, warm and cold traffic, and send each traffic source to the page that matches its temperature. The lesson treats this setup as worth the extra effort. [L0523.135]

#### Pre-frames

- A pre-frame is the mindset you establish before a prospect moves on to the following stage. Every stage of a funnel serves as the pre-frame for the next one, which is why each should be tuned for both selling and credibility. Visitors who arrive with a strong frame convert at a much higher rate than those who arrive with a weak one. [L0523.048]
- The introduction a speaker receives strongly affects how much is sold at the back of the room. A bland introduction lowers energy and sales, while a strong pre-sell lifts them. Take control of the introduction with a short, prepared intro video that features endorsements and success stories, played just before going on stage. [L0523.052]
- Sell testimonials as pre-frames. Pay customers or students to write blog posts about their results, or ask them to publish video reviews that include links and keywords, then drive traffic to those pages. Visitors arriving through someone else's endorsement convert much better. [L0523.059]

#### Cold bridge pattern

Pattern: cold bridge page that translates a visitor's problem into the vocabulary of your offer. Start with a broad question about something the visitor wants, then explain the jargon in plain words, and finally show how the offer solves the problem with the new term.

Why it works: visitors only convert when they understand the terms the offer uses, so the bridge closes that knowledge gap.

- Example 1: "Want a free website that earns money?" followed by "To earn from it, people must visit, and visitors are called traffic. Here is how to bring them in."
- Example 2: "If you wake up with stinging feet at night, the usual cause is nerve damage called neuropathy. Here is what to do about it."

[L0523.058]

### Traffic sources and their fit with awareness stages

#### Traffic foundation

- A single traffic source is not enough for a reliable e-commerce traffic base. A business should combine several sources to reach prospects across the whole awareness pyramid. [L1392.002]
- *Older (2021-22):* A full e-commerce traffic foundation rests on five components: paid search ads billed per click, unpaid search engine visibility, retargeting campaigns, building an email subscriber list together with email marketing, and marketing through social media platforms. [L1392.003]
- *Older (2021-22):* Traffic should reach prospects at every stage of the customer awareness pyramid, not only at the most aware stage. Each stage needs a page and message that matches where the prospect currently stands. [L1392.004]
- *Older (2021-22):* Targeting only the most aware buyers is a mistake. That group is small, the most competitive, and often hard to convert, and if you are absent when they reach that stage, a competitor can take the sale. [L1392.005]
- *Older (2021-22):* The goal is omnipresence: being available to prospects at any point in the awareness pyramid so the market's attention stays on your brand throughout their decision. [L1392.006]
- *Older (2021-22):* The earlier a prospect is reached and given useful help, the longer they have to experience the business. That early exposure makes the brand more likely to be remembered when the prospect reaches the most aware stage. [L1392.011]
- *Older (2021-22):* The industry average is about seven touchpoints, meaning interactions with a business, before a purchase in e-commerce. This is only an average, and the real number varies a lot between businesses, with some needing more and some fewer. [L1392.012]
- *Older (2021-22):* Prospects who have already interacted with a brand several times are more likely to buy when they reach the most aware stage, because trust has been built. A brand that appears only at the last moment must rely on having a clearly better offer than the alternatives the prospect has already seen. [L1392.013]
- *Older (2021-22):* Each traffic source must be matched with how the website engages visitors from that source. The content and landing experience should fit the awareness stage the traffic comes from. [L1392.014]
- *Older (2021-22):* Some businesses can take a prospect from first visit to purchase in a single session, but many e-commerce businesses cannot. Plan for multiple visits, because the commonly cited industry average is about seven touchpoints before a purchase. [L1392.017]

#### Traffic source fit table

*Older (2021-22):* Traffic source fit by awareness stage (e-commerce, standard product pages):

| Traffic source | Completely unaware | Problem aware | Product aware | Ninja aware |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Google Shopping | Not suited | Poor (sends visitors to a product page, which rarely moves them on) | Good | Good |
| Social media | Good (turns them problem aware) | Good | Weak | Weak, except retargeting |
| Paid search text ads | Too expensive for meaningful volume | Too expensive for meaningful volume | Suitable | Suitable |
| Organic search (problem-aware keywords) | Not the main fit | Good, at much lower cost than paid | Suitable | Suitable |

[L1392.016]

#### Platform-specific matching

- Google Shopping and paid search text ads point to product-level listings. Search text ads convert best when the landing page matches the search term closely, usually a single product page for a specific term or a narrow collection page for a broader one. A collection with hundreds of products makes the offer hard to find and lowers the chance of a sale. [L0880.006]
- Broad or long-tail keyword traffic often converts poorly at first, so the visitor must be guided through several steps of value before buying. Expect a lower immediate conversion rate after a traffic boost ends. [L0949.059]
- Smart links let traffic from specific ads or influencers land on a customized landing page built for that source. Matching the page to the visitor's entry point is meant to improve conversion from the first moment on the site. [L0593.004]
- For influencer campaigns, keep the landing page simple. Use pictures or GIFs of the influencer using the product, add customer reviews, and offer a coupon code that the influencer shares with their followers. [L0789.029]
- A website with only product pages engages only shoppers who are already ready to buy. Reaching people earlier in the customer journey, across the customer awareness pyramid, requires content. [L0500.010]

#### Ad-type and destination matching

*Source D 2026, awareness ladder with ad types:*

| Awareness level | Typical ad type | Congruent destination |
|---|---|---|
| Unaware | Problem ad | Advertorial or listicle that educates first |
| Problem aware | Problem ad | Advertorial or listicle that educates first |
| Solution aware | Solution ad | Landing page or PDP to convert |
| Product aware | Solution ad | Landing page or PDP to convert |
| Most aware | Retargeting | Straight to PDP or offer, skipping the long story |

[L1019.018]

- Not every person is ready to buy immediately; some need more information first, so sending all traffic to a buy-now page is not always the best practice. [L1019.017]
- Problem ads aimed at unaware or problem-aware people should send traffic to an advertorial or listicle to educate them first. [L1019.019]
- Solution ads aimed at solution-aware or product-aware people should send traffic to a landing page or PDP to convert. [L1019.020]
- Retargeting aimed at most-aware people should go straight to the PDP or offer, without a long story. [L1019.021]
- For every ad set, write down its awareness level and check that its destination matches that level. [L1019.022]

*Source D 2026, same mapping with merged rows:*

| Awareness level of the ad | Send traffic to |
|---|---|
| Unaware or problem aware (problem ad) | Advertorial or listicle that educates first |
| Solution aware (solution ad) | Landing page or PDP to convert |
| Product aware | Landing page or PDP to convert |
| Most aware (retargeting) | Straight to the PDP or offer, skipping the long story |

[L1195.037]

- Match the ad to the landing page: cold traffic needs to read before it buys, while hot traffic buys before it reads. Sending every ad straight to a buy-now page is not always best. [L1195.038]
- Unaware audiences are very hard to convert directly. Pre-warm them with podcast-style content, listicles, or advertorials, while flipping-style VSLs suit product-aware and most-aware audiences. [L1195.039]
- Different awareness levels need different messages and formats, even inside one CBO. Running one format with many variations will not work across all awareness levels. [L1195.040]
- Read Eugene Schwartz's book Breakthrough Advertising (at least 30-60 minutes) to understand the five awareness stages and how emotional selling works. [L1195.041]

#### Display and retargeting audiences

- Use website visitor audiences because they show prior intent; cold traffic on Display is nearly impossible to make profitable, so only run Display and Discovery to people who have already visited your store. [L0210.028]
- Retargeting is where the most-aware visitor is served the offer directly; see the ad-type table above. [L1019.021]

### Page types: product page, landing page, homepage and collections

#### Definitions and when each fits

- A product page is a short page with a specific product picture, description, price, and add-to-cart button. A landing page has many sections such as header, banner, bonuses, icons, features, and reviews. A hybrid combines both and reduces a step before checkout, but it is not always better. [L0387.015]
- Products that need explanation, such as health products (for example for back pain), often convert better with an advertorial landing page that explains how the product works before the product page, even though this adds a step. Impulse-buy products that sell on looks should have the shortest possible path to purchase. [L0387.016]
- The product page must do the selling through its product description, product images, and product copy. The site's call to action in e-commerce is primarily to buy the product. The offer is delivered and presented on the website, not in the ad. [L1507.003]
- A product page should receive visitors who are already solution aware or product aware, not problem aware or unaware ones. Visitors who do not yet know their solution should first be sent to content that educates them, which then links to the product page. [L0396.027]
- Traffic must match the page it lands on: a product page is only right for visitors close to buying, so top-of-funnel search terms should go to content or collection pages instead. High-ticket buyers rarely make impulse purchases and need a longer decision process, so expect a longer gap between click and sale for generic traffic and plan for retargeting. [L1456.002]
- A website converts only if the offer and page match what the buyer needs at that point; a product page should not be expected to educate buyers about the whole market. Where a generic search term has no good fit, the fix is often content that compares options and then links to the product page. [L1456.005]
- For direct response dropshipping the main focus is the product or sales page at the purchase stage, because it has to ask for the sale quickly. Funnels and retention pages come later as the business grows. [L0919.003]
- Lead capture pages offer something of value, such as an ebook, white paper or webinar seat, in exchange for contact details. Long form sales pages suit higher-priced single products that need more explanation before purchase. [L0919.005]
- Roughly 95% of the paid traffic that lands on a product landing page decides there whether to buy or leave, while only about 5-10% look at other products. The landing page is therefore the most important part of the funnel after the ad click. [L0916.002]

#### Homepage versus product page

- Do not send paid traffic to the store homepage. Visitors cannot see the product right away or add it to the cart, which makes the buying path harder. Send them to a collection page such as a bestseller collection or directly to the product page. [L0562.027]
- A product page can be a better landing page than the home page when most traffic goes to the product page. Test whether direct-to-product traffic converts better before building a separate landing page for each product. [L0918.018]

#### Digital products and collections

- Send traffic from a single digital product to a product page with bullet points, a screenshot or mockup, testimonials and instructions for use on tablet or phone. Send traffic for preset-type products to a collection page. [L1431.062]
- Collection pages can serve as landing pages for new ad angles, such as a feature that customers care about, without building a separate landing page. The same pages can also be used for email segmentation and Google Ads campaigns. [L1389.020]

#### Variants and separate landing pages

- Individual listings for each variant work well when the variants look clearly different, for example vibrant colors that are easy to see. Send traffic to a collection page that shows these separate listings. [L1217.049]
- A dedicated landing page works better when the product has emotional marketing angles, such as love or mother and father gifts, or targets a specific demographic. Use GIFs and images to add emotion to the copy. [L1217.050]
- When product variants confuse shoppers or do not match what they expect, separate them into their own landing pages. This clarifies what is available and tends to raise conversion. [L1315.018]
- Case: A store that lacked currency settings and had confusing variants (one product page with mixed sizes or colors) had a conversion rate of 0.4% to 0.5%. After creating separate landing pages per variant group and setting up Shopify Payments with local currency checkout, the conversion rate rose to about 1.2%. A separate variant landing page helps when customers ask for options that are not available. [L1315.017]

#### Learning which page sends the most traffic

- Check each landing page's share of a brand's ads to see where its funnel really sends traffic. In the example, one product page received 125 ads (81.2%) and a second page received 8 ads (5.2%). [L0374.022]
- The Top Landing Pages list shows the share of a brand's ads linking to each page. In a drop shipping store the top landing page received about 3 percent of the traffic, while in established branded stores one landing page can receive around 30 percent. [L0892.020]
- A landing page that receives a large share of a brand's traffic is likely its best-performing page. Checking which page a competitor sends most traffic to is a way to find a proven lander. [L0892.021]

### Pre-sell pages: advertorials, listicles, editorials and presells

#### Gaps in competitor funnels

- If a competitor sends its paid traffic straight to product pages with no listicle, advertorial or pre-sell page, that gap is an opportunity. Build dedicated landing pages, advertorials or listicles that warm up cold visitors before they reach the product page. [L0374.021]
- When a competitor sends paid traffic straight to its product page, the gap can be an advertorial that agitates the problem further before presenting the product. Such a page can be a way to beat a direct-to-product competitor. [L0772.025]

#### Advertorials and editorial funnels

- An editorial funnel is a pre-sell page placed before the product page that mixes article-style content with advertising to turn cold visitors into warm prospects who are ready to buy. The reader gets useful information first and the product pitch comes last. [L0578.001]
- An advertorial is an ad styled as an editorial article that does not ask for the sale right away. A pre-sale or advertorial page warms up cold traffic by educating about the problem and the product and building trust, then captures data such as an email so the audience can be retargeted. [L0919.004]
- An editorial widens the reachable market because it answers objections and builds trust before any sales pitch, which converts people who would otherwise leave. It also reduces dependence on luck in ad targeting, since the page itself raises awareness of the problem and explains why the product is the best solution. [L0578.007]
- Use the 70/30 rule: about 70% of the page provides information and story, and only the final 30% introduces the product and the buying option. The reader must read the whole problem and solution before the pitch, so they are warm when they click. [L0578.016]
- Build the editorial on the AIDA sequence of attention, interest, desire and action, because each stage moves a cold reader one step closer to buying. Skipping a stage leaves the reader unconvinced at the point of the pitch. [L0578.017]
- For the desire stage, handle objections by explaining why existing solutions on the market fail to work well and why the new approach is different. The product can be presented as a new method without naming the brand early on. [L0578.020]
- Social proof in the desire stage should include a customer's photo and review, a results timeline (for example after one week and after two weeks), graphs or data, and a count of other customers who had good results. These elements make the claimed benefit believable. [L0578.022]
- In the action stage, present the product in the final 30% of the page and end with a clear call to action that leads to the product page, such as "check current availability" or "choose your package". The earlier stages must already have built agreement that the problem is real. [L0578.023]
- Putting the product image, price and buy button at the top of a page meant for cold traffic makes the visitor feel pushed, and the page does not build the trust needed to convert them. Lead with the problem and story instead. [L0578.024]
- Pitching the product before the reader agrees on the problem wastes the editorial, because the reader has no reason yet to care about the solution. Always begin with the shared problem. [L0578.025]
- A salesy tone in the first part of the editorial makes the reader leave, because the page stops feeling like useful content. Keep the sales language for the final part. [L0578.026]
- Editorial funnels are an advanced step, so add one only after the current funnel already converts well at a good level. The editorial is then used to win the prospect group that product pages miss, not to fix a weak product page. [L0578.034]

Case: A sleep-pain product store sent paid Facebook traffic to a story-style page instead of its product page. The headline promised a surprising solution to pain-filled sleep. The story then introduced a character who fits the ideal customer: someone who had a car accident years earlier and now has poor sleep and constant pain. The problem was agitated with photos, and the character then visited a doctor who recommended a foam pillow. The character tried it and her sleep improved over time, and the page showed other customers with similar results. The product was never pitched as the brand's own; the final button said "check current availability" and led to the product page. [L0578.035]

#### Presell pages and third-party pre-frames

- Presell pages educate prospects before the sales page. A presell page is a longer article that provides background or explains a concept, such as what email marketing is and why it grows businesses. Affiliates use presell pages to warm up prospects before sending them to a partner's sales page. [L0523.076]
- Articles placed on third-party sites serve as pre-frames for cold traffic from banner ads. Article types include a case study, a product explanation or a piece that frames the problem without mentioning you. Add the call to action as an inline link, a button at the bottom, a linked photo or a combination. [L0523.070]
- News-style pre-frames borrow attention from current events. Linking your message to a timely story, such as an election or disaster, lifts attention while the story is hot, though the effect is short-lived. Designing a page to resemble a news page with a headline bar or sidebar can also boost credibility, but it must remain truthful. [L0523.072]
- Blog posts are a pre-frame tool for almost any topic. A guest post on a high-traffic blog in your target market works better than your own blog, and the post is best published under the blog author's name. Pair it with a link to your offer and drive targeted traffic to the post. [L0523.073]
- Pre-frame videos work well for testimonials and for educating viewers. Publish them on someone else's channel and place calls to action in the description or comments, as pre-roll or post-roll footage, or as annotations. The video should agitate a problem or teach a process that creates desire for your solution. [L0523.074]
- Email pre-frames use solo ads or joint venture partners to endorse you to their list. The partner's endorsement borrows their credibility, and the call to action is a link in the email. In direct mail the same role is played by a lift letter from the list owner. [L0523.075]

#### Educational content and the one-page versus many-pages choice

- Educational content such as guides and how-to articles helps customers choose the right product, and a site with strong guidance content turns visitors into buyers. Study content-rich stores that guide customers and adapt their approach to your niche. [L0487.057]
- *Older (2021-22):* Education-based marketing walks a prospect through the whole awareness pyramid, and this can be done on one long page, but it demands strong copywriting. Using several linked pages is usually easier, because each page needs less text. [L0481.012]
- A short quiz on the home page can ask a few questions about the visitor's situation and then recommend the right product or stage of the buying funnel. This helps undecided visitors who do not yet know which option fits them. [L1016.071]
- A Learn section with short decision guides, covering how to choose, how to understand the product and how to avoid costly mistakes, answers pre-purchase questions before the buyer leaves. Each guide should link to the matching product or kit, and the section should be finished before ads point to the site. [L1016.072]

#### Quizzes and free entry points

- Quizzes that give personalized results can serve as an entry point for a high-ticket or education-heavy product. In the example, the quiz ran on its own separate domain (neurotest.me), took about 20 to 25 minutes and ended with custom results and product recommendations. [L0334.014]
- Step: Free digital course as an entry point: build the funnel in ClickFunnels, with a short course that promises one clear result (in the example, eliminating procrastination in three days), three modules and one bonus module from a partner. End the course with product offers, and link the offer to a Shopify checkout by using direct add-to-cart, so fulfillment stays on one system; for high-ticket items, enter the fulfillment details manually instead. [L0334.015]
- Free trial entry points can be scaled only with your own fulfillment. Third-party logistics providers charge for each small task, which makes sample or trial packets expensive; a co-packer can assemble trial packets from bulk product, and the trial sign-up asks only for an email address. Test free-plus-shipping and free-only versions to see which converts better. [L0334.016]

#### Blog posts and content CTAs

- Blog posts that educate and then end without a next step waste the traffic they already earn. Add an on-topic call-to-action block or email capture so existing readers can continue into the funnel. [L1269.029]
- Choose a call-to-action based on the funnel stage of the article's reader, not because a product CTA seems expected. Readers of top-of-funnel articles have near-zero buying intent, so a high-priced product embed in an informational article will rarely convert. [L1269.030]
- Procedure: Map existing article content to funnel stages and CTAs:
  1. Ask the AI to classify each article by awareness stage, for example problem aware, solution aware or product aware, or by top, middle and bottom of funnel.
  2. Create a CTA for each stage, matching the reader's intent at that point.
  3. Map each stage to an email segment, so readers receive messages suited to where they are.
  4. Map each stage to product embeds or offers, placing products only where the reader is ready to consider them.

  [L1269.031]
- Example: A top-of-funnel article about a niche animal topic can carry an embedded signup for a topic newsletter, with the promise of regular practical tips on the subject. This captures interested readers instead of asking for a sale they are not ready to make. [L1269.033]
- On top blog posts, pairing an on-topic product block with an email capture works better than a generic store banner. The product should match the subject of the article, for example apparel for an ocean-themed article. [L1269.034]

### Funnel design and pre-frame structure

#### Funnel principles

- Revenue levels from six to eight figures depend mostly on whether you understand each phase of your funnel and can monetize each point along it. Products, traffic sources, copy, website design and launch methods matter, but they are not what separates the levels. Focus on engineering the path each customer takes. [L0523.046]
- Problem-based marketing beats product-based marketing. Don't sell product features ("take this pill for focus"). Instead: (1) Top of funnel: make audience conscious a problem exists and share content about it, (2) Middle of funnel: show how life looks without the problem and how to overcome it, (3) Bottom of funnel: present your offer as the solution. Find problems via Google Trends, not products. [L0307.011]
- Each stage of a funnel serves as the pre-frame for the next one. Use the seven-phase procedure below to structure the path:

  1. Determine traffic temperature (hot, warm or cold) and the awareness level of the prospect.
  2. Set up a pre-frame bridge matched to that temperature.
  3. Qualify subscribers with an opt-in offer that delivers something of value.
  4. Qualify buyers immediately after opt-in with a low-cost or free-plus-shipping offer.
  5. Identify hyperactive buyers through upsells and downsells on the following pages.
  6. Age and ascend the relationship over time, moving customers up the value ladder.
  7. Change the selling environment for high-ticket offers by moving to phone, direct mail or live events.

  [L0523.047]
- There are 23 interchangeable funnel components, sorted into four stages: the pre-frame bridge, screening subscribers, screening buyers, and spotting hyperactive buyers. You can combine them in any arrangement to fit a given offer. Assemble the setup, run a test, change a single element, then test again. [L0523.067]
- Send all paid traffic to a squeeze page with one goal: the visitor either gives an email address or leaves. Remove menus, ads and any other links so nothing distracts from the opt-in. Every paid click should become owned traffic. [L0523.024]
- Track traffic and offers on the basis of the stage they are at: where a squeeze page captures an email, a pre-sell or advertorial page warms cold traffic toward a sale, and a product page closes a visitor who is already ready. [L0523.024, L0919.004, L0919.003]
- Ad-level funnels should match the path: a problem ad leads through educational content, a solution ad leads to a landing page, and retargeting leads straight to the offer. [L1019.019, L1019.020, L1019.021]

#### Pre-frames within the funnel

- The seven-phase procedure above treats each prospect's temperature as the first decision. [L0523.047]
- Stage-matched copy and page choices reinforce the pre-frame: a visitor who arrives with a strong frame converts at a much higher rate. [L0523.048]

#### Ad creative as a funnel stage

- If a Kalodata rip campaign is saturated or not working, test bottom-of-funnel image ads instead of pausing entirely. You may capture sales from an audience that is already solution-aware but fatigued from watching videos. Bottom-of-funnel is cheaper to test (quick image creation) than new video production. [L0316.032]
- Some stores build a separate product page per persona or angle, keeping the same layout and changing only the copy. Track each page separately to see which angle converts. [L1164.046]
- When one product is sold to several audiences, keep the landing page structure identical and change only the copy and headline for each angle. Then split-test the variants to see which angle converts best. [L0387.035]
- The landing page must match the marketing angle of the ad. With Page Fly, create one page theme per angle; with other themes, create separate product pages and see which converts best. [L1219.037]

### Testing and scaling landing pages

#### Testing order

- Landing pages should be tested against each other after a first test, with more relevant variants built for specific audiences. Relevance between ad, audience and page is a key driver of performance. [L0918.023]
- Start with a standard original landing page. Build listicles, advertorials and whitelist pages only once daily revenue reaches roughly $500 to $1,000. [L1454.010]
- Below roughly $200 in daily revenue, do not spend time on listicles, advertorials or whitelist pages. Focus on getting first sales with the original landing page. [L1454.011]
- Do not build a pre-lander or pre-sales page until a single day has produced at least $500 in revenue. Before that threshold, pre-landers add work without enough sales data to justify them. [L1454.012]

#### Where the thresholds apply

The revenue thresholds from different units apply at different stages and do not conflict:

| Daily revenue | What to build |
|---|---|
| Below roughly $200 | Only the standard original landing page; no listicles, advertorials or whitelist pages |
| Roughly $500 to $1,000 | Listicles, advertorials and whitelist pages may be built |
| At least $500 in one day | A pre-lander or pre-sales page may be built |

[L1454.010, L1454.011, L1454.012]

- Plan up to three landing pages, one each for hot, warm and cold traffic, when several sources of different temperature are already running. [L0523.135]
- An editorial funnel is an advanced step that comes after the current funnel converts well. [L0578.034]

#### Test steps for a page

- Create at least two variants of a landing page and split-test them. Use the same page structure and change only the parts that differ between angles. [L0387.035]
- Test whether direct-to-product traffic converts better before building a separate landing page for each product. [L0918.018]
- For each ad set, record its awareness level and the page it lands on, then compare conversion by page. [L1019.022]

#### Further details

- Send cold traffic to the single simplified kit page rather than to a page with many options, because one clear problem and one clear solution convert best for people who have never heard of the brand. [L1016.044]

## Offer design and positioning against competitors

### Finding the gap in competitor offers

- Find your conversion advantage by identifying what your top competitors do poorly, then improve your store in exactly those areas. Competitor weaknesses are the biggest positioning opportunities. [L0140.001]
- Audit your top competitors and your own store for these conversion weaknesses: [L0140.002]
  1. Low-quality or low-resolution product photos.
  2. Generic or copied product descriptions.
  3. Outdated product catalogs.
  4. Missing product variants, such as sizes and colors.
  5. Unclear or missing shipping and returns policies.
  6. Poor customer support or no live chat.
  7. No promotional offers, bundles or bonus incentives.
- Competitor weaknesses to exploit on product pages include weaker photos, fewer photos, weak descriptions, unclear shipping and returns policies, and poor support. Improving these costs little money but can raise conversion. (Older advice, 2019.) [L0960.041]
- Common reasons shoppers buy from a competitor are better pricing, bonus offers, better product descriptions, more product photos, more information on the product page, and clearer shipping and return policies. Compare each of these with your store. (Older advice, 2019.) [L1068.055]
- To decide what to optimize first, study the policies, page structure and product page layout of the future competitor with the most traffic. Use the lowest rank number among your qualifying competitors as the highest-traffic one. [L1097.026]

#### Competitor website review

Steps for a competitor website review: [L0894.005]
1. Open the top two or three competitor sites by traffic rank side by side.
2. Compare the homepages for similarities and differences, noting free shipping, promotions, live chat, premium shipping options and services.
