3. Find the same product on each site: search by supplier SKU first, then by product name. If a product is missing on one site, note it as an outdated catalog.
4. On the product pages, compare price (check for MAP), reviews, images, shipping and delivery estimate, price match policy, description and specifications.
5. Add the product to the cart and check which payment options are shown before checkout.
6. Note what competitors do well that you must also do, and what they miss that you can do better.
7. Prioritize: match the basics first, then build the differentiators on top.
8. Repeat the review about every two weeks.

Case: a chandelier niche, analyzed with two top-ranked competitor sites from product research. [L0894.039]
- Homepages: both competitors showed free shipping on orders over $49. One had live chat and the other did not. One offered white glove delivery for $150 and Room and Choice service for $99, with basic threshold delivery included. Both ran supplier promotions with expiring dates. [L0894.039]
- Planned store: free shipping on all orders, an expiring coupon code in the header bar, and negotiated supplier deals of the same kind. [L0894.039]
- Product pages: a Calco product was missing from one site, which showed an outdated catalog. [L0894.039]
- For a Union matte black 12-light chandelier, both sites sold at the MAP price of $438 and had no reviews. One site showed free shipping with an estimated delivery date, live chat and a visible price match policy. The other did not. [L0894.039]
- Both used the same two supplier images and basic descriptions. One site said nothing about bulbs, while the other stated they were not included. Both hid payment options until checkout step one. [L0894.039]
- Planned response: match the better site's basics, add more images, show estimated delivery, offer bulbs as a free gift or as an add-on, and show payment options in the cart. [L0894.039]

#### Competitor product page review

Procedure: [L1446.015]
1. Pick a product page that receives traffic.
2. Compare it side by side with the product pages of likely future competitors.
3. Note where competitors are stronger and where the store has an advantage.
4. Improve the weak points and keep the strengths.

### Positioning against competitors

- Find the strongest unclaimed position after competitive research has identified the standard market positions. Look for an angle with high emotional resonance that competitors ignore despite an obvious market need. [L0221.018]
  - Example: every competitor in financial products for married men sells *maximize your money*, but none claims *the check you choose is the check she lives on*. [L0221.018]
  - Identify which emotional truth competitors systematically skip, then build bottom-of-funnel ads around it. [L0221.018]
- Compete with large retailers by offering more: better pre-sale product information, a more specific buying experience, service, and sometimes bonus gifts. The small share of sales a niche store takes from giants can still be a multimillion-dollar business. [L0806.052]
- A monoproduct store is not automatically a problem. It can be made to look more professional. Competitors in the same market may run general stores, so compare yourself against them. [L0691.009]
- Niche-specific stores achieve higher conversion rates than multi-niche stores, because messaging is clearer and brand positioning as an expert in that niche is stronger. The conversion advantage justifies the extra $29 per month for a separate Shopify store. (Older advice, 2021.) [L0270.014]
- Few competitors offer financing, so placing it prominently can serve as an extra value proposition when a shopper compares two options. [L1295.003]
- Frame the product against competitors in the most favourable light, presenting the alternatives' weaknesses and the product's advantages side by side. Pairing this with a social proof number such as *why do thousands of people choose us* strengthens the comparison. [L1209.024]
- Competitor product sites with a plain layout, no unique offer and an intrusive pop-up give shoppers little reason to choose them. A store that adds a clear extra, such as a free accessory or a free gift on the first order, can stand out against these sites. [L1508.036]

#### Benchmark positioning formula

- Compare the product against two familiar alternatives: one that is cheaper or more common, and one that is premium. Claim that it beats both on a specific dimension. [L0918.031]
- It works because the buyer understands the advantage immediately from known reference points. [L0918.031]
- Example 1, cordless vacuum: *quicker to grab than a corded model, stronger than a budget stick.* [L0918.031]
- Example 2, travel mug: *keeps coffee warmer than a standard flask, lighter than a steel tumbler.* [L0918.031]

#### Comparison against the substitute

- Instead of comparing with other brands, compare with what the customer already does, such as a common alternative. Show that the product is faster, easier, more convenient or more ethical. [L0919.019]
  - Example 1: *single-serve pods cost more per cup than our bulk powder; our powder is quicker and costs less per cup.* [L0919.019]
  - Example 2: *ready-made shakes contain added sugar; our powder lets you control sweetness and cuts out the extra sugar.* [L0919.019]

#### Comparison table against alternatives

- Build a feature table with the brand in the first column and two or three competitor categories beside it. Use Yes, No, Sometimes, Usually no and Rarely to show differences. [L0886.018]
- Example layout for a new brand: rows for a unique technology, a durability guarantee and premium positioning, with competitor categories such as generic tools and budget tools. [L0886.018]

| Feature | Brand | Generic tools | Budget tools |
|---|---|---|---|
| Unique technology | Yes / No / Sometimes | Yes / No / Sometimes | Yes / No / Sometimes |
| Durability guarantee | Yes / No / Sometimes | Usually no / Rarely | Usually no / Rarely |
| Premium positioning | Yes / No / Sometimes | Usually no / Rarely | Usually no / Rarely |

[L0886.018]

### Offer design

#### Offer tiers and choice

- Present offer tiers with minimal visual clutter so customers compare options quickly without becoming overwhelmed. Three to four distinct tiers convert better than excessive choice. [L0232.017]
- Presenting more than four offer variations at the same time overwhelms customers, slows decisions and reduces conversion rate compared with simplified choice architecture. [L0232.023]
- Keep the checkout simple, such as a minimum order quantity of three or a fixed price for a set, because extra steps make customers think harder and leave. [L0774.021]

#### Friction and value

- Most sellers focus marketing on the dream outcome and the likelihood of success, but neglect minimizing implementation friction. Reducing the time required and the effort or sacrifice toward zero multiplies perceived value far more effectively than improving outcome promises alone. [L0233.005]
- The consumer's perception of value matters more than the real cost of the add-on. A free add-on bought for 10 cents to 1 dollar can be presented as a 20-dollar value. [L0577.010]
- Conversion gains on a store come mainly from three visible elements: good offers, a strong money-back guarantee, and a free add-on or cheap upsell presented as valuable. Each must be clearly visible on the site, and the copy must read sensibly. FAQ videos on the site help too, but they are secondary. [L0577.001]
- A one-size-fits-all product removes sizing friction. There are no size charts, no confusion and less second-guessing, so customers buy faster and return less. Sizing friction is a major conversion killer for apparel, and removing it is a strong advantage for testing. [L0347.021]

#### Case: necklace store turnaround

Starting point (before March 17): earning 2,000 to 3,000 USD per month with a weak offer. Problems: a basic €29.90 necklace-only offer, 21 to 30 day shipping, weak copywriting, no PayPal, and a lengthy checkout. [L0233.011]

Changes implemented on March 17 after the owner joined the mentorship and consulted on restructuring the offer: [L0233.011]
- Switched to a US-based supplier, reducing delivery from 21 to 30 days to 2 to 3 days. [L0233.011]
- Integrated PayPal one-click checkout. [L0233.011]
- Rewrote product copy to emphasize style and heritage. [L0233.011]
- Created a bundled offer: €29.90 for a necklace, a second necklace free, a storage box, a style guide ebook and free shipping. [L0233.011]
- Installed automatic multi-buy discounts, with no codes required. [L0233.011]
- Added a one-click checkout app to skip the cart page. [L0233.011]
- Collected and displayed customer reviews as social proof. [L0233.011]

Results: 700% growth in revenue rate from April 17 to May 11, and 60,000 USD total revenue over the 4-month period. [L0233.011]

Learning: strong offer structure (bundled value, guarantees, social proof) and operational efficiency (fast shipping, frictionless checkout) drive sales more than product uniqueness. [L0233.011]

#### Free gifts, bonuses and bundles

- Bonus offers create a decisive advantage during comparison shopping. When customers evaluate the same product across several competitor sites, a meaningful bonus they cannot get elsewhere often tips the purchase in your favor. [L0314.016]
- Offering a bonus gift in ads and on product pages can raise both click-through rate and conversion rate. Showing the bonus as a clear promotion helps the offer stand out against competitors. [L0360.027]
- A free gift should be profitable at the chosen threshold. Place a line such as *free gift with this product* in the description of best-selling products whose price already exceeds the threshold. [L1094.071]
- Free gift promotions have not caused Google Ads problems for this seller. Bonus products are an accepted promotion for conversion rate and average order value. [L1094.072]
- A landing page built for one product can still convert well when a bundle of that product is offered, since customers can choose to buy more than one unit. [L0999.066]
- Stand out from competitors selling the same products at the same price by adding an offer they lack. Examples: a gift that costs about $30 to $50 but looks like about $100, expedited shipping, or white glove delivery. Test each option on customers. [L0804.086]

#### Offer structures seen in live examples

- Offer structures seen in live examples included a percentage discount on a first subscription purchase, a free gift with a subscription, a *bestseller* tag on a product page, and a *frequently bought with* discount on a second item combined with an add-to-cart button. Use offer structures like these to test which offer tips visitors into buying. [L0918.026]
- Other offer and value examples from the lesson: free shipping as a benefit, *20% off on our new joint care chew when you subscribe today* as a hero offer, and savings that scale with quantity. Use a percentage offer at the top, then a call to action that scrolls to the product selection. [L0918.027]

#### Offer framing

- Move from a temporary promotion headline, such as *Up to X Off*, which reads like a sale brand, to a value-anchored headline that names the outcome. Then list included perks such as free shipping, free gifts and a risk-free guarantee. [L0886.017]
- Example: *Protect Your Hair While You Style*, followed by a list of premium-quality claims and perks, with a risk-free trial line. [L0886.017]

#### Quantity discounts

- Quantity discounts raise average order value and conversion, because customers buy more units at a lower average price. Pairing the offer with a limited-stock message, for example *only five left*, adds urgency to complete the purchase. [L0774.016]
- Case: a fitness product (stretching band) sold at 29.99 USD with some sales but a weak conversion rate. Adding a free yoga mat to the same order raised the conversion rate sharply, at the same price. [L0773.062]

#### Test offers, not just the product

- Test offer variants to find the conversion multiplier. Case: a home decor test. [L0307.021]
  - Offer A, $29.90: failed.
  - Offer B, *buy one get one free*: failed.
  - Offer C, *buy two get two free* with an increased price per unit: succeeded, with consistent sales and high repeat customers. [L0307.021]
  - The winning offer had higher perceived value while maintaining margin. Lesson: with the same creative and high CTR, run multiple offer tests to find the conversion multiplier. [L0307.021]
- Offer tests such as a free gift versus a price reduction can both produce sales, but compare the results. In the lesson, a £5 price cut with the gift removed also produced sales, so test which version converts better. [L0640.041]

#### Sampling

- A seller who does not let buyers sample a superior product before purchase loses sales. For products that clearly outperform alternatives, offer a small sample or a try-before-you-buy option. [L0378.079]

#### Differentiating on the same product

- Improving conversion without price discounts, in this order: (Older advice, 2019.) [L0960.040]
  1. Ask your supplier for better pricing tiers or negotiating room, before anything else.
  2. Improve product pages with unique descriptions.
  3. Get unique photos and videos from the supplier, and add them to the product pages.
  4. Find competitor weaknesses on the same products and turn them into your strengths.
- Stand out from competitors selling the same product at the same price. Procedure: [L0804.087]
  1. Find the top stores selling the same product at the same price.
  2. Look for weak spots in their product pages.
  3. Fix those weak spots on your own product pages for the same products.

### Pricing, price guarantees and MAP

- Customers typically comparison shop across three or more websites before buying, searching for the lowest price. This creates decision paralysis. A price guarantee removes this friction by letting them purchase immediately while keeping the option to research competitors within 6 months, converting browsers into customers. [L0145.003]
- Dropship suppliers enforce Minimum Advertised Price (MAP) policy, requiring all authorized retailers to advertise at identical prices. This eliminates price-based competition between retailers and makes price-match guarantees sustainable with minimal refund payouts, since no authorized competitor can legally undercut your price. [L0145.004]
- Secret shopper method for a dropshipping store: [L1293.007]
  1. Google at least three of your own products and check whether your store appears in the results, without assuming it does.
  2. Use a VPN set to the US (the speaker used Utopia) when outside the US, so you see the ads a US customer would see.
  3. Compare your price with the lowest price shown in Google Shopping and match the lowest price instead of undercutting it.
- How the two price rules fit together: where MAP applies, all authorized retailers advertise the same price, so there is no lower price to match or undercut. [L0145.004] Where MAP does not apply and you find a lower price in Google Shopping, match the lowest price instead of undercutting it. [L1293.007]

#### Small price and offer changes

- Small changes can move conversion rate noticeably, for example a $2 price increase or decrease, a free gift, or a bundle saving. Test these small variations rather than relying on one price. [L0436.021]
- Once ads are running, test a new price or offer about every second day, and track conversion rate and customer engagement on the store. Keep testing until the store reaches a conversion rate of at least 2%. [L0436.022]
- Keeping one price and one offer unchanged for a long period prevents finding the best-performing setting. Continuous price and offer testing is necessary after ads start. [L0436.023]
- If a store receives 150 to 200 sessions and shows poor conversion, with no add-to-cart events, the issue is likely the offer, not the page design. After implementing the page structure and copywriting, change the offer (discount percentage, guarantee, bundle) and retest. [L0321.038]
- Improve a landing page by testing prices, bundles and offers to find the combination that converts best while keeping a good margin. Start scaling only once the page turns clicks into sales, because a good CTR alone does not produce sales. [L0562.006]

### Guarantees, returns and shipping framing

#### The offer must be clear and strong

- Roughly 40% of the chance that a visitor converts depends on how strong the offer is compared with the offers other sellers in the same market make. The 40-40-20 idea treats the offer as one of the main conversion levers. (Older advice, 2021 to 2022.) [L0411.001]
- A strong offer sells even with average presentation, while a weak offer cannot be rescued by a beautiful website. Improve the offer before investing in design. (Older advice, 2021 to 2022.) [L0411.006]
- The offer must be clear. Its elements must be easy to find on the homepage, product pages and about-us pages, and easy to understand within seconds. The customer should never wonder what something means, whether it applies to them, or how it helps them. (Older advice, 2021 to 2022.) [L0411.008]
- Remove fine print, caveats and exclusions from offer claims, because they make the offer look weak and reduce its perceived value. Regularly check customer questions to find places where the offer is unclear. (Older advice, 2021 to 2022.) [L0411.009]
- The offer does not need to beat the best offers in unrelated markets. What matters is being better on balance than direct competitors selling the same or similar products. (Older advice, 2021 to 2022.) [L0411.032]

#### Returns policy as a visible advantage

- Case: a seller of oversized home lighting, such as chandeliers, found that buyers hesitated because they could not judge size and fit from product photos. The seller introduced a free returns policy and promoted it strongly, and the change roughly doubled revenue in that first business. Competitors had return policies, but they were hidden in fine print and not shown on product pages, so buyers assumed none existed. (Older advice, 2021 to 2022.) [L0411.011]
- Procedure: (Older advice, 2021 to 2022.) [L0411.012]
  1. Identify the main reason customers hesitate, for example worry about fit, size, or wrong product.
  2. Build a stronger guarantee or policy that removes that specific worry.
  3. Check what competitors offer and how visibly they show it.
  4. Present your policy in clear language on the product page, homepage, ads, about-us page and everywhere else on the site, not hidden in policy pages.
  5. Give the policy a short, distinct name, such as *free and easy returns*, and use that wording consistently so it becomes your own term.

#### Free-shipping framing

Three framings appear in the sources. Each is a different choice, not a conflict. [L0232.024, L0894.039, L0233.011]
- Label as *Free Shipping on First Orders* even when all customers receive the same benefit. This creates the perception of special treatment and increases order likelihood without changing actual costs. [L0232.024]
- Threshold framing, as used by competitors: free shipping on orders over $49. [L0894.039]
- Free shipping on all orders, as planned for the chandelier store, or bundled into a package offer as in the necklace case. [L0894.039, L0233.011]

### Product pages and competitive presentation

- A product page must win on the total offer compared with competitors, including visuals, guarantees, bundles and value, not only on price. Improving only the price or the look rarely fixes a low conversion rate. [L0626.015]
- Competitors often have weak landing pages, so improving the page is a fast way to beat them. Focus on high-quality images, a stronger offer and clearer copy. [L0335.040]
- If a brand's own site is outdated or hard to use, build a better page on your own store and sell at the same price. A better buying experience can win the sale even when the price is identical. (Older advice, 2019.) [L0663.027]
- If your offer, price or product page is weaker than competing sellers shown beside you, your conversion rate drops, because shoppers open several retailer tabs and compare them. Product pages must stand out quickly and communicate clearly the information shoppers compare. Product page conversion work is therefore a core requirement for Google Shopping, not an optional extra. (Older advice, 2021 to 2022.) [L0739.009, L0741.005]

## Product page and landing page structure

### Where visitors land and what the page must do

- Most traffic lands directly on product pages rather than the homepage, so product page optimization deserves the most attention [L0894.021].
- Most paid traffic arrives on product pages through searches for a brand name, product name or SKU, so visitors already know what they want. Product pages must therefore work as complete sales pages on their own [L1130.013].
- The product page is a sales page. It must sell the product to a cold visitor who came from an ad and knows nothing about the store yet. If the page does not persuade, the visitor leaves without buying [L1346.015].
- Visitors who arrive from ads rarely read long text, so the default features and benefits must be clear enough that the visitor understands the product within seconds [L1346.020].
- Shoppers open several tabs and compare pages side by side. Product pages must stand out immediately with a clear title, price, delivery information and support options. A page that looks less convincing or less clear than a competitor's loses the sale [L0739.010] (older, Source B 2021-22).
- The goal of a product page is to remove every reason for the visitor to leave. Price, images, shipping, delivery timing, product details and answers to common questions should all be available in one place [L1098.022].
- Place the manufacturer description, product specifications, customer reviews and a customer question-and-answer section on the product page, so visitors can make the buying decision without leaving [L1098.021].
- A landing page has to deliver two things visitors look for: a clear offer and visible proof that the store can be trusted. Both should be visible early, because visitors decide quickly whether to stay [L0916.001].
- Every element that does not help the buying decision adds friction and distracts the visitor. Remove or reduce anything that pulls attention away from the product after the click [L0916.003].
- A landing page has to convey the offer informatively, compellingly and persuasively, and push the visitor toward one specific action. That action depends on the page's place in the funnel: buying, submitting an email, or downloading a lead magnet [L0919.001].
- A clean, trustworthy design with fewer elements works better than a cluttered one. Product pages need optimized images, persuasive copy, urgency elements and trust badges to convert [L0491.019].
- The top of the page should give enough information to decide without overloading the visitor. Balance the amount of information in each section [L0709.028].
- Put key information at the top: benefits, social proof and lower-risk elements such as guarantees. Put additional detail further down for visitors who want to research [L0709.026].
- A common mistake is placing important product information far down the page, where visitors rarely scroll. Information should sit near the product image and the calls to action [L0709.027].
- Each section should move the visitor forward by answering one question at a time: what is it, why should I believe it, what do I get, what if it does not work, and how do I buy [L0919.036].
- The product page is built around the buying questions in order: what it is, how it works, what is inside, whether it is trustworthy, and what happens if it fails. Each block answers one of these questions with proof such as videos, reviews or a guarantee [L1188.037].

### Mobile-first layout

- Design mobile first. Create the mobile version of the page, then adapt it for desktop. Starting from desktop usually produces product pages where the add-to-cart button is buried on mobile [L0390.041].
- On mobile product pages where the add-to-cart button requires three to four scrolls before it appears, customers become frustrated and leave. Place the main call to action where it is visible without scrolling on mobile [L0390.042].
- Mobile share of traffic, two figures from two sources:
  - Over 70% of sessions typically happen on mobile, so optimize layout, navigation and above-the-fold copy for mobile first [L1016.049].
  - About 80-85% of customers buy on their phones, so the page must be optimized for mobile first. The first view on a smartphone is where the five key elements should appear [L0916.016].
  - About 80-85% of visitors arrive on smartphones, so there is little time to convince them on the first screen. Put the most important trust element and the product image at the top [L1260.027].
  - Use your own store's device data to decide which figure applies.
- On mobile, place as much as possible above the fold: product benefits, testimonials and trust signals, the offer, and how to buy. Balance this with enough white space so the page does not become too busy to read [L0755.018].
- Five key elements for the mobile first view, in this order [L0916.017]:
  1. Good-quality imagery, such as a lifestyle or user-generated image of the product in use.
  2. A bold headline or USP that states the product name and what the buyer gets.
  3. Social proof, such as a star rating line.
  4. The call-to-action button.
  5. Trust badges directly below the add-to-cart button.
  
  If the call-to-action or trust badges do not fit in the first view, place them in the first scroll [L0916.017].
- Example of a social proof line: "4.8 out of 5 from 1,000+ happy customers," placed directly under the product name or headline in the first view. End it with a benefit phrase, such as "enjoying stress-free sleep," so the trust signal also carries an emotional message [L0916.018].
- Reduce the size of the navigation bar and trim above-the-fold product copy to two or three lines. Move secondary points into bullet points so the critical buying information fits above the fold [L1016.048].
- On mobile, do not stack all features and testimonials vertically, because this creates a very long page. Use side-scrolling carousels for feature sets and testimonials so visitors can skip sections they do not need [L0918.012].
- Use accordions or drop-down menus for detailed features and ingredient lists, so visitors expand only the topics they care about [L0918.014].

### Above the fold: hero and buy box

- Place the key fundamentals above the fold: product title, a short two- to three-sentence description, variant selection, the add-to-cart button, and payment terms such as installment options [L1016.029].
- Use large, motion-rich hero imagery on the left side of the product page to create energy and immerse the visitor from the first screen [L1016.030].
- Show the review count and the number of answered customer questions near the top of the page. These signals reassure buyers early [L1098.018].
- Show the price with the amount saved, the shipping cost (free shipping where offered), the estimated delivery date and the stock status near the top of the page. Buyers need these facts to decide quickly [L1098.019].
- Show made-to-order status directly under the add-to-cart button, for example "product is custom made, estimated manufacturing and delivery time is four to six weeks," so buyers know the lead time before they purchase [L0806.048].
- Trust elements for the product page: star reviews (for example 4.8 out of 5), quoted testimonials, a secure checkout badge directly below the call-to-action button, and a benefit-driven semi-headline directly below the product name that states the problem or outcome [L1259.018, L1260.029].
- Product page structure: a headline as one benefit-driven line, followed by at most two lines of paragraph text, then an image or GIF. Long educational paragraphs are justified only for complex products where the target buyer needs a detailed explanation [L1259.019].
- Use a unique custom product title that is not the generic supplier name [L1207.046, L1209.032, L1210.024].
- Show the pack size or weight, for example "2.65 oz (75 g) net weight," near the product name and image [L0409.015] (older, Source B 2021-22).
- For a consumable product, show the yield or serving size in plain units, for example how many cups or servings one pack makes and how much one serving needs. This lets buyers judge cost per use and signals convenience. Tie the figure to the packaging so the claim can be checked [L0918.032].
- When selling to several countries, show both size systems next to each other, for example "EU 41 / UK 5.5," so buyers recognise their size immediately. Check the numbers against real conversion in each market [L1219.005].
- Show portability, battery life, size and ease of setup on the page. These answer the practical objections about whether a product will be bulky, how long it will last, or where it can be used [L0919.034].
- Use social cues, such as the most common choices, to pre-fill configurable products. Shoppers who start from a blank selection tend to hesitate and drop off [L1225.022].
- Headlines and copy should be plain and direct. Most visitors leave when they are confused about what they are looking at, so the hero headline must be obvious rather than clever [L0387.023].
- Put the product highlights at the top of the product section, the offer near the top right under the reviews, then the quantity or variant selector below the offer, and the add-to-cart button directly below that [L1225.030].
- Keep the top of the product description short, with about three bullet points, so the add-to-cart button stays visible above the fold. Move longer details into lower sections such as benefits, reviews and FAQs [L1331.017].

### Buy box content and order

- Recommended buy box order, from top to bottom [L1225.030]:
  1. Show product highlights at the top of the product section.
  2. Place the offer near the top, right under the reviews.
  3. Place the quantity or variant selector below the offer.
  4. Place the add-to-cart button directly below that.
- Product page layout in this order [L0409.015] (older, Source B 2021-22):
  1. Product name, main image and pack size or weight, for example 2.65 oz (75 g) net weight.
  2. Variant choices such as scent, type or sensitive-skin version, shown as clearly selectable options.
  3. Price, with the one-time price and the subscribe-and-save price shown side by side.
  4. A large add-to-cart button in the single accent colour.
  5. Short benefit bullets directly under the button, for example "aluminum free, effective odor protection, goes on smooth, vegan ingredients."
  6. A headline that names the common problem with this product type and promises the solution.
  7. A section on the key ingredients or mechanism and what each one does for the customer.
  8. A feature list in which each feature is followed by its benefit.
  9. The review count, the star rating and a few review quotes, with a link to see more reviews.
  10. Related products at the bottom.
  
  [L0409.015]
- Specifications should be listed on the product page as part of the logic element that confirms the purchase. They are not a unique element of the copy, so they can be pasted in from the product data [L0396.030] (older, Source B 2021-22).
- Give the product page all options: variants, add-ons, removable items and multiple image angles. Offer two actions: add to cart to keep shopping, and buy now to go straight to checkout [L1098.020].
- Show the ingredients or components of each product variant on the product page, because variants can differ in formula. A visual breakdown next to the variant selector helps buyers choose the right one [L0524.074].
- When one product comes in several intensity levels, such as four grit levels, list each level clearly and name it by the customer type it suits. This lets each customer find a matching option without guessing [L0524.075].
- If several products are similar but differ in tier, such as basic, pro and max versions, buyers usually choose one version rather than buying them together. Structure the page so each product can be selected directly and treat products as separate choices rather than a bundle [L0918.017].
- Show the comparison price next to the sale price with a simple discount label. State the discount as an exact percentage or plain amount [L1207.046, L1209.032].
- Under the add-to-cart button of a reseller page, a compact image-based block can explain the store's own USP, such as free shipping, a phone line, easy returns and a price-match promise. Keep it visually simple so it does not compete with the buy button [L0409.044] (older, Source B 2021-22).
- Add a USP block to each product page that shows why a buyer should choose you, such as shipping, returns or price promises. Visitors then see the reasons on the product itself, at the point where they decide to buy [L1145.026] (older, Source B 2021-22).
- USP icons can sit in the pre-footer or on product pages. Placing them near the buying decision lets buyers see the reasons to purchase where they decide [L1045.048].
- Short USP lines for skimmers, such as suitability for all users or no experience required, go directly beneath the headline and price [L1210.024].
- Recommended sibling products on a conversion-focused product page should be removed or moved below the add-to-cart button. Upsells can come later when average order value is the goal [L1225.035].

### Buy button: label, placement and checkout route

- Make add-to-cart buttons large and repeat them, with one near the top and one lower on the page. A sticky button can also keep the cart reachable while the visitor scrolls [L0356.044] (older, 2019).
- Test the wording of the purchase button, such as "Add to cart," "Buy now" or "Shop now," because buyers respond differently to each. Keep the main purchase button directly under the product title and images, where it is the first thing visitors see [L0352.058].
- Use "Add to cart" as the product page button label instead of "Order now" for dropshipping stores [L0665.125] (older, 2022).
- Use a specific call to action tied to the product outcome, such as "remove dents now," rather than a generic "shop" or "buy" label. The call to action should link directly to the product page [L1225.026].
- Change the button text from "Add to Cart" to "Get My [Product] Now" and direct customers to checkout, rather than adding to cart, to reduce friction during purchase [L0321.009].
- A direct payment button shortens the path to purchase for visitors who want to buy immediately with a provider they already trust. Visitors who are not ready to buy can still use Add to Cart and keep browsing [L0339.003].
- For a single-product store, send the main call to action directly to checkout, with wording such as "Add to my bag" or "I want it now," instead of the add-to-cart page. This removes a step where visitors leave [L1259.021, L1260.034].
- Remove the accelerated checkout button, such as a buy-with-shop option, from product pages. In the lesson's tests it lowered conversion rates [L1225.028]. This conflicts with the direct buy options above ([L0339.003, L1098.020]). Use the direct buy option or the accelerated button only where your own test shows a gain.
- When a product page offers Buy It Now next to Add to Cart, both buttons should use the single accent colour, or Buy It Now should use a plainer style, so visitors see one clear group of purchase actions. Never let other bright coloured elements sit around either button [L0409.052] (older, Source B 2021-22).
- The call to action must be explicit and visible, especially in the hero section, because some visitors are already ready to buy and only need a clear button such as "Add to Cart." Do not assume visitors will work out the next step [L0919.008].
- A call to action must be present on the page, because visitors who see value may still do nothing unless they are asked to act [L1363.006].
- Every product description needs a clear call to action at the bottom, such as "Scroll back up and click add to cart" or "Buy today," because many visitors read to the bottom and leave if nothing tells them what to do [L1293.017].
- The speaker estimated that up to 99% of visitors leave a product page without buying when the only call to action is the add-to-cart button at the top [L1293.018].
- Repeat the primary call to action at the bottom of the homepage and product page, so a visitor who scrolled the whole page has a clear next step [L0409.038] (older, Source B 2021-22).

### Sticky add-to-cart bar

- Sticky add-to-cart bar setup [L0635.023]:
  1. Install a sticky add-to-cart app.
  2. Open the sticky bar settings.
  3. Set the checkout button to "Add to cart" or "Buy now."
  4. Configure the desktop and mobile versions separately.
  5. Keep the product name, variants, price and quantity; the compare-at price can be removed.
  6. Show the bar after 15% scroll on desktop and on mobile, so it does not appear at the top, where the main add-to-cart button already sits.
- A sticky add-to-cart bar increases the add-to-cart rate, because shoppers who are ready to buy while scrolling can act without scrolling back up. Show it only after about 15% of the page has been scrolled [L0635.024].
- Use a sticky add-to-cart button that stays visible while scrolling. Place it unobtrusively so it does not cover the page content [L1016.033].
- Keep the sticky add-to-cart bar that stays visible while scrolling on mobile, because it helps conversion [L1225.029].

### Product page sections below the buy box

- Build the sections below the buy box in this order [L1331.023]:
  1. Add an image-with-text section above the other sections for the first key benefit.
  2. Write a heading, a short text block and a related image for each benefit.
  3. Duplicate the section for each further benefit and keep the same image size.
  4. Add an icon bar for quick features.
  5. Add reviews.
  6. Add a collapsible FAQ block near the end of the page.
- Pair each benefit with an image or short video that shows the feature, so each claim has visual proof. This listicle-style layout builds trust better than copy alone [L1331.024].
- Include an explicit what-is-included list and a specifications section. For example: one device, six massage attachments, one rechargeable lithium-ion battery and one charger, so buyers can see the full contents at a glance [L1016.035].
- Keep a short marketing message section separate from the detailed description, so visitors can scan the message first and read details later. Below the description, place reviews, shipping details and returns information [L0409.049] (older, Source B 2021-22).
- Product page layout for an ingredient-led or feature-led product, in this order [L1194.031]:
  1. Benefits of using the product.
  2. How it works.
  3. How to use it.
  4. Who it is for.
  5. FAQ.
  6. Testimonials.
  7. Guarantee, if one is offered.
- Stacking content on a single-product page in this order keeps visitors on the page until they reach the buy button [L1013.022]:
  1. A short how-it-works block.
  2. A what's-inside list.
  3. A satisfaction guarantee.
  4. A see-it-in-action video.
  5. A customer reviews block, with reviewer names labelled "Verified Customer."
- Layout for a product with several ingredients or features [L0918.029]:
  1. List every feature or ingredient of the product.
  2. Write a one-line plain description of each.
  3. Add a credible quality reference for each, such as where it is sourced or which standard it meets.
  4. State how it works.
  5. State the resulting benefit for the customer.
  6. Place customer reviews below the feature list as social proof.
  
  Use accordion menus for the detailed list so visitors can expand only the topics they care about [L0918.029].
- Do not put buying guides or comparison charts on the product page. Keep them in a separate resource section [L0894.029]. This conflicts with the comparison-chart advice below ([L1076.038, L1330.020]). Use the separate resource section for guides; use a product-page chart only where a higher-end version is the logical next choice.
- Remove frequently-bought-together sections, unrelated product recommendations and extra navigation elements, so visitors stay focused on the main product [L1259.020, L1260.033].
- Upsell suggestions such as "you may also like" below the main product can raise average order value, but they may divert attention from a single-product page, which tends to convert better [L0693.030].
- Avoid "you may also like" or other unrelated product sections on a landing page, because they pull visitors away from the offer. If a related product adds value, include it only inside the bundle [L0916.030].

### Descriptions: length by product complexity

| Product type | Description length | Where it goes | Source |
|---|---|---|---|
| Simple product | Short text and a clear image | Product page | [L0409.019] (older, Source B 2021-22) |
| High-ticket or complex product with long technical description | Long text, but not as a column beside the buy box | Full-width tabbed section across the bottom, usually done with an app | [L0370.007] (older, Source B 2021-22) |
| Short description on a conversion-focused page | About three bullet points above the add-to-cart button | Top of product description, with details lower on the page | [L1331.017] |
| Trimmed above-the-fold copy | Two or three lines, with secondary points as bullets | Above the fold | [L1016.048] |
| Above-the-fold description | Two to three sentences | Top of the product page | [L1016.029] |
| Headline with paragraph | One benefit line plus at most two lines of paragraph | Product page | [L1259.019] |

- Simple products need only short text and a clear image, while complex or expensive products can use more text. The same clean layout works for both, and the number of elements should never exceed what the buyer needs [L0409.019] (older, Source B 2021-22).
- The depth of the description should match the product's complexity. A simple product needs only a short description. A complex product, such as a solar generator, needs detailed features, benefits and specifications [L0370.007] (older, Source B 2021-22).
- Long educational paragraphs are justified only for complex products where the target buyer needs a detailed explanation [L1259.019].

### Tabs, accordions and collapsibles

- Set up tabbed product pages with three tabs in this order [L1130.024]:
  1. Product Description, which holds the item description.
  2. Shipping Policy.
  3. Price Match Policy.
  
  Visitors start on the description and can check shipping and price match without leaving the page.
- Placing description, shipping policy and price match in one long column wastes space and makes the page endless. Tabs keep the page short and let visitors reach the information they need quickly [L1130.025].
- Product pages can have as many extra tabs as needed to help the buyer, such as care instructions in addition to description, shipping and price match [L0667.028].
- Answer shipping and returns questions through tabs. Every time a shopper has to leave the page to find this information, the chance that they come back and buy drops. Product descriptions, images and videos answer product questions, while tabs answer delivery and return questions [L1300.023] (older, Source B 2021-22).
- Give tabs extra padding on each side and inside the tab content, so they are bigger, easier to tap and less cramped against the page edges. Check the result in the live preview before saving [L1300.027] (older, Source B 2021-22).
- Tabs for description, shipping, return policy and price guarantee that do not work are a major conversion problem, because visitors cannot find the information they need to buy [L0409.035] (older, Source B 2021-22).
- Use accordions or drop-down menus for detailed features and ingredient lists, so visitors can expand only the topics they care about. This avoids cluttering the page with text that puts off some visitors [L0918.014].
- Auto-opening a product feature section on page load draws attention to key product details that the shopper might otherwise miss. A fake click simulates a recorded interaction, such as clicking, typing or scrolling, so the change happens without shopper action [L0389.016].
- Auto-expanding product details sections on page load for first-time visitors only increases conversion rate by +2.1% [L0197.014].
- First-time visitors lack brand familiarity and need to understand all product benefits before deciding to purchase. Returning customers have already reviewed product details and built brand trust, so they do not benefit from expanded details shown automatically [L0197.016].
- Do not delete supplier-provided links from product descriptions without replacing them. Remove external links and recreate the warranty information directly on your product page using collapsible sections [L0253.028].

### Out-of-stock product pages

- Showing targeted product alternatives immediately below the add-to-cart button on out-of-stock product pages generates a +9.5% conversion rate lift [L0197.005].
- Shoppers who encounter out-of-stock products assume they will not find a suitable alternative and abandon the store. Prominent in-stock alternatives prevent this abandonment by redirecting purchase intent [L0197.007].

### Trust, reviews, guarantees and FAQs

- Include an FAQ section if the product solves a problem, and display a risk-free or 30-day money-back guarantee section [L1260.031].
- Build the FAQ from the questions customers ask most, such as how soon results appear, how to take the product, the daily dose, what the product is made of and whether it is third-party tested. Place the FAQ below the product description, so buyers find answers before deciding [L0600.029].
- Answer common buyer questions, such as how the product works or how heavy it is, in a frequently asked questions section. This reduces doubt and helps the shopper decide [L1207.039].
- Answer common buyer questions on the page in an FAQ section. Each answered question removes a doubt and gives the buyer more peace of mind before buying [L1209.027].
- An FAQ section should answer the questions buyers typically have about how a product works, its size, weight or quality. Answering these on the page provides peace of mind at the point of decision [L1210.018].
- Competitor stores use a timeline section to show what buyers should expect over time. Adapt the rows to the product's real results [L0600.030].

| Period | What the timeline shows |
|---|---|
| Week 1 | An early, noticeable first sign, for example less loss or less build-up in daily use |
| Week 2 to 4 | A stronger, steadier change, for example more normal routine results |
| Day 30 to 90 | The full result the product is designed to deliver, for example visible improvement |

- Show the rating and customer count, for example "4.8 out of five from a thousand happy customers" [L1207.046].
- Testimonials worded as "customers like you," with a rating and review count [L1209.032].
- Address the buyer as part of a like-minded group, paired with a satisfaction count [L1210.024].
- Show happy customers as social proof, including customer testimonials and a star rating with the number of happy customers [L1210.024, L1209.032].
- Customer reviews placed near the end of the page, in detail [L1210.024, L1209.032].
- Place customer reviews below the feature list as social proof [L0918.029].
- Reviews and social proof section ideally with video testimonials. Image-based testimonials are more effective than text [L0321.004].
- Most visitors will scroll past nothing: essential content such as reviews must not sit only at the end of the page. According to heatmap figures cited in the example, about 10% of product page visitors reach the bottom, and a well-performing page reaches about 20% [L0409.013] (older, Source B 2021-22).
- A well-performing natural deodorant product page showed a 4.5-star rating with 16,969 reviews beside the buy area, a Sensitive Collection link, one-time and subscribe prices, and four benefit bullets under the add-to-cart button. Its description opened with the usual complaints about natural deodorants (they often fail to hide odor, go on chunky, and the market dislikes aluminum), then explained the ingredients' benefits and cited over 10,000 five-star reviews [L0409.018] (older, Source B 2021-22).
- In the desire section, add testimonials and reviews so visitors see that other buyers have the same experience. Also add a 30-day money-back guarantee to increase trust [L0916.013].
- A comparison section shows how the store differs from competitors. An FAQ section clears the small doubts a buyer still has before paying. Reviews and a 30-day money-back guarantee serve as the final safeguards for a visitor who is not yet fully convinced [L0916.037].
- Use a template with a proven layout logic, then replace copy and imagery with brand-specific content. The layout often follows psychological principles, so keep the structure and rewrite the content [L0918.025].
- A landing page should contain at least three or four sections that explain the product, connect with the audience and build trust. Useful sections are a how-to section, a testimonial slideshow, a money-back guarantee section, a brand comparison, an FAQ and a reviews section [L0916.024].
- If the product is complicated, or its use is not clear from the image, add a step-by-step usage section after the first or second section, such as Step 1, Step 2, Step 3. This section makes the purchase easier to trust [L0916.025].
- Three-step setup or usage, shown as simple numbered steps [L1209.032, L1210.024, L1207.046].
- Show a three-step easy setup, and address the cheap-knockoff objection directly [L1207.046].
- Show the 30-day money-back guarantee [L1207.046].
- Show the step-by-step guide (for example, Step 1, Step 2, Step 3) as part of the high-converting product page structure [L0321.004].
- Scarcity and urgency elements, such as a cart timer and low-stock messages, used only when true [L1207.046, L1209.032]. Cart timer, low-stock notice and time-limited offers [L1210.024].

### Comparison charts

- Add comparison charts on product pages where a higher-end version is a logical next choice, showing features such as capacity or size side by side. Comparison charts can be built with Shopify HTML, a free app from the App Store, or a freelancer [L1076.038].
- A self-made comparison chart takes about 1 to 3 hours and can be made free inside Shopify or with a free app [L1076.039].
- A comparison table helps visitors compare products side by side, which can support purchase decisions on product pages and on the home page [L1330.020].
- A comparison chart can highlight the features where a competitor is weaker, or better terms such as a longer money-back guarantee, instead of comparing against the most famous brands. Comparing to a traditional product or an older version of your own product is also acceptable and less aggressive [L0387.027].
- Brand comparison with three to five unique benefit points, placed as a comparison section against competitors or an older product version [L0321.004, L0387.020].
- Frame the product against competitors on the points where it wins [L1207.046].
- Objection handling: address cheap copies and competitors before the buyer raises them [L1210.024].

### Landing page structure: AIDA and section order

- Structure the landing page in AIDA order [L0916.004]:
  1. Attention: show a bold, clearly visible headline (USP, bold claim or benefit line) and a clear product image in the first view.
  2. Interest: add benefit-driven points and emotional experience copy that describes life with the product.
  3. Desire: add testimonials, reviews and a 30-day money-back guarantee.
  4. Action: add a call-to-action button after every two to three sections and one before the end of the page.
- The attention phase has to work within the first 2 to 5 seconds of a visitor landing on the page. Avoid large blocks of text in the first view, because they cause visitors to leave [L0916.005].
- AIDA (Attention, Interest, Desire, Action) is the structure used for landing page copy. In the attention part, the first screen should show the product name with its main USP, a testimonial or star rating, and payment trust logos such as PayPal and Visa [L1218.017].
- The first screen of a landing page should show the product name, a clear USP, a star rating or testimonial, and payment trust logos before the visitor has to scroll [L1218.019].
- Place a call to action button after every two or three sections and once right before the end of the page. Visitors should not need to scroll up and down to find the buy button [L0916.014].
- Landing page guide: the visitor should be led through four questions in order: what is this, why should I pay attention, should I believe you, and is it a good deal at this price. Structure the page to answer these in sequence while the visitor scrolls [L0387.019].
- Section order for a conversion-focused landing page [L0387.020]:
  1. Hero bar at the top: a headline that states clearly what the product is (with authority), the offer (price, discount), a reviews element, and a Shop Now button.
  2. Why bar: three short points with icons and big, simple words that explain the value (for example safety, design, value), with small details below for readers who want more.
  3. Brag bar: credibility proof such as customer reviews, press mentions or a counter-example.
  4. Shop section: product details with description, price, add-to-cart button, clean pictures and main features in bold.
  5. Reviews section, ideally with video testimonials.
  6. How-it-works section: step-by-step explanation combined with a picture or GIF of the product in use. If not applicable, use an icon-based value proposition section instead.
  7. Brag bar 2 or comparison chart against competitors or an older product version.
  8. Shop section 2: a repeat of the shop section, so the add-to-cart button is reachable without scrolling far.
  9. Footer.
- Section order for a product landing page, top to bottom [L1225.044]:
  1. Header.
  2. Brag bar.
  3. User-generated content.
  4. Product box.
  5. Second brag bar.
  6. Comparison chart.
  7. Description.
  8. Footer.
- Build order for a landing page for a store with similar products [L0918.015]:
  1. Create a main hero with the big value proposition and an offer or call to action.
  2. Add trust signals immediately, such as a review rating or testimonial.
  3. Add authority and social proof, such as press mentions, expert endorsements or customer counts.
  4. Add striking product visuals with benefit overlays on the images.
  5. Add risk-lowering elements, such as a free trial, easy cancellation or guarantees.
  6. Add benefit-focused copy and objection handling.
  7. Add a features list with each feature translated into a customer benefit.
  8. Add reviews and testimonials.
  9. Repeat calls to action further down the page.
- A complete landing page sequence [L0918.011]: a big benefit call-out, trust signals, striking visuals, risk reduction, the offer and a call to action at the top; then benefit-focused copy and objection handling; then product features and social proof; then calls to action further down. The page should build trust and reduce risk while showing the desired outcome.
- The offer and call to action should appear at the top of the landing page. Further calls to action should be placed lower, after the reader has absorbed the benefits and trust content, because some visitors need that persuasion before buying [L0918.008].
- A strong landing page surfaces the desired outcome, adds social proof and authority, lowers the risk for the customer (for example with money-back guarantees), offers a clear deal and call to action, and then shows the collection [L1315.039].
- A landing page should convey the desired outcome, not only the product: keep the page simple and focused on the pitch and the offer, so visitors do not have to move around [L1195.042].
- A dedicated features section is optional. Simpler landing pages can skip it and fold feature-to-benefit explanations into the benefit bullets. More complex product brands may use a separate features section that states each feature and its benefit [L0917.011].
- Use a template with proven layout logic, then replace copy and imagery with brand-specific content. The layout often follows psychological principles, so keep the structure and rewrite the content [L0918.025].
- Different visitors respond to different benefits, especially when paid traffic comes from different Facebook interest groups. A landing page should carry several benefit lines and several call-to-action points, so each type of visitor finds a reason to buy somewhere on the page [L0916.036].
- Six core elements of a strong sales page: a hero section, persuasive copy, trust building, objection handling, an irresistible offer, and removal of anxiety and risk. Design each element deliberately rather than leaving it to chance [L0919.006].
- A single-product sales page, working from top to bottom [L0919.035]: a hero section with a benefit headline and social proof, a discount or offer, benefit-driven copy that surfaces the problem, features that prove the benefits, a comparison table, guarantees and risk reversal, FAQs, then a final block of customer reviews.
- Show the desired outcome and make the offer clear in one pass. A strong landing page surfaces the desired outcome first [L1315.039].
- Products with several similar offers: route the visitor to each product directly and avoid bundling unrelated items on the landing page [L0918.017, L0916.030].
- A landing page must answer what the buyer will get in the first view. Show the offer clearly and keep the product name, benefit and call to action as the first elements seen [L0916.001, L0919.007].
- The hero section sits above the fold and must communicate the value proposition, striking visuals, early trust signals, the offer and a clear call to action on the first screen. Visitors should be able to scan it quickly and decide whether to buy [L0919.007].

### Hero section build

- Six hero elements for a high-converting landing page: a clear value proposition, striking visuals, trust signals, risk reduction, an offer, and a call to action. Missing any one of them lowers the chance that a visitor with buying intent converts [L1363.002].
- A landing page hero needs a strong headline built around one big benefit claim, striking visuals, trust signals such as social proof, risk or loss reduction, an offer and a call to action. Copy is primarily the big benefit plus benefit bullet points placed directly beneath it [L0917.001].
- Hero section build order used for a top-converting page [L1363.007]:
  1. Title that includes the free gift or offer.
  2. Benefit callout stating the desired outcome in plain language.
  3. Review badge (social proof).
  4. Offer line, for example 40% off plus free gift.
  5. Short testimonial.
  6. Trust line with free shipping and a 30-day money-back guarantee.
  7. Three benefit bullets written as what the product does for the customer, not features.
  8. Add-to-cart call to action.
  9. Secure checkout trust line and a risk-free note (30 days).
- The first screen should show the product name, a clear USP, a star rating or testimonial and payment trust logos before the visitor scrolls [L1218.019].
- Copy is primarily the big benefit plus benefit bullets placed directly beneath it [L0917.001].

### Landing page copy: case and benchmarks

- A conversion rate of 7% was achieved on the Shaper Loop template on TikTok. This is a tested benchmark after 3 days of traffic [L0321.005].
- Starting point: the store's conversion rate was 1.85%. The owner rebuilt the landing page with the AIDA structure, added the five key first-view elements (imagery, bold headline, social proof, call to action and trust badges), replaced studio images with user-generated images, used a brand palette of black, white and one brand colour, removed distracting product sections, and added testimonials, a 30-day money-back guarantee and trust badges. After these changes the conversion rate rose to roughly 4.25%, and at that level daily ad spend of about 2,000 to 3,000 was possible. The lesson does not state the currency [L0916.035].
- Trust elements on a product page can be built from the same set: star reviews, testimonials, a secure checkout badge and a benefit-driven subheadline [L1259.018, L1260.029].

### Single-product landing page templates

- Section order for a product landing page [L1210.024]:
  1. Product title: use a unique custom product name instead of the supplier's generic product name, which can be less competitive in search.
  2. Headline: a bold benefit claim that states the desired outcome.
  3. Social proof: customer testimonials and a star rating shown with the number of happy customers.
  4. Price frame: show a comparison price next to the sale price and state the saving as a plain figure.
  5. Shorthand USPs: short benefit lines for skimmers, such as suitability for all users or no experience required.
  6. Trust and risk signals: payment and security badges, international delivery, secure payment, responsive customer service and easy returns.
  7. Peer-group line: address the buyer as part of a like-minded group, paired with a satisfaction count.
  8. Problem statement: a universal pain point, followed by the product as the fix, dramatized with a metaphor.
  9. Features as proof: each feature tied to the promised outcome.
  10. Three-step setup: usage or setup shown as three simple steps.
  11. Objection handling: address cheap copies and competitors before the buyer raises them.
  12. Guarantee: money-back guarantee with a clearly stated period.
  13. FAQ: answer common questions about how the product works, its size and its weight.
  14. Reviews: detailed customer reviews placed near the end of the page.
  15. Urgency and scarcity: cart timer, low-stock notice and time-limited offers.
  
  After checkout: a "today only" post-purchase upsell.
- Order of elements on a single-product landing page, top to bottom [L1207.046]:
  1. Give the product a unique custom title that is not the generic supplier name.
  2. Show a large, bold benefit headline that states the desired outcome.
  3. Show happy customers as social proof.
  4. Show a comparison price next to the sale price, with a simple discount label.
  5. Add short unique selling points that lower effort or sacrifice.
  6. Add trust badges and trust signals: international delivery, secure payment, fast customer service, easy returns.
  7. Add "customers like you" wording.
  8. Show the rating and customer count, for example 4.8 out of five from a thousand happy customers.
  9. Name the universal pain point, then introduce the product as the fix.
  10. Describe the outcome with a vivid, dramatised metaphor.
  11. List the features that support the outcome and prove it is reachable.
  12. Show a three-step easy setup.
  13. Address the cheap-knockoff objection directly.
  14. Frame the product against competitors on the points where it wins.
  15. Show the 30-day money-back guarantee.
  16. Add a frequently asked questions section.
  17. Add customer reviews.
  18. Add scarcity and urgency elements, such as a cart timer and low-stock messages.
- Order of sections for a product landing page [L1209.032]:
  1. Product name, using a distinctive name rather than a generic one.
  2. Headline with the big desired outcome, a novelty word and an effort-reducing word.
  3. Social proof with happy customer numbers.
  4. Sale price next to a comparison price, with the exact discount percentage.
  5. Short USP lines for skimmers and high-intent visitors.
  6. Trust signals: secure checkout, payment badges, international delivery, secure payment, customer service and easy returns.
  7. Testimonials worded as "customers like you," with a rating and review count.
  8. Problem section that names a universal pain, then introduces the product as the fix.
  9. Features, each shown as proof that the desired outcome is reachable.
  10. Simple setup in three numbered steps.
  11. Objection handling, such as warnings about cheap imitations, plus a competitor comparison.
  12. Money-back guarantee.
  13. FAQ answering common buyer questions.
  14. Customer review section.
  15. Scarcity and urgency elements, such as a cart timer or low-stock notice, used only when true.
- Step-by-step product page template for a single-product store, with the full sequence in the table above [L1210.024].
- Product page order for a single-product landing page with all 18 elements, in the list above [L1207.046].
- Product page order for a landing page with steps 1 to 15, as above [L1209.032].
- Product page structure, step by step [L0321.004]:
  1. Star ratings (trust signal).
  2. Payment badges (trust).
  3. One-line USP highlighting benefit, not features.
  4. Four benefit-driven bullet points describing the end experience for the customer.
  5. Slideshow with product images and user reviews.
  6. Image-based testimonials, which are more effective than text.
  7. GIFs demonstrating product use and setup (how-to steps).
  8. Paragraph section with a benefit-driven narrative.
  9. Step-by-step guide (for example, Step 1, Step 2, Step 3).
  10. Comparison section showing unique advantages.
  11. Brand comparison with 3 to 5 unique benefit points.
  12. 30-day money-back guarantee section.
  13. FAQ section with 4 questions and answers.
  14. Reviews and social proof section.
  15. Call-to-action button in the brand colour, with the text "Get My [Product] Now," directing to checkout.
- Product page sales-page sequence for a single product [L0919.035] as listed above.
- Product page order for a single-product template [L1013.022] as listed above.
- Benefits, how-it-works and how-to-use content ordered as in the seven-step product page layout [L1194.031].

### Inverted product pages

- An inverted product page looks like a homepage but is built as a product page. It works for both warm and cold traffic. It lets you test products quickly without writing a separate advertorial or listicle for every angle [L0772.024].

### Ready-to-paste blocks and prompts

- Product page bonus block, ready to paste; replace the bracketed placeholders [L0860.022]:

```
🎁 FREE BONUS
Get Our Exclusive Ebook

"[EBOOK TITLE AND SUBTITLE]"

Value: $[VALUE]

Included Free Today
```

- Prompt for reworking the top banner and the above-the-fold part of the product page [L0886.003]:

```
Rework the top banner of the homepage and the part of the product page visible without scrolling so the core promise reads more plainly and precisely and is geared toward closing a sale. Stay true to the brand's personality, but make sure a shopper instantly understands why they should buy now.
```

- Use the ready-made structure of a template, but keep the content in the brand's own words so that buyers see what is specific to the product [L0918.025].

### Common product page mistakes

- Common product page errors include showing only one image, showing the sale price without the MSRP discount, stacking all content in one long page, and copying supplier features without explaining their benefit [L1130.033].
- Copying supplier features without a benefit leaves the buyer without a reason to buy. Each feature needs a benefit line that describes life with the product [L1130.033, L0409.015].
- Make sure the page does not become too crowded. Remove FAQ items that make the page feel crowded [L1331.029].
- Add FAQs to the product page with these steps [L1331.029]:
  1. Add a collapsible row block to the page.
  2. Add a collapsible content block inside it.
  3. Create one question and answer per collapsible item.
  4. Place the FAQ near the end of the page.
  5. Remove any FAQ items that make the page feel crowded.

### Product page and landing page structure

### Work order: offer first, then trust

- Converting a visitor into a buyer depends on two areas: the offer and the trust signals on the site. Work on both, starting with the offer, because visitors judge the page within seconds. Judge the product only after both are in place. [L1382.029, L1436.032]
- Older advice (2021-22): clarity, quick understanding of the product, a clear delivery of the core offer, and the marketing message matter most on a product page. Trust cues, bundles, and recommendations add value only once these basics work. [L1522.041]

### Hero and above the fold

- A visitor forms a first impression from the first image and the headline or product name, so both must be strong. [L1436.035]
- The headline should be emotional and benefit-driven and address the visitor's current pain point. [L1436.035]
- Supplement pages open with a hero headline that states a clear benefit, plus a discount banner (for example "Lowest price of the year" or "Up to 70% off until midnight"). [L1479.021]

### Section order

- Reference order from a top-selling supplement product page, top to bottom: [L1479.021]
  1. Hero headline with a clear benefit and a discount banner (for example "Lowest price of the year" or "Up to 70% off until midnight"). [L1479.021]
  2. Stock label such as "Low stock - selling fast". [L1479.021]
  3. Star rating with review count near the top (for example 5.0 with 3,749 reviews). [L1479.021]
  4. Price tiers for single, multi-pack, and subscription purchases. [L1479.021]
  5. Benefit bullets and a "Power through your day" style section. [L1479.021]
  6. "Backed by science" section with study and ingredient claims. [L1479.021]
  7. Supplement facts panel. [L1479.021]
  8. Customer photos and reviews. [L1479.021]
  9. How it works section. [L1479.021]
  10. FAQ. [L1479.021]
  11. Shipping information. [L1479.021]

### Mobile-first layout

- Most visitors look at the top of the page on mobile, so the hero must work on a phone screen. Use this sequence: offer, benefit, social proof, more benefits, add-to-cart, then a risk-free message. [L1363.009]
  1. Offer. [L1363.009]
  2. Benefit. [L1363.009]
  3. Social proof. [L1363.009]
  4. More benefits. [L1363.009]
  5. Add-to-cart. [L1363.009]
  6. Risk-free message. [L1363.009]

### Buy box content

- On a supplement product page, show the dosage per serving (for example 1300 mg per serving) and the supply length (for example a 30-day supply) close to the price and the star rating. Buyers can then judge value before they scroll down. [L1479.033]
- Showing the fit and shape of the item on the product page before checkout helps buyers decide with confidence. This supports the sale and reduces returns. [L1438.025]

### Sticky add-to-cart

- A sticky add-to-cart button helps visitors who are ready to buy, including buyers who have already seen the full product description. It stays visible while the visitor scrolls, so the purchase is one tap away, and they can buy at any point without scrolling back through the description. [L1382.034, L1436.038]

### CTA placement and labels

- Older advice (2021-22): place a call-to-action button at the bottom of the product page and reconfirm the price there too. This helps mobile shoppers who scroll through the full description without scrolling back to the top. [L1522.015]

### Tabs, accordions, FAQs, and comparison charts

- A comparison table that separates the brand from competitors is useful once the product is known in the market. [L1363.018]
- Frequently asked questions address common buyer objections. [L1363.018]
- FAQ sits near the end of the supplement page, after the "How it works" section. [L1479.021]

### Templates and single-product pages

- Only remove sections of the template when needed. Keep every element in the top section (hero), keep at least one testimonial, the trust signals, the value adds, and the benefits somewhere on the page. Minimal designs are allowed once these are in place. [L1363.029]
- For a simple dropshipping product page, use a short intro paragraph at the top, show the product in use with a GIF near the top, and list five to six key benefits as bullet points. [L1380.054]
- Keep the homepage short and avoid extra content that slows the buying decision. [L1380.054]

## Copywriting and messaging

### Copy foundations and the job of copy

- Copywriting is persuasive writing that aims at one specific action, such as a click, an opt-in or an add to cart. In ecommerce most of these actions are add to cart and purchase, so any product page text that pushes toward them is copywriting. (older, 2021-22) [L0396.001]
- Content writing informs and educates, but a product description has to sell the product. Describing what the product is does not persuade on its own. (older, 2021-22) [L0396.002]
- The marketing message can be delivered as text, video, images or any combination, and no single format is required. What matters is that every element of the marketing message is present, whatever the format. (older, 2021-22) [L0396.003]
- Copy has to answer three questions:
  | Question | Where the answer comes from |
  |---|---|
  | Why buy this product? | Mainly the marketing message |
  | Why buy it from you? | Can be covered by a unique selling proposition of up to five points |
  | Why buy it now? | Mainly the marketing message |

  (older, 2021-22) [L0396.005]
- Copy does not need to be the best copy in the world. It only needs to be more developed than the marketing messages of direct competitors to persuade customers to buy from you. (older, 2021-22) [L0396.016]
- Marketing messages for the product (copy, videos and images) should be better than competitors' messages. This element is separate from the offer structure and gets its own treatment later. (older, 2021-22) [L0411.022]
- Write website copy from the customer's perspective. Answer their questions before they ask them, such as shipping time, return policy and product materials, so they feel comfortable buying without uncertainty. (older, 2020) [L0268.033]
- Education builds trust by giving useful information before asking for money. Explaining the problem and its causes gives the reader value up front and makes the product feel like the logical answer. [L0516.035]
- Consistency also means people want their actions to match the identity they show publicly. Copy that appeals to an identity, such as "of course you want the best for your pet", makes the reader more likely to act in line with it. [L0516.018]
- A complete landing page must surface the customer's problems, accentuate benefits, demonstrate the features that prove those benefits, show the desired outcome, and handle objections, usually in an FAQ section. [L0917.003]
- Use an AI chat tool such as ChatGPT to rewrite benefits in a more striking or conversational style, and to produce problem-solution copy and feature-to-benefit lists from the product description. If the copy is weak, use such a tool to draft it. [L0917.008]
- Specific, consumer-addressed wording in guarantees and product copy outperforms generic phrasing, because generic text reads as template text that buyers have seen many times. [L0577.003]
- Copy quality rarely decides a page alone. A poor headline or unclear page is a common reason for a 1 to 2 percent conversion rate, and small changes to landing page structure often produce larger gains than split testing. [L0387.026]

### Bottom-of-funnel and psychological framing

- The path to conversion runs through negative emotions, not around them. Bottom-of-funnel ads must address the psychological state of prospects by first acknowledging the problem they face, before presenting how your solution transforms their situation. [L0221.008]
- To generate bottom-of-funnel ads, ask Claude for ad copy and concepts built from all gathered market research, the emotional framework analysis and the target evidence metrics. Claude creates the ad copy and concepts from the customer psychology and language patterns you provide. [L0221.011]
- Competitor headlines pair a specific benefit with a time frame, which makes the promise measurable and sets clear expectations. Use the same formula of benefit plus time window, for example *Fall Asleep Faster in 14 Days* or *Feel Less Stiff by Week Three*. Only use time frames you can back with real customer results. [L0600.031]

### Mapping the product before writing

- Product copy is built from separate elements such as headline, benefits, features and proof, not written in one pass from top to bottom. Gathering these elements in a structured document before writing reduces the stall that comes from facing an empty product description box, and it shortens total writing time even though it adds a step. (older, 2021-22) [L1160.001]
- Answer these questions about the product before writing any copy, in this order:
  1. Product name (used as the tab name and header, so the record can be tracked)
  2. Primary problem: the one main problem the product solves for most customers
  3. Secondary problems: any additional problems the product solves
  4. Unique mechanism(s): the attribute that solves the primary problem in a way competing products do not
  5. How the unique mechanism works: the process that produces the result
  6. Features: the attributes of the product you would tell a customer to justify buying it
  7. Benefit of each feature: a list that mirrors the feature list point for point
  8. How the product improves the ideal customer's life: a picture of what the customer experiences once they have used it
  9. Proof points: reviews, demonstration videos, third-party tests

  (older, 2021-22) [L1160.002]
- Use a Google Sheets product mapping template for the exercise. Make a copy and save it to Google Drive, or convert it to Excel if you prefer to work offline. (older, 2021-22) [L1160.003]
- Keep a master template tab in the mapping sheet. For each new product, right-click the template tab and choose Duplicate, then right-click the copy and choose Rename to set the product name. Each product then gets its own tab in one file, so products can be flicked between and compared. (older, 2021-22) [L1160.004]
- The primary problem should be the biggest and most common problem the product solves for most customers, because people buy a product to solve a problem they have. Pick that one for the main slot and record any other problems as secondary problems. (older, 2021-22) [L1160.005]
- Secondary problems can be recorded in addition to the primary problem. A product may solve several, and that is not a problem. Only the biggest one goes into the main slot when writing copy. (older, 2021-22) [L1160.006]
- A unique mechanism is an attribute of the product that solves the primary problem in a way competing products do not. Ideally it is built into the product, such as a technology or a specially designed feature, and a product can have more than one. (older, 2021-22) [L1160.007]
- Claiming a unique feature without explaining how it works is a mistake. After naming a unique mechanism, always state how it produces the result, otherwise the claim is not convincing. (older, 2021-22) [L1160.008]
- Five product features is the recommended number to list. Three is acceptable if you want to focus, fewer than five is fine, and beyond five the value of each added feature drops. (older, 2021-22) [L1160.009]
