- Judge.me marks reviews as verified in the widget on the product page. The verified label helps visitors trust them. [L0908.021]
- On the Judge.me free plan, the star colour can be changed with the colour picker in the review widget settings. [L0908.023]

#### Looks (Product Reviews and Photos)

1. Install Looks (Product Reviews and Photos) from the Shopify App Store.
2. Choose the Beginner plan (it starts with a 7-day free trial).
3. Click Approve, Continue, and Add Looks to your theme; keep it enabled and save.
4. Click Add rating widget and place it, for example just after the product title, then save.
5. In the admin, set the review request timing to 14 days after delivery (21 days if delivery is slow; a discount offer to customers can help, and 10 is also mentioned).
6. Enter the admin and click Import reviews. [L0635.017]

#### Looks: importing AliExpress reviews

1. Drag the Looks import bookmarklet to your bookmarks bar.
2. Open the product on AliExpress and click the bookmark to import it to Looks.
3. Set filters: four stars and up, all countries, translate to English, only reviews with photos, most recent.
4. Click Preview, then Import.
5. Check Manage reviews in the admin; the reviews appear on the product page after a refresh. [L0635.018]

#### LUX Reviews

1. Install LUX Reviews from the Shopify App Store.
2. Choose the Beginner plan: click the 14 day free trial option, then take Beginner instead, and approve.
3. Click Continue and set the store's primary language to English.
4. Choose the star colour: your brand colour or the default gold.
5. Set the review request timing to 28 days, which means after the product should have arrived following fulfillment.
6. Keep the picture review reward at 15% discount, then continue. [L1297.010]

#### LUX Reviews: importing AliExpress reviews

1. Drag the LUX import button to your bookmarks bar.
2. Open the matching AliExpress product page and click the bookmark.
3. Import into your store's product URL.
4. Filter for 4 stars and up, all countries, with reviews translated to English.
5. Select the product, leave extra filters (only with pictures, most recent) off at first, and click Import.
6. Go through the reviews and import only those with good text and pictures, then click Finish importing.
7. Check the Reviews section and the product page to confirm the count and ratings. [L1297.011]

- Start with the LUX Beginner plan and upgrade to Growth or Unlimited once the store starts to scale. [L1297.012]
- In the example, 19 five-star reviews were imported, and the product page then showed 19 five-star reviews. [L1297.013]

#### Stamped.io

- Start Stamped.io on the free-forever plan at launch, because a store with no orders does not need paid review features yet. Move to a paid plan once order volume makes the extra functions worth their cost. (older, 2021-2022) [L1298.014]
- Paying for a higher review plan before the store has orders wastes money. Higher plans only pay for themselves once order volume and display needs grow. (older, 2021-2022) [L1298.017]
- Stamped.io collects product reviews, photo reviews, video reviews, checkout reviews, community questions and answers, Net Promoter Score and Instagram curation. It also supports review requests with an in-email form, manual review requests, and importing AliExpress and Facebook reviews. (older, 2021-2022) [L1298.016]
- Stamped.io's AliExpress reviews importer lets dropshippers import reviews for AliExpress-sourced products with one click. It is part of the Reviews & UGC app. (older, 2021-2022) [L1298.023]

##### Stamped.io plans

| Plan | Audience | Monthly price | Free SMS credits | Order limit |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Free | Free-forever | $0 | none | Up to 50 orders |
| Basic | For Individuals | $23 | $19 | Up to 200 orders |
| Premium | For Professionals | $49 | $39 | Up to 500 orders |
| Business | For Growing Businesses | $119 | $99 | Up to 1500 orders |
| Professional | For Established Brands | $299 | $149 | Up to 3500 orders |

Paid plans include a 3-day free trial and can be cancelled at any time. [L1298.015]

##### Stamped.io setup

1. Install Stamped.io Product Reviews UGC from the Shopify App Store (search for stamped.io).
2. Click through the welcome screens. Skip the selling step because the store is not selling yet, then click Next.
3. In the customization step, set up the automatic review request email that is sent a number of days after the order.
4. On the Choose a Plan page, select the free-forever plan.
5. On the import step, skip importing past orders because the store has none.
6. In the main Reviews & UGC platform, open Customize Main Widget to adjust the review widget. Optionally connect social links and add a logo. (older, 2021-2022) [L1298.020]

#### Junip

- Junip can show review stars under product titles across the whole store, not only on the product page. This puts ratings in front of shoppers before they click into a product. [L0589.008]

### Widget placement and display

- An empty review widget, with no sign of any store reviews on the page, removes social proof entirely. Add a Reviews tab or section with store reviews, even when the individual product has none yet. (older, 2021-2022) [L0409.037]
- Place a customer review bubble above the collapsible tabs so the most persuasive quote is seen as the buyer scrolls. Keep the quote short and specific to a benefit the product delivers. [L1188.043]
- Use real customer testimonials and reviews on the page. The more people report success, the more shoppers trust that the product will work for them. [L1207.022]
- Reviews placed on product pages are meant to increase conversions. The vendor claims double-digit conversion gains. This is a vendor claim, not a verified benchmark, so measure the effect on your own product pages before relying on it. [L0589.007]
- Show the star rating and count near the buy button, using real customer feedback. Competitor stores use emoji-style reviews and short testimonials with first name and last initial, plus customer photos when available. [L0600.028]

#### Customer video testimonial placement

- Embed customer videos in a gallery-style section of the theme or page builder. [L0387.029]

### Styling the widget

- Match the star colour to the store's main brand colour so the stars stay consistent with the store design. A gold or yellow tone is a common alternative if the brand colour does not work. [L0908.023]
- In Stamped.io, use Reviews & UGC > Customize > Customize Main Widget to match the widget to the store's colours and layout. The widget appears on product pages automatically and displays star ratings. (older, 2021-2022) [L1298.021]
- Style review widgets after reviews are live, so colours and layout match the real content. With no reviews yet, the widget will show little on the storefront. (older, 2021-2022) [L1298.022]
- In LUX Reviews, choose the star colour as your brand colour or the default gold during setup. [L1297.010]

## Urgency, scarcity and promotions

### Why urgency and expiring offers work

- Expiring coupon codes create urgency and force the visitor to decide. They convert better than permanent discount offers. [L0137.001]
- Expiration forces a binary choice: claim the discount now or risk missing it. This reduces hesitation and speeds up purchase decisions. [L0137.007]
- In one store, expiring coupon codes doubled sales compared with non-expiring coupons. Introducing coupons without expiration first raised sales by 30%. [L0137.006]
- Most potential customers decide within two to three days. An expiring coupon accelerates that decision, and estimated delivery dates reduce hesitation and support questions. Both substantially increase conversion rates. [L0139.001]
- Most visitors decide within two to three days of first finding a store, though some buy immediately and others take much longer. [L1192.001]
- Visitors are often distracted by other tabs, devices, messages and competitor sites, so they postpone buying. A visible deadline pushes visitors who are close to buying to decide during the visit. [L0531.001]
- Visitors who arrive from searches for the exact products being sold are already near a purchase decision. A time-limited offer shown at that moment shortens the time between interest and buying. [L0531.002]
- An expiring coupon on the product page displays a deadline and pushes visitors to decide now rather than postpone. [L1192.002]
- Urgency should come from the link between the customer's problem and the solution, not from invented scarcity. Discounts, limited stock and time-limited offers create urgency only up to a point. [L0396.011]
- The strongest urgency comes from the customer's problem: show that the product solves it now and that the purchase is the only step left. Stores with products people genuinely want usually need no countdown timers or fake scarcity. [L0473.029]
- Older (2022): twice-weekly coupon codes expiring in 2 to 3 days create urgency on product pages, and most first-time visitors decide within that timeframe. [L0249.030]

### Which products suit expiring codes

- Expiring codes suit high-ticket products that customers buy only every few years, because about 90% of site visitors are first-time visitors who have never seen the code. [L0531.006]
- For low-ticket recurring products with many repeat visitors, the same code seen again and again is likely to work less well. [L0531.006]
- Use scarcity only when it is genuine, and use it sparingly. If a product is always on sale or sales run every week, repeat visitors stop reacting to urgency. (Older: 2021-22.) [L1522.011]
- The average e-commerce customer has about seven touchpoints before buying, so many shoppers return several times. A scarcity message that stays on the page for weeks loses credibility. (Older: 2021-22.) [L1522.012]

### Expiring coupon settings

| Setting | Value or rule | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Text field | State the discount amount and its scope, such as 5% off all orders, with each word capitalized for consistency | [L1192.012] |
| Code | Enclose the code in bold brackets so it appears bold, and type the exact name of the discount code created in Shopify | [L1192.013] |
| Countdown | Set to three days, the recommended value | [L1192.014] |
| Colour | Choose from the theme colours using the colour icon | [L1192.015] |
| Date format | Defaults to month, day, year; change it for stores selling outside the US | [L1192.015] |
| End date | Does not deactivate the Shopify discount code; it only resets the countdown shown on the page, which restarts at the configured number of days | [L1192.016] |
| Automatic refresh | The box recalculates its deadline every three days, so visitors always see a fresh three-day window; set it once, then edit over time | [L1192.019] |
| Refresh and checkout | The displayed expiry refreshes every three days, and the code stays active at checkout regardless of the countdown display | [L0139.005] |

- The discount text and code shown on the product page must match the discount created in the Shopify Discounts section exactly, or shoppers will not get the advertised price. [L1192.017]
- Keep the coupon text limited to what the code actually grants. Benefits that apply to every order anyway, such as free shipping offered on all orders, should be removed from the coupon message. [L1192.018]
- Test different discount percentages over time to optimize conversion. [L0139.005]

### Shopify coupon rules

- Coupon codes cannot be combined. If a 5% and a 10% code both exist, the customer picks one and does not receive 15% off. (Older: 2021.) [L1090.072]
- A coupon discount is applied in the cart when the code is entered, not on the product page. Product prices shown on the store stay at MAP (the compare-at price), and the discount appears only at checkout. (Older: 2021.) [L1090.073]
- Shopify's coupon settings can exclude specific products from a promotion. Set the exclusion in the Shopify promotion settings where coupon codes are configured. [L0964.129]
- In the Shopify promotion settings where coupon codes are configured, exclude the products that are not eligible. The discount then applies only to eligible products in the cart. [L0964.130]
- Coupon codes can be set to show only on eligible product pages, so the discount is not shown on non-eligible products. [L0964.131]
- The code shown in the bar must match a discount code that already exists in Shopify, because the bar only displays the code and Shopify applies it at checkout. [L0531.013]

### Announcement bar: placement and behaviour

- Show the discount code prominently on every page, in a bar or banner that stands out, so every visitor sees it on arrival. [L0137.004]
- The announcement bar appears on every page, including the visitor's first view, so use it for the most important site-wide offer or information. [L1326.002]
- The announcement bar is used for urgency and conversion, so it only makes sense once the store has live products and real traffic. Before that, hide it in the Announcement bar section. [L0775.004]
- Show the sticky top bar on every page, including product pages, on both desktop and mobile. After saving, refresh the storefront and check that the bar stays visible while scrolling. [L0531.019]
- Keep the announcement bar non-clickable when it applies sitewide. Visitors should see the code and remember it for the cart rather than leave the page. Do not add a close button, so the offer stays visible. [L0531.018]
- A promotion bar with light text can get lost on the background. Test a higher-contrast text colour, such as black, to make the sale message stand out. Test the bar on all pages and for all visitors, mobile first. [L0389.002]
- The bar can also hold a second short text at the start, such as a highlight word like `Hot`. When a discount is running, show the discount code in the bar so visitors see it immediately. [L1326.005]
- A sample bar could read a weekend sitewide sale of 25% off everything, paired with a code for a free gift on orders over a set amount, running for a limited time. Enter both messages as separate announcements in the same bar. [L1230.006]
- Older (2020): Hello Bar, or a similar dynamic notification app, can display warehouse status notifications above the Add to Cart button. The text can change based on product collection or supplier, informing customers of shipping status before purchase. [L0245.044]

### Announcement bar message patterns

- Pattern: state the discount amount and its scope, then the coupon code, then the expiry day, time and time zone. It works because it gives a concrete saving, a clear action (enter the code) and a visible deadline in one line. [L0531.003]
  - Example 1: `Take 7% off all orders with code SPRING7. Offer ends Wednesday 11:59 p.m. PST.`
  - Example 2: `Get 5% off your whole order with code HOME5 until Sunday 11:59 p.m. EST.`
- Express the deadline with the day, the time and the time zone, for example Sunday 11:59 p.m. EST. Any time zone works as long as the deadline is clear and consistent. [L0531.007]
- Announcement bar messages work best when they combine three parts: an offer or benefit, a condition or threshold, and a credibility signal or icon. This keeps the message quick to scan and gives a reason to act. [L1326.003]
  - Examples: `Free delivery on orders over $60 | 20% off your first order` and `Trusted by 8,000+ happy customers | Free returns within 30 days`.
- Template for a holiday promotion line. Use it in the announcement bar, and keep the same wording and code in the homepage slider and on the product pages' expiring discount code section while the promotion runs. [L1224.037]

```
[HOLIDAY] promotion live now: [OFFER] with code [CODE], guaranteed delivery before [DELIVERY DEADLINE].
```

- If the bar still shows an expired code or an old expiry date after a cycle change, the urgency is lost and visitors may distrust the offer. Update the bar at every Wednesday and Sunday change. [L0531.008]

### Header space and scarcity lines

- The header bar is the most visible space on the site. Reserve it for the strongest current offer, such as an expiring coupon code. Do not spend it on a generic message that competitors already show, such as a shipping threshold. [L0894.041]
- A header with a scarcity line such as `buy now before we run out of stock`, or a limited stock and free shipping today message, can increase conversion. The reviewer reported that customers wrote in asking for the bundle not to be removed after this header was added. [L0530.020]
- Scarcity and call-to-action messages in the header are a proven conversion tool and should be used on a store, not left out. [L0530.021]
- Scarcity text is less effective now than before, because many shoppers know the tactic. Use scarcity sparingly and test it rather than relying on it. [L0657.041]

### Cart top bar and checkout banners

- Pattern for an urgency warning in the cart top bar: a warning label, a specific sold-out count over a period, and a call to order now before the product runs out again. The specific count makes the scarcity credible, and the deadline phrasing pushes the decision. [L0560.029]
  - Example 1: `Stock alert: this item has sold out 6 times this year. Secure yours now before it runs out again.`
  - Example 2: `Almost gone: we sold out twice in the last 30 days. Check out today before the next batch sells.`
- Pattern for a low-stock banner at checkout: a short stock-level statement that the product is running low, followed by a clear action to finish the order before the stock is gone. It works best in the checkout banner rather than the cart. [L0560.030]
  - Example 1: `Units are running low on this bundle. Finish checkout now to lock in today's price.`
  - Example 2: `Only a few units remain. Place your order today to avoid a backorder wait.`

### Promotion consistency across the site

- Promote every offer across the whole website, not only in emails and ads, so all visitors know the offer exists. Use the notification bar and the exit-intent overlay for this. [L0757.031]
- The website must show the current promotion's offer in the homepage slider, the announcement bar and an expiring discount code, and it must match the offers in the emails. [L1222.010]
- Use the same promotion message in every place: the announcement bar, the homepage image slider, the discount code section on product pages, and the email and ads. Mismatched codes or percentages, such as 5 percent in one place and 7 percent in another, confuse shoppers and reduce trust. [L1287.062]
- Keep the discount code and offer identical across the announcement bar, the homepage, product pages and the discount code section. Never show 5% in one place and 7% or free shipping in another. Mismatched offers confuse buyers and reduce sales. [L1224.031]
- Show active coupons and brand deals with their expiry dates directly on the homepage and category pages. Burying promotions behind a small link such as "see all sales" hides them from shoppers who are not looking for a sale. [L0894.040]
- Create a permanent collection page for rotating promotions, such as yourstore.com/promotions or yourstore.com/sales. Add and swap products into it as seasonal sales or supplier promotions become available. Customers can bookmark it and return during sales, and you manage the included products without creating new collection pages each time. (2023.) [L0251.058]
- Promo bars on product pages must match the product. A generic outdoor sale banner on a chandelier page makes no sense to the shopper, so tailor on-page messages to the product category. [L0894.023]
- Remove banners for past events, such as a Black Friday banner still showing in May, because stale promotions make the store look neglected. (Older: 2021-22.) [L0409.031]
- Older (2021): use a header banner with an expiring coupon to boost early conversions. Once real traffic arrives, replace an earlier free shipping banner with a 10% off banner, but not before suppliers are on board. [L1090.070]
- Older (2021): the discount is applied at checkout, so product prices stay at MAP (see the Shopify coupon rules above). [L1090.073]

### Free gifts and landing page text

- Template for free gift image text on a landing page (transparent background, brand font, product image). Place it just below the benefit line. [L1363.021]

```
Your order comes with a free gift
[gift title]
automatically added to cart
```

- A second promotion message, such as a free gift code on orders over a set amount, can run in the same bar as a separate announcement. [L1230.006]

### Discount size and margin

- A discount should only be offered if your margin still covers it. Test whether the discount raises conversion, and base the size on current margins. (Older: 2020.) [L0468.096]
- For high-priced items, fixed-amount offers work better than a percentage with a cap, such as spend 1,000 USD and save 100 USD. (Older: 2021.) [L1090.074]
- Percentage discounts on expensive products can still be profitable when margins are large. A 10% discount on a high-ticket sale can still leave thousands of dollars in profit. (Older: 2021.) [L1090.074]
- If the margin does not allow a discount in the announcement bar or exit-intent popup, use a free shipping offer for those locations instead. (2023.) [L0855.117]
- Traffic from TikTok expects strong discounts. Small offers such as 5 or 10 percent off rarely work, so test discounts of at least 20 to 50 percent. [L1259.024]
- A small discount such as 5% off a $3,000 product with a six-hour validity will not change the buying decision. Expensive products need a clear value message, not a token discount. (Older: 2021-22.) [L0409.027]

### Countdown timers on product pages

- A countdown timer on the product page shows a short validity window for a special offer, for example 10 minutes, after which the offer disappears. It works when no fixed calendar date is used and pushes visitors to act much faster than usual. [L0683.006]
- A countdown timer set to about 10 minutes worked well for one seller, who had also tested 5 minutes. [L1381.039]
- Configure the countdown timer app (Ultimate Sales Boost was named) so the timer disappears after it expires and does not repeat for the same visitor. A repeating timer can make the offer look fake and hurt sales. [L1381.040]
- A countdown timer next to a limited-time discount for new customers reinforces the deadline and supports the urgency of the offer. Place it where the offer is visible, such as on the product or landing page. [L1399.008]
- Show the remaining stock count on the product page with a countdown, so buyers have a reason to decide now instead of later. The author says this works well, using custom code in his Shopify theme. [L1346.016]
- A countdown timer that restarts with every visit looks fake. A timer that shows three hours and resets on every visit removes trust, and a returning visitor who sees the same three-hour timer will stop trusting the store. [L0355.018]
- Older (2021-22): a countdown timer that restarts each day is fake urgency. When a visitor returns and sees the same offer again, trust drops, especially for expensive purchases where people visit several times. [L0409.026]
- Product pages must show exact shipping time, warranty and full product details, and should not use fake urgency such as a countdown timer that constantly resets. Clear information supports the sale without misleading the buyer. (2025.) [L0807.045]

### Cart timers

- Kaching Cart > Cart settings > Timer sets a countdown in the cart, set to 3 minutes in this setup. Turn on "Reset timer when products are added to cart" so the window restarts with each new item. The timer is meant to create urgency at checkout. [L0560.014]
- Timer message in the cart (app placeholder kept as shown): [L0560.015]

```
Your cart will expire in ((timer))
```

- Show a countdown in the cart that tells the shopper how long their order is held. This adds scarcity and pushes the buyer to act now. [L1207.041]
- Pattern: [item or cart] is held for [number] minutes while you check out. A visible time limit creates urgency without changing the price. [L1207.042]
  - Examples: `Your cart is reserved for 5 minutes while you check out` and `Your item is held for 15 minutes at checkout`.
- Add a visible cart timer that tells the shopper their order is held for 10 minutes during checkout. The countdown creates urgency to finish the purchase now. Show this only if the stock or price is actually reserved for that time. [L1207.047]
- Add a countdown timer in the cart that reserves the order for 10 minutes, to create scarcity and encourage the buyer to act now. Use this timer as an additional trust-and-urgency element on the checkout page. [L1209.029]
- Cart timer: display a countdown in the cart stating that the order is held for a set time, such as 10 minutes, to create scarcity and urgency that encourages the buyer to complete the purchase now. [L1210.020]
- The Cart Urgency countdown timer block has settings for the number of minutes the timer runs, the message shown when it expires, the background colour, the timer colour and the headline colour. Its position on the cart page can be changed by moving the block. (Source A, undated.) [L1450.011]
- The Cart Urgency countdown timer shows the remaining time in minutes and seconds, for example "cart will expire in nine minutes, 55 seconds". When the countdown reaches zero, the block shows the expired message set in its settings. [L1450.019]
- A countdown timer that runs out in a few minutes creates urgency on the cart page and is expected to raise conversion rates. Test it with your own traffic data before relying on it, since the benefit is not guaranteed. [L1450.012]
- In a demo store or a store without traffic, the countdown timer adds no value and should be hidden or deleted. Enable it once live traffic arrives so its effect on conversion can be measured. [L1450.013]
- Use this timer only if the stock or price is actually reserved for that time. Urgency works best when the offer really disappears after the stated window. [L1207.043]

#### Timer durations by source

| Duration | Context | Source |
|---|---|---|
| 3 minutes | Kaching cart timer, reset on each new item | [L0560.014] |
| 5 minutes | Tested by one seller alongside 10 minutes; example cart reservation | [L1381.039, L1207.042] |
| 10 minutes | Cart reservation during checkout; product page validity window; seller's preferred setting | [L1207.047, L1209.029, L1210.020, L0683.006, L1381.039] |
| 15 minutes | Example item hold at checkout | [L1207.042] |
| Set by you | Cart Urgency block, configurable minutes | [L1450.011] |

- Choose the duration from your own data. The sources disagree: the 3-minute setup and the 10-minute recommendations are both reported, and the 10-minute setting was the one that worked for one seller after a 5-minute test. [L0560.014, L1381.039, L1450.012]

### Stock messages

- A real stock count on the product page, such as 37 left in stock, is a legitimate scarcity tactic that increases conversions. Only show a number that matches actual inventory. [L0355.017]
- Add a short stock hint in brackets next to the size selection, such as `only 3 left` or `only 2 left`, to create urgency on the product page. Use only stock levels that are true. [L1219.006]
- Show urgency on product pages only for best-selling products that are extremely low in stock, not site-wide. This has not caused problems with Google. (2025.) [L1287.021]
- Shopify lets you hide stock levels with a single toggle, so customers do not see low-stock alerts. Keep the hidden element rather than deleting it, so it can be restored later. (2025.) [L0472.044]
- Competitor stores show low-stock messages such as `Only 9 units left` or `Low stock selling fast`, and some show how many people are viewing the product now. Only display stock or viewer numbers if they reflect the real situation, because false scarcity can trigger refund or platform problems. [L0600.026]
- Urgency labels such as a countdown discount or a low-stock message only work when they are true. Use them only when the discount deadline is real and the stock is actually limited. [L1479.024]
- Use scarcity messages such as low-stock notices to create urgency, but only when the stock really is low. Combine urgency with real limited-time offers to give the buyer a reason to decide now. [L1209.031]
- Urgency and scarcity tactics include time-limited offers, such as a `today only` message shown after purchase, and low-stock notices. They aim to raise the buyer's excitement and motivate immediate action. [L1210.021]
- Use scarcity tactics such as low-stock messages and urgency copy with a clear end, such as today only. [L1207.043]

### Limited quantity and application-based scarcity

These units come from direct-response sales letters. They apply to high-priced or limited offers only.

- Scarcity can be used to intimidate, through limited-quantity wording, a bandwagon message that most people will buy, a message that only qualified people will be accepted, a qualification process before buying, or a select-group message. Scarcity works best when the offer is honest, because a limited number or application process has to be real. [L1370.084]
- Limit the offer to a quantity that is actually limited. State that a response arriving after the supply is gone will be returned, uncashed if needed. Use this only when the stock or spot is truly limited. [L1370.085]
- For high-priced products, use an application or qualification step, such as a questionnaire, reference letters, a required listening period or an interview, because it makes the buyer feel selected. Only state qualification rules you actually enforce. [L1370.086]
- Most of a sales letter's losses come from the `later` group: readers who want the offer but set the letter aside. Most real response arrives in the first days after mailing, so the letter must create urgency to act now instead of tomorrow. [L1370.092]
- Use limited availability to create urgency only if the product, premium or discount is truly limited, and say so specifically, for example that the race is on for a limited number of items. [L1370.096]

### Rules against fake urgency

- Fake urgency, such as a stock count or a deadline that resets every day or week, is dishonest and erodes trust. Customers are a business's most important asset, so urgency built on lies damages it. (Older: 2021-22.) [L0396.012]
- Do not use scarcity messages such as `Limited stock on hand` unless they are true and change over time. A stock message that never changes signals fake scarcity. (Older: 2021-22.) [L0409.030]
- Use urgency only when it is true. Real limited stock, such as a product that is really down to its last 10 units, can be stated. A blanket claim that every product is low on stock is not believable. (Older: 2021-22.) [L0473.027]
- Fake urgency, such as countdown timers that reset, fake stock levels or small token discounts, is not a recommended way to build urgency. Visitors notice it, and it damages trust once discovered. (Older: 2021-22.) [L0473.028]
- Fake urgency tactics, such as false countdown timers, claimed low stock, or offers that never actually expire, usually give small, temporary conversion gains. They are often misleading, so they should not replace a stronger offer. (Older: 2021-22.) [L0414.049]
- A customer's consideration period cannot be shortened by pressure. People who truly want the product buy it regardless of a small discount, so do not rely on urgency tricks to close the sale. (Older: 2021-22.) [L0409.029]
- A strikethrough reference price combined with a large `You save` percentage, shown on a sale that never ends, reads as a fake discount and lowers trust. Show a reference price only when it is a real list or previous price, and make the saving amount match the numbers shown. (Older: 2021-22.) [L0409.054]
- Case: a Shopify reseller of high-ticket bathroom products ran a `48 hour sale` banner for at least three months. The countdown date changed by one day each day while the offer stayed at 5% off with code FLASH5, and the page still said `Limited Stock On Hand`. A $2,999.99 product was shown next to a $4,838.69 reference price, with `You Save: $1,838.70 (38%)`. The store also had `Some exclusions apply` on its tax statements, `No Shipping to California Addresses` with no explanation, a financing line with no link, reviews hidden at the bottom, an empty review widget and non-working tabs. Its USP sat in small header text that did not show on mobile. The verdict: this combination makes the store look like a commodity competing on price and reduces trust. (Older: 2021-22.) [L0409.025]

### Pop-ups and overlays

- Avoid pop-ups to present offers, since they distract customers and interrupt the buying decision. [L0549.029]
- Use the notification bar and the exit-intent overlay to make every offer known across the site. [L0757.031]
- If the margin does not allow a discount in the announcement bar or exit-intent popup, use free shipping for those locations. (2023.) [L0855.117]
- Source conflict: one source advises avoiding pop-ups entirely, while two others use exit-intent overlays for offers. Avoid general pop-ups that interrupt the buying decision. Use an exit-intent overlay only as a last-chance offer, and only where the margin supports it. [L0549.029, L0757.031, L0855.117]

## Checkout, payments and shipping friction

### Checkout steps and customization

- Keep checkout to one or two steps, because every extra click before payment lowers conversion. Where possible, use an express payment method such as Apple Pay, which allows a one-click purchase because the delivery address is already linked to it. [L0387.001]
- On Shopify you cannot match express wallets such as Apple Pay or Amazon Pay, because those services already hold the buyer's payment details. The realistic lever is reducing the number of steps and the friction before checkout. [L0387.002]
- Shopify offers two ways to change the checkout:
  - A third-party checkout app that fully replaces the Shopify checkout.
  - A theme or app that adds features on top of it.
  
  Some themes already include a faster checkout option. [L0387.003]
- Fast Checkout and Instant Checkout apps bypass the add-to-cart page and send users directly to checkout, which reduces friction and can improve conversion rate. [L0209.013]
- *Older advice (2018):* Do not pay for a one-page checkout tool such as CartHook. Add every payment option available to your customers instead, because such a tool is not necessary for these store models. [L0466.047]
- To change the checkout, open Settings > Checkout and click Customize checkout. Newer Shopify stores may show only a one-page checkout option by default. [L1326.023]
- Avoid distracting or overwhelming the buyer with extra requests before the main goal is reached. Simplify the checkout as much as possible so the customer can complete the purchase. [L1208.018]
- In the checkout customer information section, only last name and company name are set as required fields. Marketing options such as SMS stay disabled by default in all regions. [L1306.015]
- Under Customer accounts in Checkout, customer accounts are turned on and Show sign in links is turned on. No other changes are made to that section. [L1306.016]

### Express wallets and button order

**Apple Pay and Google Pay**

- Apple Pay is a top priority for conversion, because customers tap once and skip manual address and payment entry. Enable it before anything else in the checkout setup. [L0352.013]
- Enable Apple Pay and Google Pay alongside Shop Pay Installments in Shopify Payments to improve checkout speed and conversions. [L0153.005]
- *Older advice (2018):* Enable Apple Pay and Google Pay on Shopify when you accept Shopify Payments. A shopper on an iPhone can then complete a purchase in about 30 seconds. [L0466.046]
- Set up Apple Pay in the Shopify store for any new store. If it does not work, contact Shopify support, since a domain-related fault was fixed within half a day. [L0820.038]
- Keep Apple Pay, Google Pay, and Visa and Mastercard credit and debit cards as the main payment options, since most customers pay with these. Apple Pay gives one-click checkout, which speeds up the transaction. [L1381.046]
- Keep Apple Pay as the visible option. One seller reported that disabling Shop Pay helped, because it can hide Apple Pay at checkout, and most of that seller's US customers had Apple Pay. [L1381.044]
- Reduce checkout effort by offering fast payment options such as Apple Pay or PayPal. At the time of recording, Shopify was working on a one-page checkout to replace a multi-click checkout. [L0502.007]

**Button order and visibility**

- Check the payment buttons shown on the product page under Shopify > Settings > Payments. Make sure Apple Pay or a Shop Now button is shown before the Buy with Shop Pay option. [L0530.022]
- Buy with Shop Pay as the first and main checkout button confuses most shoppers who do not have Shop Pay. They hesitate and click add to cart instead. The reviewer advised replacing it with Apple Pay or Shop Now as the first option. [L0530.023]
- If a store's payment methods are limited, contact Shopify support as a worst-case step to get help configuring the checkout buttons. [L0530.024]
- Shop Pay is toggled on by default in the checkout area. Turn it off in markets where Shopify Payments and Shop Pay are not available. [L1231.049]
- Accelerated checkout buttons include PayPal, Amazon Pay, Google Pay and Apple Pay, depending on what is integrated in your Shopify admin. The layout can be set to a number of columns so the buttons take up little space, for example three buttons in one row. [L1231.050]
- *Older advice (Source B, 2021 to 2022):* Turn on "Show dynamic checkout button" on product pages. It displays the payment method a customer usually uses, based on device, shopping history and the options enabled in the store, such as PayPal, Apple Pay or Google Pay. The customer can pay directly without going through the full cart and checkout. The dynamic button only appears for methods that are enabled, so offer these payment options in the store. [L0370.005]
- Express checkout buttons such as PayPal or Venmo depend on the visitor's geolocation, so you may see a button that other visitors do not. A payment option customers do not recognize may be skipped, so make sure the common options are clear. [L0363.035]
- Shop Pay is Shopify's accelerated checkout, provided through Shopify Payments. You can offer it as an extra payment option next to cards and PayPal. It gives buyers a faster way to pay that can support conversion. [L1512.048]
- Use a direct Shop Now button as the main purchase path instead of a PayPal cart button. The hosts said they no longer used PayPal much and found it a headache to manage. [L0657.040]

**PayPal Express buttons**

- PayPal Express checkout buttons on the product or cart page can break e-commerce tracking. Buyers leave the site to pay on PayPal and do not always return, so orders go untracked. These buttons also bypass your own checkout, your email system and abandoned-cart sequences. [L0575.026]
- Remove these buttons and send all buyers through your checkout, offering the standard PayPal option there. [L0575.026]

**Payment icons and visibility**

- Show accepted payment icons, a guaranteed safe checkout note and an accelerated checkout option such as Shop Pay near the buy button. Competitor stores use these to reduce hesitation at checkout. [L0600.032]
- Show payment icons close to Add to Cart so buyers see trusted payment methods before they commit. Include Apple Pay, Google Pay, Shop Pay, PayPal and major cards in the icon row when they are enabled in the store. [L1188.038]
- Show the accepted payment options early, for example in the cart, not only after the shopper fills in the first checkout step. Hiding payment information likely increases abandoned carts. [L0894.037]

### Payment method coverage

- *Older advice (2019):* Offer several payment methods, including Shopify Payments, Amazon Pay, Apple Pay, Google Pay, PayPal and PayPal Credit. Amazon Pay accepts credit cards, debit cards, and personal and business cards. Missing options give buyers a reason to leave. [L0356.038]
- *Older advice (2020):* Offer as many payment methods as possible, including Apple Pay and Google Pay through Shopify Payments, plus Amazon Pay and PayPal. More choices improve conversion. [L0468.091]
- *Older advice (2018):* The following setup was described for accepting payments:
  - Shopify Payments (cards).
  - PayPal, for buyers who only use PayPal.
  - Amazon Pay, set up in the Shopify back end and paid directly to your bank account.
  - Google Pay and Apple Pay, both enabled in Shopify Payments in the Shopify admin.
  
  [L1085.059]
- Accepted payment methods are a basic conversion fix. Each missing method can lose sales from buyers who do not trust the site or use a different wallet. Check the payment methods before changing the offer or the product page. [L1085.060]
- Payment options such as Apple Pay, Amazon Pay, PayPal and a payment plan can increase conversion, because shoppers have different payment preferences. A missing preferred option can lower conversion, so add the options the store can support. [L0970.097]
- A strong offer can produce sales with only a card processor, so PayPal is not strictly needed to start testing a product. Missing well-known payment methods, however, can lower checkout completion and limit scaling. [L1512.007]
- Run at least two payment methods: one card processor, such as Shopify Payments or Stripe, plus PayPal. This helps avoid conversion drops caused by a missing option. PayPal can accept guest checkout without an account, but the store landing page should explain how to do it. [L1512.047]
- Address payment concerns by clearly showing payment security and the payment options available at checkout. Buy-now-pay-later providers such as Klarna, Zip or Afterpay let price-sensitive buyers spread costs, so mention them when cost is the likely blocker. [L1208.011]

### Financing and buy-now-pay-later

**Why financing helps**

- Removing payment friction through financing options increases conversion by making purchases accessible to cash-constrained buyers who would otherwise abandon. [L0040.009]
- Shop Pay Installments significantly increases conversion rates on high-ticket products by reducing the upfront cost and perceived financial risk customers face, which results in higher average order completion rates. [L0153.007]
- Instalment payment options, such as four interest-free payments, can overcome price objections by making the purchase feel affordable in smaller payments. [L0919.029]
- Offering pay-over-time financing at checkout can raise conversion, because shoppers who would hesitate at a high price are more willing to buy when they see a small first payment. It is especially useful for products priced from several hundred dollars upward. The effect is strongest when the option is visible on product pages, not only at checkout. [L1295.001]
- Financing can raise average order value, because shoppers may choose a more expensive upgrade when they can spread the cost over time. This is most relevant for high-ticket stores where customers would otherwise trade down to a cheaper option. [L1295.002]
- Offering installments lowers the amount a buyer must pay upfront, which removes a purchase barrier and raises conversion rates. The effect matters most for high-ticket products, where one large upfront payment is most likely to stop a sale. [L1296.003]
- *Older advice (2020):* Offer financing such as Klarna, Affirm or PayPal Credit, as long as the store does not finance the purchase itself. Financing helps with high-ticket items. [L0468.092]
- *Older advice (2020):* Financing options can help conversion for high-ticket products, especially when customers can pay in four installments. Klarna and Affirm are the main options cited for orders from $500 to $5,000. [L1111.084]
- *Older advice (2020):* Klarna and Affirm charge no fee to apply or to be approved, so applying to both is free. Application approvals were reported as slow in 2020, so applying early is recommended. [L1111.185]
- Shop Pay Installments is recommended as a payment plan option when using Shopify Payments, and its limits have grown over time. Affirm or Klarna can be used if Shopify Payments is not in use. [L0970.098]
- Shop Pay Installments also appears as a selectable payment method at checkout, next to cards, Apple Pay and Google Pay. Shoppers who choose it complete the purchase through the same flow, so no extra checkout step is needed for the merchant. [L1296.011]

**Threshold: sources give different values**

Sources disagree on the price point where financing starts to pay off. Use the value that matches your tool and your niche.

| Source | Threshold or rule | When it applies |
|---|---|---|
| Older advice (2019): Affirm, Klarna or PayPal Credit | Show only on high-priced products, typically from about $1,000 upward. The threshold is set by niche and average order value. [L1069.056] | Third-party financing in a high-ticket niche |
| Pay-over-time generally | Especially useful for products priced from several hundred dollars upward. [L1295.001] | General guidance for pay-over-time at checkout |
| Older advice (2020): Klarna and Affirm | Main options cited for orders from $500 to $5,000. [L1111.084] | Orders in that range, paid in four installments |
| Source C: buy-now-pay-later options | For high-ticket products above about $100 to $150 USD. One store with an average order value around $120 reported a clear benefit. [L1512.013] | Products above that band |
| Shop Pay Installments | Lower-priced items show two interest-free payments. Higher-priced items typically show four. [L1296.004] | After activation, on product pages and at checkout |

**Installment messages on low-priced items: a conflict**

- *Older advice (2019):* An installment message under a low-priced product distracts from the purchase and does more harm than good. For example, four payments of about $20 under a $70 item. [L1069.057]
- Shop Pay Installments shows its own message on product pages, with two payments for lower-priced items and four for higher-priced items. [L1296.004]
- Where both apply, check the live product pages to see what customers actually see, and compare with the thresholds in the table above. [L1296.010]

**Product page messaging**

- *Older advice (2019):* Customers who choose financing on high-priced products can be offered a financing option that is shown on the product page with the installment amount, using the financing provider's built-in Shopify integration. Set the messaging so that it asks whether the customer wants financing and only appears on expensive products. [L1069.059]

```
Customers who choose financing on high-priced products can be offered a financing option that is shown on the product page with the installment amount, using the financing provider's built-in Shopify integration. Set the messaging so that it asks whether the customer wants financing and only appears on expensive products.
```

- After activation, product pages display a small installment message. Lower-priced items show two interest-free payments, while higher-priced items typically show four interest-free payments of the price divided by four. [L1296.004]
- Formula for the product-page installment message:

```
pay in [N] interest-free payments of [price divided by N] with Shop Pay
```

  N is 2 for low-priced items and 4 for higher-priced items. The shopper sees a small per-payment amount instead of the full price, which makes the purchase feel affordable.
  - Example for a $30 product: "pay in two interest-free payments of $15.00 with Shop Pay".
  - Example for a $200 product: "pay in four interest-free payments of $50.00 with Shop Pay".
  
  [L1296.005]
- Installment messaging on product pages is part of the offer itself. After enabling it, check the live product pages to verify that customers actually see the options before considering the task complete. [L1296.010]

### Local payment methods

- Local payment methods can be enabled on Stripe and Shopify Payments:
  - For Stripe, check the payment methods in your Stripe account and turn on the needed option, or ask Stripe chat support to enable it.
  - For Shopify Payments, ask Shopify support to turn the method on for you.
  
  [L1512.014]
- Locally preferred checkout methods named for European markets (verify before use, as preferences change):

| Country | Methods named |
|---|---|
| Germany | Klarna, PayPal |
| Switzerland | Twint |
| Spain | Google Pay, Apple Pay, credit card |
| Several European countries | Bancontact (direct bank transfer) |

  [L1512.011]
- Running ads in a European market with only cards limits both conversion and how far ads can scale. Local methods such as Klarna in Germany or PayPal are important in those markets. [L1512.012]
- Adding a local payment method increased sales noticeably. Customers in the local market trust payment providers that they already know. [L0492.036]
- Show the logo of a local payment method, such as Twint in Switzerland, on the store to build trust. Twint is limited to Swiss companies, and the customer scans a QR code to pay. [L1439.040]

### Currency and price display

- Set the store's main currency to the currency of the target market. Buyers who first see USD and then a local currency at checkout lose trust and leave, even if a currency converter is available. [L1185.042]
- If most visitors come from a country other than the one shown by default, show prices in that visitor's local currency via geo-targeting. At minimum, display prices in USD when the main ad traffic is from the United States. Check the traffic origin in Google Analytics to decide which currency should be the default. [L0838.002]
- Which rule applies: use the single target-market currency [L1185.042] when the store is built for one market. Use geo-targeting [L0838.002] when traffic comes mainly from a different country, and USD when the main ad traffic is from the US.
- Converting prices from a supplier's currency with an app's automatic conversion produces odd, uneven price points such as 42.95, 41.36 and 46.13. These make the store look less trustworthy, so align them to clean USD price points. [L0838.003]
- Plain sale prices do not show the original price at checkout. Sale prices show the original price in the cart or checkout only when a coupon code is applied, unless custom code or an app adds it. [L0806.053]

### Shipping costs and fees

**Show costs early**

- Show all costs, including shipping, fees and taxes, early in the buying process rather than revealing them at the final checkout step. Unexpected extra charges at the last step lead to abandoned carts. [L0390.055]
- Show shipping costs and the final price before the cart, for example on the product page. When shoppers add to cart but do not buy, a frequent cause is an unexpected total, often shipping. Visitors should not need the cart to discover the total. [L0640.054]
- Shipping costs and fees should be shown before checkout to avoid shock. Customers who return a product because of an unexpected extra payment are a sign of hidden fees. [L0950.034]
- The most common reason for checkout abandonment is a shipping fee that the buyer did not see before reaching the checkout page. Show shipping costs before checkout to prevent this. [L0473.025]
- A shipping fee of around $5 at checkout can stop buyers who expected free shipping. Offering free shipping or showing shipping costs earlier can prevent drop-off at checkout. [L0361.032]
- *Older advice (Source B, 2021 to 2022):* For buyers who are anxious, state the shipping method and all shipping costs clearly before checkout. Do not use fine print or asterisks whose meaning is hidden, and do not leave any charge undisclosed, because hidden costs create doubt. [L0473.024]
- Before sending paid traffic, check at checkout that the shipping cost matches what the store advertises and that any "free shipping" claim holds for the customer's cart. A mismatch at checkout reduces trust and conversion. [L0657.052]

**Free shipping and offer elements**

- Show free shipping together with an estimated delivery date on the product page. A competitor that displays free shipping without a delivery estimate is at a disadvantage. [L0894.025]
- Free shipping, including free worldwide shipping, is a common offer element that removes a purchase barrier. A free-shipping message placed near the price lowers hesitation. [L0919.030]
- Shipping language matters. Compare "free tracked shipping" with "standard shipping" when you write the offer. [L0067.030]

**Shipping protection**

- Offer shipping protection at checkout only if it is real insurance and not an upsell. Check the wording before you enable it. [L0667.021]

**Answering shipping cost questions directly**

- Formula: answer the buyer's direct question, for example about the shipping cost, right away. Then add a true scarcity note, such as low remaining stock. The direct answer removes friction, and the honest scarcity note pushes the buyer to act. Reported results were around 20 to 30 percent of such conversations ending in a sale. [L1437.018]
  - Example 1: "Standard delivery is $7.95 for this item, and there are only four left at this price, so order today."
  - Example 2: "Shipping to your address is $5 with 5-7 business days, and just two units remain in stock."
  
  [L1437.018]

### Delivery estimates and backorders

**Estimated delivery dates**

- Estimated delivery dates reduce purchase hesitation by communicating clear fulfillment expectations on the product page. They also decrease inbound support requests about shipping timelines. [L0139.006]
- Showing an estimated delivery date range on the product page removes doubt about when the product will arrive. This raises conversion and reduces customer messages asking about delivery timing. [L1192.003]
- The estimated arrival range on a product page is counted from the day the visitor views it. A shopper who orders today sees the earliest and longest delivery days counted from today. A shopper who visits on a later day sees the same day counts shifted forward. [L1192.031]
- Show the delivery estimate on the product page and in the cart as a date window, such as X to Y. Shoppers buying for a fixed date, such as a gift or an event, feel more confident when the window fits their deadline. [L0955.024]
- Show estimated arrival dates on the product page so the buyer knows when the product will arrive. Unknown delivery times add doubt for visitors who do not yet trust the store. [L1346.017]
- Show the estimated time of arrival (ETA) for each product on the product page, before checkout. Shoppers want to know when the item will arrive before they buy, and few stores show this. [L1130.022]
- Estimated delivery answers the common question "when will I receive it" before the visitor asks, which removes an objection to buying. It should be visible on a live store, but it can be hidden on a demo store where nothing is actually shipped. [L0976.024]
- Shipping time shown on the store can deter buyers. Show a long delivery time, for example two to three weeks, on the product page and in the email instead of hiding it. This sets the expectation before purchase, so customers are not surprised later. [L0650.031]
- Being transparent about shipping time and origin helped close a sale when the store did not state it clearly. In the example, the buyer was told about 10 to 20 days from China when they asked. [L0650.033]

**Lead-time confirmation**

- Setting correct expectations before purchase reduces refunds, complaints and angry emails after the order. A clear lead-time message on the product page is a cheap way to prevent many service problems. [L1472.043]
- Template for a lead-time confirmation before checkout:

```
We are working hard to get your {product_name} to you, but this item currently takes about {lead_time} to arrive. Please confirm you are happy with this timeline before you continue to checkout.

[Yes, continue to checkout]   [No, keep browsing]
```

  [L1472.042]

**Backorders**

- *Older advice (2020):* Do not close your store when supplier inventory is uncertain. Instead, notify customers clearly about delays before they purchase, so they make informed decisions and are not surprised after payment. [L0245.042]
- Show a backorder notice in a highlighted block above the Add to Cart button on every backordered product page, including the expected ship date. Use this exact wording style so customers see the date before they buy. [L0359.058]
- Template for the highlighted block above the Add to Cart button on backordered product pages:

```
This product is currently backordered and is on its way to our warehouse. Expected shipping date: [EXACT SHIP DATE]. [OPTIONAL: This delay is due to REASON.] All orders will ship first come, first served, starting [SHIP START DATE].
```

  [L0359.059]
- Replace the placeholders in square brackets before use. [EXACT SHIP DATE] is the exact date from the supplier, not a vague month. [SHIP START DATE] is the first date shipments begin. The reason line is optional. [L0359.059]

**Payment and inventory check**

1. Authorize the payment at checkout without capturing funds.
2. Check that the product is in stock.
3. If it is in stock, capture the payment and proceed with fulfillment.

[L1094.070]

### Checkout trust and branding

**Branding the checkout**

- An unbranded checkout that looks different from the rest of the store can make customers abandon at the last step, even after marketing has built trust. Apply your branding to every step of the purchase process, including checkout. [L0390.047]
- The checkout is a different-looking page that may feel untrusted. A branded checkout banner with secure checkout, privacy protection and satisfaction guarantee badges can raise conversion. In one case, conversion rose from 0.29 percent to 2.15 percent after these changes. [L1259.048]
- The checkout page should look trustworthy. Add a checkout banner with secure checkout and satisfaction guarantee messaging, payment processor logos such as PayPal, Visa and Mastercard, and optionally a 30-day money-back guarantee, because the checkout is otherwise unbranded. [L1260.040]
- Add the store logo and store information to the checkout. Optionally add a background image in the theme settings under Checkout. This keeps the checkout looking like part of the store, so customers do not feel they have moved to a different site after adding items to the cart. [L1326.024]
- Showing the business logo on the checkout page reassures customers that they are still on the store. Shopify controls the checkout for security, so it can be branded with a logo and colors but not fully redesigned. [L1371.052]

**Trust signals**

- Shoppers feel more uncertain at checkout because they must enter email, address and payment details. Add trust icons such as privacy protected, secure checkout and satisfaction guaranteed. Also show a contact number so customers feel supported. [L1377.041]
- Add a trust badge or logo at checkout to increase credibility. [L0067.030]
- Show customer review counts or ratings directly on the checkout page as well as on product pages. Buyers who are close to paying benefit from seeing that other customers have completed their purchase successfully. [L1208.023]
- Payment technical problems and worries about payment security can make buyers leave checkout quickly. Make security signals and the accepted payment methods clearly visible during checkout, and add payment plan options for buyers who may hesitate because of cost. [L1208.024]
- A PayPal study found that adding a padlock (lock) icon to the checkout button raised mobile conversion by 17 percent. Small, thoughtful design details at checkout can have a measurable effect. [L0390.030, L0423.022]

**Case**

- A store started with a checkout conversion rate of 0.9 percent, while TikTok traffic showed a high add-to-cart rate. The owner added trust badges and icons to the checkout page, and the conversion rate rose to 2.05 percent within one day. Only two or three changes were made, all on that page. [L1377.043]

### Checkout completion checklist

Steps to improve checkout completion:

1. Ensure the shipping language is attractive, for example "free tracked shipping" rather than "standard shipping". [L0067.030]
2. Add a trust badge or logo at checkout to increase credibility. [L0067.030]
3. Consider a limited-time free gift offer or an extra product at checkout to incentivize the purchase. [L0067.030]
4. Make sure the cart display is not minimized on mobile. Shopify now minimizes cart contents on mobile checkout. [L0067.030]

### Testing checkout

**Desktop and mobile**

- Test the add-to-cart and checkout flow on both desktop and mobile. Look for issues such as an unexpected extra fee, a cut-off logo, or a button that does not work. [L0469.067]
- Test the complete purchase path after every change, including add-to-cart, subscription selection, upsells and the cart. A cart that stays empty after items were added will stop almost all sales. [L0524.022]
- Verify the checkout on the live store. [L1296.010]

**Test purchase with no conversions**

1. Add a cheap product priced around 1.50 USD.
2. Buy it on a desktop device.
3. Buy it on a mobile phone.
4. If either purchase fails, fix the checkout before running more traffic.

[L0963.056]

**Usability test with a tester**

1. Set up the store and add a specific product to the cart.
2. Find the add-to-cart button and confirm it is easy to locate.
3. Start and complete the checkout process.
4. Watch the tester without giving any guidance.
5. Note every place they hesitate or get stuck.
6. Ask them why they got stuck.
7. Optimize each problem point and repeat the test.

[L1098.017]

#### Further details

- A fast-delivery promise reassures the buyer that the order will arrive quickly, which makes the purchase feel safer. [L0919.031]

## Cart optimization and upsells

### Cart drawer and cart page design

- Replace the Buy Now button with an Add to Cart button and use a cart drawer. Shoppers stay on the product page, can close the drawer, and keep shopping without losing their place. [L0339.004]
- Open a slide-out cart on add to cart instead of sending the customer to a separate cart page. This removes one step between product page and checkout and keeps the customer on the page while they read. [L1225.036]
- Build the cart and checkout for simplicity (older, Source C):
  1. Use a cart drawer (AJAX cart) that opens on add to cart.
  2. Put a one-click button in the cart that leads directly to checkout.
  3. Do not show extra steps or questions before checkout.
  4. Place the terms and conditions on the checkout page itself, using the Shopify option for this, instead of as a required step earlier in the flow. [L0387.006]
- Use this element order in a slide cart:
  1. TrustPilot rating and payment badges placed under the cart.
  2. Urgency message: "Your order reserved for 5-10 minutes."
  3. Free shipping bar.
  4. Progress bar.
  5. Optional cross-sell, added only with care. [L1299.017]
- Slide-cart apps: UpCart is the default. AMP Slide Cart is another solid option, and some themes such as Shrine include their own slide cart. [L1299.016]
- Choose a single or double column layout based on target market and product mix, not on what is trending. Double column carts were popular for a time but are used less often now. [L1231.012]
- Components can be moved by drag and drop anywhere in the cart, so placements can be A/B tested later. [L1231.022]
- A login button in the cart can drive sign-ups and segmentation, for example by linking to a loyalty program form on the customer account page. Customer rules can use login status and customer account tags to target promotions. [L1231.019]
- The checkout area can show a view cart button linking to a full-page cart, which is useful for products with delivery date rules. A continue shopping button closes the cart modal, and its label can be edited to match the brand voice. [L1231.048]
- On desktop, shoppers may not know that the X or a click outside the cart closes it. A visible continue shopping button helps; change its label to match brand voice and target market. [L1231.064]
- A discount code input in the cart lets customers enter a code and see its effect on the subtotal before checkout. A code entered in the cart is already applied at checkout, so it does not need to be re-entered. [L1231.053]
- Example of a checkout cart: a free shipping progress message, a free product unlock at $40.06 added as a line item, a subscription upgrade offer saving 20%, an SMS sign-up offer of 10% off, and an order notes field. The subtotal of $59.94 was shown with two interest-free installments of $29.97 through Shop Pay, Klarna or Afterpay. [L1231.063]
- A cart can show that an order includes a free gift. This signals value before checkout and makes the buyer feel they get a lot for the price. [L0919.025]
- Kaching Cart warning message: set it under Kaching Cart > Cart settings > Top bar. Write it as a stock-scarcity statement that includes a sold-out count and a call to order now. [L0560.013]
- Trust badges in Kaching Cart: under Kaching Cart > Cart settings > Trust badges, upload a custom badge image in PNG or JPEG format showing the rating and the Trustpilot logo, and name it "Trustpilot". Set it to show under the checkout button, and adjust the Logo size, Icon size and Text size fields. The lesson's example badge width is 150 px. [L0560.022]
- Name custom trust badges clearly so they are easy to find in the badge list, for example "Trustpilot" or "Choice by Trustpilot", matching the uploaded image. [L0560.039]

### Cart-page recommendations and reminders

- A "Your cart is ready" reminder box on the homepage, showing cart items, total price and a "Continue to checkout" button, generates a +9.2% conversion rate lift. [L0197.011]
- The homepage reminder delivers stronger lift when visitors have multiple items in the cart rather than a single item, and the biggest improvement comes from returning customers. [L0197.013]
- Product recommendations on cart pages with zoom and add-to-cart buttons, which keep customers on the cart page, generate a +4.5% conversion rate lift. [L0197.017]
- Customers who click cart recommendations to open a full product page interrupt their checkout flow and add friction. Keeping zoom and add-to-cart on the cart page maintains purchase momentum. [L0197.019]
- Recommendations driven by customer behavior and purchase history outperform manual picks based on bestsellers or store assumptions, because they match individual interests rather than store preferences. [L0197.002]
- Example: Oodie replaced its 2-item PDP recommendation slider with a 2x3 grid using Visually's behavior-based engine (browsing history plus purchase history), which increased CVR and revenue per session. It then replaced SearchSpring with Visually recommendations across all product detail page and cart regions. [L0197.004]
- Cross-sell widgets can sit at different points in the cart, such as the top to draw attention, or just above the checkout button so shoppers do not forget free samples. This is the main way to build a selectable gift with purchase. [L1231.038]
- A new Smart Cart automatically creates and installs a cross-sell widget, so the cart has recommendations from the start. Duplicating a cart keeps the same widget ID, and the data source or layout is edited in the widget settings, not the cart settings. [L1231.044]
- Several cross-sell widgets can be added to one cart. Use separate widgets to show different recommendations to different customer groups, or to run customer-based discounts that need their own widget ID. [L1231.045]
- Cart-page offers convert well because the customer is already in buying mode, so upsells placed there are worth testing. [L1410.013]
- The cart page is a strong place for pre-checkout upsells because buyers there are close to paying and in a buying mood. The product page suits bundle and buy-one-get-one offers. [L1412.006]
- Tiered cart goals with a progress bar in the cart drawer: at about $15 cart value free shipping unlocks, at $30 a free gift, and at $60 to $70 a 20% discount. [L1410.015]
- Free-shipping upsell: show the amount still needed to unlock free shipping, for example "add $20 to unlock free shipping" when the cart is under $40, or "add $3.99 for free shipping". This makes customers add an item. [L1410.016]
- Checkout message: show the amount needed to qualify for free shipping (for example "Add $11 more to qualify for free shipping") with a suggested product. This encourages customers to raise order value to reach the threshold. [L0298.057]

### Pre-selected add-ons and cart cross-sells: conflicting advice

- Pre-selected add-ons on the cart page, such as priority processing, shipping protection or a warranty, are rarely unchecked, reportedly about 80% of the time. Keep add-on prices low, for example $3.90 to $5 or under $5. About 60% of orders may accept an extra $3 to $5 this way. [L1410.014]
- Source D (2026) advises caution: cart cross-sells can lower conversions and cause complaints about products that were added automatically. Test them carefully, and keep cart upsells off until the store is scaling. [L1299.018]
- Both values apply in different situations. Pre-selected, low-priced protection and add-ons suit a store that tests them with a clear benefit and a small price. Unrequested automatic cross-sells belong after the store is scaling and has measured their effect. [L1410.014, L1299.018]
- Source A (2022, older) says upsell recommendations can be placed before the cart rather than at checkout, which reduces clutter at checkout. Test this with an upsell app before removing anything. [L0963.057]
- Source B (2021-22, older) says not to add a pre-checkout upsell to a cart page that already carries several extra elements beyond the standard Shopify cart. Add one only when the cart page is otherwise empty, because upsells must not deter the purchase or overload the page. [L1409.007]
- Too many cart extensions crowd the cart and distract shoppers. If a store already runs two or three cart extensions, weigh carefully before adding Checkout Plus or package protection. [L0955.023]

### Upsell and cross-sell placements

- Upsells can be deployed at three moments: on the product page before checkout, in the shopping cart, or on a post-purchase page before the thank-you confirmation (Source B, 2021-22, older). [L0203.002]
- Upsells and cross-sells can be placed at four points: pre-sell, the product page, the cart page, and a post-purchase funnel after checkout. Each placement can raise average order value and profit margin. [L0434.017, L0435.017]
- Pre-checkout offers appear on the product page or in the cart before payment. Post-purchase offers appear after payment and before the thank-you page. [L1412.003]
- Pre-purchase upsell and cross-sell offers can appear on the collection page, product page, cart drawer, cart page or checkout. Test each placement separately. [L1411.006]
- Post-purchase offers appear after payment and before the thank-you page. The buyer has already entered card and address details, so they can accept with one click. This is why the stage is a must-have funnel. [L1411.007]
- Post-purchase upsells convert better than pre-checkout upsells because the buyer has already decided to buy. They also need no new card details, only one click on a Pay Now button. [L1410.019]
- Post-purchase upsells convert better than pre-purchase offers because the buyer has already committed to the store. Stores with only 1 to 5 orders per day should implement them. [L1412.004]
- Post-purchase upsells are worth setting up even for a store with only one to five orders per day, because a customer who has just paid is more likely to accept an extra offer than one still browsing. [L1410.040]
- A complementary product added in the cart drawer works well because the buyer is already in buying mode. A supplement or closely related item, such as a matching tool set, is more likely to be added than an unrelated one. [L1411.008]
- Complementary items convert better than unrelated ones. A second funnel can add another offer on a product compatible with the item the customer is buying. [L1141.021]
- Offer add-ons before checkout, on the product page, because customers decide early. Pre-checkout add-ons such as a $200 paddle or $100 case increase order value (Source A, 2018, older). [L1109.042]
- Add-ons on the product page: add-on product options, bold text highlighting customizable features, and optional product bundles can be built into Shopify without external apps (Source A, 2020, older). [L0245.078]
- Product page upsell apps with options should be added to product pages, not used to split product variants into separate products when you need one shared product page with different options (Source A, 2024). [L0856.039]
- Show a single clear image on the product page for buy-one-get-one offers and bundles. Cross-selling an unrelated product through a free-item offer lowers conversion unless the product is simple and easy to understand at a glance. [L1410.017]
- Free ebooks or guides with purchases, and $1 upsells on nominally free products, raise perceived value and goodwill without eroding margins through percentage discounts. [L0324.044]
- Source A's sales sequence for upgrades:
  1. Display comparison charts on product pages showing feature and benefit differences between standard and premium versions.
  2. Offer shoppers the option to upgrade to a premium version directly in their cart before checkout.
  3. Present an extended warranty option at checkout.
  4. Offer priority or expedited shipping as an upgrade.
  5. Follow up with a phone call after purchase to recommend complementary services such as installation, design consultation, or membership programs. [L0240.014]
- Source A's cross-sell pop-up setup (2025):
  1. In Shopify go to Online Store > Themes > Customize > Theme settings and scroll to the cross sell pop-up settings.
  2. Turn the cross sell option on and enter a heading.
  3. For the first option, choose a collection that contains at least one product, for example a wetsuit collection.
  4. Save the settings.
  5. Open each product that should trigger the cross sell and add the tag cross-sell-1, then save.
  6. Preview the product, add it to the cart and confirm the pop-up appears. [L0667.036]
- Gift-with-purchase: a cross-sell widget at the right point in the cart is the main way to build a selectable gift with purchase. [L1231.038]

### Multi-channel and customer-group upsells (Source B, 2021-22, older)

- Make each upsell offer through more than one channel at the same time. Customers engage with different formats, and some only act after hearing the same offer several times. [L1409.001]
- Upsells can be presented on-site, by email, and through customer service (phone or chat). Using all three is recommended, but a single on-site position can be skipped if other page elements already fill the site. [L1409.002]
- Among the three channels, email and phone or chat customer service have been reported as the highest-converting, so they are the priority when time or resources are limited. [L1409.003]
- A post-checkout upsell is the most consistently effective on-site position. The sale is already completed and the customer is in a buying mood, and it interrupts the purchase less than other positions. [L1409.005]
- Hyperspenders keep buying from the same business after a first purchase and are the main audience for upsells. For this group, several upsells in a row can work well. [L1409.006]
- On-site upsell setup steps:
  1. Decide which on-site positions to use (product page, cart page, post-purchase) based on what already runs on the site.
  2. Install Frequently Bought Together and place its box under the Add to cart button on the product page.
  3. Attach each core product's related upsell products exactly as listed in the upsell planner sheet.
  4. Install One-Click Upsell and assign the post-purchase upsells to each top product.
  5. If the cart page has room, add pre-checkout upsells in the same app.
  6. Design the upsell page and set the offers and quantities per product, using split tests where useful.
  7. Let the apps run and check which upsells appear and convert over time. [L1409.015]

### Bundles

- Bundle offers with strong discounts, for example two items for $30 when one costs $25, drive customers to buy multiple units even for simple products, which increases AOV significantly. [L0298.034]
- Bundle Bear sends customers directly to checkout without an intermediate bundle-selection page. This reduces friction compared with apps that require a separate step, and the advantage benefits simple two-unit offers. [L0298.055]
- Dynamic bundle widget on a product page: the current product is listed as "This item", and each additional bundle item shows its price and a selection control. A "Total Price" line sits above the Add to Cart button, and the shopper can deselect any item before adding the bundle. A separate "What's Trending" section can sit below the bundle. [L0559.026]
- Savings labels need a visible saving. A saving of only $0.05 in the SAVE label does nothing for conversion, so the compare-at price must be set higher than the bundle price, around $30 to $40 or about 50 percent above the price (for example for a bottle selling near $20). [L0861.013, L0862.011]
- Showing the saved amount next to the price helps customers see the value of buying more units and raises the chance they move from one unit to two. The compare-at price is the reference point for that saving. [L0861.014]
- Savings label pattern: show the amount saved against a higher compare-at price, using the saved-total variable in a label such as "SAVE" followed by the saved amount. It works because customers see the gap between the most expensive reference price and what they pay now.
  - Example 1: a label reading "SAVE $20.00" on a two-pack priced at $29.99 against a $50 compare price.
  - Example 2: a label reading "SAVE $15.00" on a three-pack priced at $44.95 against a $60 compare price. [L0862.010]
- Pricing options in bundle settings: Show prices per item, Use product compare-at price, Show prices without decimals, and Price rounding. Cart option: Skip cart and go to checkout directly. [L0862.012]
- Badge styles per bar: "Simple" for the one-unit bar, "Most Popular" for the two-unit bar, and a "Best Value" badge text for the three-unit bar. The "Show quantity selector" option controls whether the customer can change the quantity on a bar. [L0862.013]
- Kaching Bundles has a "Setup A/B test" option in the bundle editor for testing two versions of a deal. Use Save as draft and check Product preview and Country preview before publishing. [L0862.014]

### Add-ons, replacement offers and expedited shipping

- Add-ons that raise AOV include an expedited shipping upgrade, an extended warranty, or a related physical product. The best option depends on the product and what the niche buyers want. [L0970.100]
- Replacement-item upsell: after the customer clicks Add to Cart, a pop-up offers a larger or better version of the same product, for example a 2 lb bag of coffee instead of a 1 lb bag for $3 more. The buttons are "Replace item", which swaps the cart item, and "No, thanks", which keeps the original. [L1076.040]
