  - Keep total page size and the number of requests low, since GTmetrix reports both alongside the fully loaded time. A page with more than 100 requests and close to 2 MB of total size, as in typical unoptimized Shopify stores, usually has clear app and image problems to fix. [L1266.044]
  - Shopify's Largest Contentful Paint (LCP) and CLS are driven mainly by apps and JavaScript (for LCP) and by theme and apps that push page elements around as they load (for CLS). FCP is harder to control and usually not a problem. [L1266.028]
- **Speed score availability (older, 2021-22):** The Online store speed score is not available while password protection is on. After the password is disabled, it can take 1 to 3 days for the score to be calculated. Installed apps, edited theme code, and the size of images and videos all affect the score. [L0370.025]

### Speed fix sequence

Work from easiest to hardest. Procedure from Source A:

1. **Reduce homepage slideshows.** Six to ten slides that nobody watches waste resources and annoy visitors, so remove them unless you need them. [L0390.035]
2. **Reconsider quick-shop functionality.** It is heavy on resources. Keep it where it helps upselling, such as customers buying several low-price items together, and remove it for high-ticket products where it adds little. [L0390.035]
3. **Choose the right image format.** Use SVG for icons and logos, JPEG for photos such as hero images, and PNG only when a transparent background is needed. [L0390.035]
4. **Crop and scale product images to the size the site needs.** For example, reduce a 5000 pixel wide supplier image to 2048 pixels, which keeps high definition without loading unnecessary data. [L0390.035]
5. **Compress images.** Compression tools can reduce file size by up to 70 to 80% without visible loss, using the manual upload tool TinyPNG or an automatic image app such as RushPix. [L0390.035]
6. **Remove apps you do not need.** Review your app list, since apps are among the heaviest elements after images, and keep only the few that the business truly needs. Replace small functions with simple code where possible, for example a terms-and-conditions checkbox on the cart page. [L0390.035]
7. **Update the theme if it is old or has been patched by multiple developers.** Check the browser console for JavaScript errors, since broken scripts can stop features like cart upsells from working. [L0390.035]
8. **Minify and optimize the code.** This usually takes 2 to 4 hours at most for a skilled developer, so treat longer estimates with caution. [L0390.035]
9. **Back up the theme before any change and test the entire store afterwards.** [L0390.035]

**Source C procedure (no year given):**

1. Run the store through GTmetrix or Google PageSpeed to find the main bottleneck, which is usually images or GIFs. [L1259.023]
2. Compress images with TinyPNG or Photoshop, targeting image files below 300 KB and GIFs below 800 KB, with product images ideally under 50 KB while keeping visual quality nearly unchanged. [L1259.023]
3. Remove unused apps and their code, since each active app loads its own JavaScript on every page view. [L1259.023]
4. Page builders such as PageFly add an extra layer on top of the theme and slow the page somewhat, but can still be worth it for their conversion features. [L1259.023]

**Image thresholds from both procedures (the sources give different kinds of target and are used together):**

| Item | Value | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Compression reduction without visible loss | Up to 70-80% | L0390.035 |
| Example resize | 5000 px to 2048 px wide | L0390.035 |
| Image file target | Below 300 KB | L1259.023 |
| GIF target | Below 800 KB | L1259.023 |
| Product image target | Ideally under 50 KB | L1259.023 |

### Images, media and video

- **Compress selectively (older, 2021-22):** Compress image files selectively during website setup. Focus on larger files and those noticeably affecting page load speed. Compressing every image on the site is unnecessary. This refines the blanket compression step above. [L0300.011]
- **Autoplay and loop (older, 2021-22):** Turn off video autoplay and video looping in the product page media settings when the store has no videos yet, and turn them off again whenever videos are added later. Autoplay and loop can distract visitors and slow the page down. [L0370.006]
- **Homepage video test (older, 2021):** Before adding a video to the homepage, test the page with GTmetrics. A video can noticeably raise the load time, so remove it if the score drops sharply. [L0962.049]
- **Videos on product pages:** Keep videos where they help buyers, but compress images so they do not slow the page. (Older advice, 2021.) [L0801.118]
- **Self-host or embed:** Embed product videos from your own YouTube channel instead of self-hosting them, because self-hosting slows the page more. Embedding even from YouTube lowers the page speed score, but the page still loads fine for visitors. [L1094.068]
- **Social links in a new tab (older, 2022):** Social media links on the store can be set to open in a new tab so visitors do not leave the site. This needs a small code snippet from the store developer. [L1281.049]

### Apps, code and theme minimalism

- **Less is more:** Add features and apps one at a time and test whether revenue or sales increase after each change. Without testing, it is impossible to know which feature or app improves conversion. [L0709.024]
- **Avoid extra apps and custom code (2023):** Avoid adding extra apps or custom code that slow down the store, because page speed affects both conversion and search performance. Remove unused apps and verify that no code is left behind after uninstalling them. [L0835.078]
- **Leftover code (2024):** Mobile traffic that does not convert can point to page speed issues. Remove leftover code from uninstalled apps and check speed for mobile before adding more apps. [L0806.051]
- **Store speed factors (older, 2021-22):** Shopify store speed depends on how many apps are installed, whether the theme's code has been modified, and how large the images and videos are. To keep the speed score healthy, limit the number of apps, keep custom code lean, and make media files small. [L1371.049]
- **Plugins and pop-ups (no year given):** Stores with bugs, errors, unused plugins that slow the site, or several stacked pop-ups for the same offer frustrate visitors and push them away. Remove plugins that do nothing and limit pop-ups to one useful message. [L0390.025]

### Hidden faults and pre-launch checks

- **Case: a faulty app hid behind a healthy-looking store.** A store had substantial traffic, enough revenue to employ a small team, and a conversion rate of 0.24%. The owners did not see the problem because they were too close to their own site. An outside expert checked the store within about five minutes of trying to check out and found a faulty app plus code left three months earlier by an inexperienced developer. After the expert removed the faulty app and corrected the code, conversion rose roughly eightfold within 24 hours. The expert charged only for a review instead of a fee because the fix had taken five minutes. [L0390.018]
- **Buy your own product before launch:** Before launching a store or checkout on a new platform, buy a product through it yourself. Walk through the full customer journey and test each payment method to confirm it works end to end. [L1216.036]

### Mobile responsiveness and device split

- **Share of mobile visits, by traffic source:**

| Traffic source | Mobile share | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Facebook and Instagram ads (heavy traffic) | 70-80% of visits | L0390.043 |
| Landing page traffic | Roughly 80-85% of visitors by smartphone | L1259.015 |
| TikTok | 95-99% of users shop on a phone | L1410.018 |

- **Consequence:** A website that is not responsive will lose most of its potential customers when 70 to 80% of visits come from mobile devices. [L0390.043]
- **Responsiveness signal:** A relative mobile conversion rate well below about 30% usually means the website is not responsive. Fix layout and responsiveness problems before drawing conclusions about mobile traffic quality. [L0390.053]
- **Landing page top section:** Landing page conversion depends heavily on mobile. The top of the page must convey value within seconds, with product images, star ratings and a benefit-driven headline visible without scrolling. [L1259.015]
- **Mobile-first design:** When traffic comes from TikTok, 95-99% of users shop on a phone, so the mobile version of the store should be built first. [L1410.018]
- **Pre-launch phone check:** Before launch, check the landing page on a phone-sized preview (such as the iPhone 12 screen size) and scroll the full page, because stacked content on mobile can become very long. Confirm that:
  - carousels scroll sideways smoothly,
  - the offer and add-to-cart button stay visible near the top,
  - accordion sections open correctly. [L0918.036]
- **Mobile bounce (older, 2021):**
  1. Compare bounce rate and session time on mobile and desktop in Google Analytics. [L1090.068]
  2. If mobile is much worse, speed up the site on mobile connections. [L1090.068]
  3. Optionally set mobile bid adjustments in Google Ads to reduce spend on the weaker device. [L1090.068]
  4. Check which search terms send traffic and add negatives for terms that do not match your pages. [L1090.068]
- **Mobile browsing, desktop buying (older, 2021):** Customers often browse on mobile and buy later on desktop. If desktop buying is high, adjust bids by device rather than moving all traffic to desktop. [L0962.052]
- **Device review every 30 to 60 days:**
  1. Set a recurring reminder for the review. [L0510.052]
  2. Compare the conversion rate for desktop and for mobile in your reports. [L0510.052]
  3. Group products by price or sales cycle into separate campaigns. [L0510.052]
  4. Adjust device visibility at the campaign level, since device bids are not set per product. [L0510.052]

### Bot traffic and quick exits

- **Investigating visitors who leave within seconds (older, 2021):**
  1. Find the pages where many visitors leave right away and test their speed. [L0962.050]
  2. Check the traffic source of those visits. [L0962.050]
  3. If one source causes the quick exits, adjust or pause those ads. [L0962.050]
  4. If the visits are direct or from unknown sources, check the IP addresses and look for bots. [L0962.050]
- **Direct and unpaid visits (older, 2021):** Visitors from direct or unpaid sources who leave within seconds are often bots. Verify by looking up their IP addresses, and do not panic over unpaid traffic. [L0962.051]

### Tracking and conversion events

- **Phone calls count once per person:** When a single person makes multiple calls to your tracked phone number, it counts as one conversion total, not multiple conversions. [L0234.005]
- **Revenue value for calls is optional:** Assigning a revenue value to phone call conversions is optional; you can leave this field empty. [L0234.009]
- **Bundle discounts skip the add-to-cart event:** Skipping the add to cart step through a bundle discount may not be tracked as an add to cart in Shopify tracking, so check whether the bundle path is counted correctly before judging the page. [L0950.032]

### Technical hygiene: indexing

- **Duplicate URLs in Search Console (2024):** Google Search Console may report pages as not indexed because Shopify generates duplicate variant and collection URLs. Only the main product URL is meant to be indexed, so a count like 204 indexed out of about 5,000 pages can be normal and needs no fix. [L0836.009]

## Testing methodology and the CRO cycle

### When to test and what to expect

- Do not recommend A/B testing by default when the brand likely lacks enough traffic. Label obvious, low-risk changes as a "fix" rather than a "test" [L0886.007].
- In the first audit output, hero positioning, risk reversal and comparison modules should each be labeled a test only when traffic supports it. Otherwise, label them fixes [L0886.021].
- Low-traffic stores can run fewer tests at once [L0572.012].
- Do not run A/B tests with very small traffic. Splitting 20 sales into 10 and 10 gives results that are not statistically reliable. Beginners should skip formal testing at this stage and fix obvious problems first [L0390.012].
- Results from low-traffic stores have low statistical significance and should be treated with caution. Large platforms such as Amazon test nearly everything, but a small store should test as often as its traffic allows and should not overread small differences [L1077.052].
- Conversion rate optimization tests need enough traffic for a statistically reliable sample. With low traffic, such as outside peak season, a clear winner can still appear, but small differences are not trustworthy and should not be acted on [L0999.021].
- A large change, such as 40 percent, can be acted on even with limited data. A 2 percent change is too small to trust [L0999.022].
- Low daily spend produces unreliable A/B results. With low traffic, duplicate the product page and product, then send separate traffic to each [L0524.043].
- Social proof and other conversion tactics should be tested once steady traffic arrives, so the page can be measured accurately [L1077.042].
- Avoid site-wide changes such as theme swaps during the first two to four weeks of running ads (Source A 2022, older). Wait until a product page has a few hundred unique visitors before optimizing it, so you can tell what caused a change in conversion rate [L0803.053].
- Do not change a checkout that still produces a good return on ad spend. If a test is needed, change one element at a time [L0963.058] (Source A 2022, older).
- Conversion testing is a long-term process that needs time and learning, not a single app install. A small gain, such as moving from about 3% to 3.5% conversion, can represent a meaningful amount of money [L0390.014].
- A realistic yield is about 8 tests per year. Of those, typically around 3 are confirmed as improvements. In the other cases, the current version was already the best option [L0390.010, L0390.011, L0423.014].
- Best practices such as button color are not universal. The right choice depends on the business, audience and website, so test the option in your own store rather than copying others [L0390.007].
- Conversion tests on real results can show gains of about 0.5 to 1 percent across more than 50 test variations [L1218.018].

### Research and hypotheses

- Good hypotheses come from real signals, such as customer feedback about something missing on a page, or a comparison of your unique selling proposition against competitors. Each hypothesis should name the exact problem it addresses [L1364.007].
- Each hypothesis should be based on data or customer feedback collected so far [L0390.006].
- Test a specific hypothesis about one change, for example "a bigger add-to-cart button will increase sales", instead of a broad goal such as "more sales" [L0390.006].
- Use this template to write each hypothesis:

```
Hypothesis: If I change [specific element on a specific page], then [named metric, such as conversion rate or add-to-cart clicks] will [increase or decrease] because [customer reason based on feedback or data]. Write it down before the test starts so it can be checked against the result afterwards.
```
[L1364.006]

- Each test needs a reason, so do not test changes without a basis. If you already have the lowest price in your market, testing a further price cut has little reason. Testing a price increase that keeps conversion stable can be worthwhile [L1364.028].
- Research step before a test (Source B 2021-22, older):
  1. Collect the customer feedback behind the hypothesis.
  2. List the options for fixing the gap.
  3. Pull the current performance of the page for the same time period the test will run.
  4. Break that data down by traffic type, device type and demographics in Google Analytics.
  [L1364.008]
- Session recordings (Lucky Orange or similar) show where customers drop off and what changes are needed [L0321.011].
- Turn every insight from a page review into a conversion test. Change the flagged element, run the test and check whether results improve. Keep the six-factor checklist next to the workspace and run it for each page reviewed (Source B 2021-22, older) [L0473.030].

### The CRO cycle

- Run the CRO cycle in this order (Source B 2021-22, older) [L1364.005]:
  1. Form a hypothesis.
  2. Research it using customer feedback and current performance data.
  3. Document the planned test.
  4. Run the test.
  5. Document the results using the same metrics as the baseline.
  6. Analyze the results and write down the insight.
  7. Use the insight to form the next hypothesis and repeat the cycle.
  [L1364.005]
- Write down ideas for future conversion tests in a tracking system before running any test. This keeps testing organized and makes clear what is planned, running and finished (Source B 2021-22, older) [L0399.005].

### Choosing the test type

- **A/B test:** Change one element and split traffic 50/50 between version A and version B, then implement the better version [L0390.008].
- **Split test:** Compares two fundamentally different designs or page structures instead of one small detail [L0390.008].
- **Split test for solutions:** Compares two distinct solutions, such as two brand or product options, to see which performs better [L423.008].
- **A/B testing (concept):** Tests one element with two versions to check one specific hypothesis. Example: whether moving the add-to-cart button higher on the product page increases conversions [L423.007].
- **ABC test:** Checks one hypothesis against three potential solutions, with traffic split across all variants. Example: which of three positions for a call-to-action button converts best [L390.009, L423.009].
- **Multivariate test:** Combines several elements, such as 3 button sizes and 3 copy options, which gives 9 combinations and requires a much larger sample size. It is the most complicated method [L390.009, L423.010].
- Note on the multivariate example: the unit's own example gives 9 combinations for 3 options and 2 variants. The stated rule (options multiplied by variants) gives 6 for those numbers. Check the count before using it [L423.010].
- **Simultaneous split (A/B), preferred:** Show both versions at the same time to split traffic evenly. Comparing a page before and after a change can give false conclusions because of differences in weekday mix and traffic sources (Source B 2021-22, older) [L1364.014].
- **Consecutive test:** Compares a fixed period before a change with a period after it, such as two four-week blocks. It is simpler to run but more exposed to other changes, so it is weaker than a split test, though still better than no testing (Source B 2021-22, older) [L1364.015].
- **Consecutive procedure (Source A 2019, older):**
  1. Pick one element to test, such as a bonus offer or the button text.
  2. Run version A for one week with enough traffic.
  3. Run version B the following week and compare the results.
  Use this only when enough visitors come in, because time-based testing is less reliable [L356.042].
- **Page-builder split test (Source A 2019, older):** Changes the content on the same URL and shows each variation to about 50 percent of visitors, instead of splitting URLs. Typical test items are offers, bonus items and button text [L356.043].
- Setting up a simultaneous A/B test (Source B 2021-22, older) [L1364.016]:
  1. Create two identical versions of the page.
  2. Make only the planned change in one version.
  3. Use a tool that alternates visitors evenly between the two versions.
  4. Collect and compare the results for both versions at the end.
- Use the tool to test a new Shopify theme against the current one before all traffic switches over. This shows whether the new theme converts better or worse first (Source B 2021-22, older) [L1364.049].

### Keeping variables isolated

- Test one single change at a time on a page, so any difference in results can be traced to that change. Testing several changes together makes results confusing (Source B 2021-22, older) [L1364.017].
- Test one variable at a time. For example, test an offer by keeping the same creative and audience, then compare the conversion rate to the existing one [L1315.044].
- When the target audience and angle are known, test one page element at a time. For example, test the headline first for about five days, then the product pictures, then the brag bar. Changing several elements at once makes results hard to attribute [L387.014].
- Test several different marketing angles together only when the audiences and angles are truly different. Example: gaming, home decor and a therapeutic angle for the same product, to show which audience converts best without separate ads [L387.013, L387.014].
- Group small changes into one concept when they belong to the same part of a page, such as the area around the add-to-cart button. Testing every combination of small elements one by one can take six months or more, which is too slow (Source B 2021-22, older) [L1364.018].
- Example: If a product page has four elements around the add-to-cart button that may distract buyers, remove them together as one concept test. If the concept wins, refine it later rather than testing each element alone (Source B 2021-22, older) [L1364.019].
- Never run two concept tests on the same page at the same time, for example a change to the add-to-cart area and a change to the product description. Concepts can run in parallel on different pages (Source B 2021-22, older) [L1364.020].
- Stores with many product pages can run many tests at once, one per page. Stores selling one or two products have fewer pages to test in parallel, so testing is slower (Source B 2021-22, older) [L1364.021].
- Verify that each variant is delivered correctly before reading the data. Split testing is technically demanding, and setup errors can easily invalidate results [L423.013].
- Changing the price of the live store in Shopify is less reliable than split testing, because it compares two periods rather than two groups at the same time. Use split testing for prices [L999.067].

### Setting up split tests and tools

- **Google Optimize, how it works:** Turns one landing page URL into a single link that distributes visitors across several landing page variants according to set weights. Google reports how each variant performs [L387.010].
- **Weights:** With three variants use 33 percent each. With two variants use 50 percent each. Any uneven split can be set to favor one variant [L387.011].
- **Google Optimize setup procedure (historic; the method still works with another tool):**
  1. Create the page variants, one per angle or version you want to test.
  2. Open Google Optimize and set up a split test (A/B test).
  3. Add the variants and set the traffic weights (e.g., 33/33/33 for three variants, 50/50 for two).
  4. Copy the generated link and use it in ads, bio or organic posts.
  5. Read the performance data per variant to see which one converts best.
  [L387.012]
- Google Optimize was shut down in September 2023, so it can no longer be used for weighted landing page split tests. The method still works with another split-testing tool: one link distributes visitors across several page variants by set weights, and results are compared per variant [L387.032].
- Current tool: since Google Optimize was discontinued in 2023, use a current A/B testing tool for this procedure [L1260.067]:
  1. After optimizing the landing page, collect another 200 to 300 visitors and measure the funnel again.
  2. For a split test, send traffic 50/50 to two page versions, or 33/33/33 to three versions.
  3. Use a current A/B testing tool for this, since Google Optimize was discontinued in 2023.
  [L1260.067]
- Historic procedure: Google Optimize (historic tool) can divert traffic 50/50 or 33/33/33 across three landing page variants. Check results after another 200 to 300 visitors [L1259.047].

**Tools overview**

| Tool | Status and notes | Ids |
|---|---|---|
| Google Optimize | Shut down September 2023. Recommended as an A/B tool connected to Google Analytics in 2021-22 (older advice) | L0387.010, L0387.032, L0399.020 |
| Neat A/B testing (Shopify) | Alternative to Google Optimize on Shopify (older advice, 2021-22). Can run alongside another tool and compare, or pick one | L0399.020 |
| VWO (Visual Website Optimizer) | Reported at about 5,000 USD per year after an acquisition (2019, older). Pays off only at high sales volume. Check app store alternatives first | L0356.041 |
| Google Optimize on Shopify (PageFly) | Install the Google Optimize snippet after the PageFly page builder loader so it can replace page elements for testing. Used to test headlines on an advertorial page | L0999.020 |
| GrowthBook | Open-source A/B testing tool, presented as an option since Google Optimize is being discontinued | L0709.029 |
| Kaching Cart | "Create A/B test" section in the left menu for cart variants. Analytics compare variants by visitors, AOV, added revenue (Add. rev), total revenue and revenue per visitor (Rev/visitor). Finished tests are kept in an A/B test archive | L0560.037 |
| Visually | Test names must include device, scope, element and change (see documentation section) | L0389.026 |

- Any split test approach: the tool is not the test. Testing is the way to measure which page changes improve results [L709.029].

### Sample size, duration and significance

**Sample thresholds (sources disagree; each applies in its own context)**

| Threshold | Value | When it applies | Ids |
|---|---|---|---|
| Benchmark per version | About 10 samples per version. Results from smaller samples are unreliable | General benchmark (Source A) | L423.012 |
| Meaningful A/B result | At least 1,000 samples per variant. In a 50/50 split this is 2,000 total recipients or visitors in the funnel, not 500 per variant | Source E 2025-26 | L570.001 |
| Start testing offers and page elements | 150 to 200 unique sessions, with session recordings to find drop-offs | Source C | L321.011 |
| Judge a landing page change | About 200 landing page visitors, judged by add-to-cart rate | Source C; ads are judged by CTR and CPC instead | L871.028 |
| Measure after a landing page change | Another 200 to 300 visitors | Source C | L1345.011, L1259.047, L1260.067 |
| Conversions in a test | One or two conversions are not a valid result. More are always better | Source B 2021-22 (older) | L1364.023 |

- Sample at 1,000 per variant: a result can reverse once more data arrives. Small samples produce unstable winners, and the confidence score rises as the sample grows [L570.002].
- Declaring a winner after only 1,000 samples per variant risks choosing a version that loses once more data is collected. Wait for a larger sample before acting [L570.012].
- Low-traffic tests must run longer to collect enough conversions [L1364.023].
- Judge a test by whether the result is statistically significant, not by a rough impression. If there is no significant increase, the element may not have been a real problem. Keep the current version or test a different option (Source B 2021-22, older) [L1364.013].
- A/B tests need a large amount of traffic to reach statistical significance [L524.043].

**Duration (sources disagree; each applies in its own context)**

| Rule | Value | When it applies | Ids |
|---|---|---|---|
| Minimum test length | At least four weeks, longer if traffic is low | Source B 2021-22 (older) | L1364.022 |
| Typical conversion test | Four to six weeks | Most online store tests (Source B 2021-22, older) | L1364.047 |
| Low-traffic or no-history products | About eight weeks | Products with little traffic or no sales history (Source B 2021-22, older) | L1364.022, L1364.047 |
| Idea to statistically relevant A/B result | About 6 weeks, so about 8 tests per year | Source A | L390.010 |
| Realistic pace per test | About 1.5 months, so about 8 hypotheses per year | Source A | L423.014 |
| Fixed period per change | Two weeks | Source A; pre-purchase return option test uses about two weeks | L410.011, L955.054 |
| Two landing pages for different buyer intents | About 10 days, then pick the winning page | Source E 2025-26 | L1225.045 |
| Consecutive test (older) | One week per version | Source A 2019 | L356.042 |
| Campaign-level test | About three days of normal conversion campaign | Source C | L1217.051 |
| Ad campaigns | Do not turn off after 1 or 2 days unless performance is extremely poor. Analyze around day 3, because early data is incomplete | Source C | L280.015 |

- Stop a test that shows no result after four weeks. Record it as not working and move to the next test idea. Do not keep it running indefinitely without signs of a result [L1364.024].
- Products with little traffic or no sales history can need about eight weeks [L1364.047].

**Significance and what counts as a win**

- A test counts as winning if it shows a positive improvement toward the stated hypothesis. A test with no change or a result going backwards counts as failed or inconclusive [L1364.043].
- Use both checks: a winning test shows a positive improvement toward the hypothesis [L1364.043], and the result must be statistically significant to be trusted [L1364.013].
- A test that shows no gain or a loss still has value, because it shows the hypothesis was wrong or incomplete [L1364.012].
- A small change of about 1 to 2 percent is too small to trust [L999.022].

**Traffic changes during a test**

- During a CRO test, avoid big changes to traffic, such as launching several new Google Shopping campaigns that send new traffic to the tested pages. Traffic changes make results useless because the conversion change can no longer be attributed to the page change (Source B 2021-22, older) [L1364.025].
- Traffic targeting is cited as a large share of conversion outcomes, roughly 40% in the course framework. Natural shifts, such as growing organic search traffic and small pay-per-click adjustments, can be accepted without stopping the test (Source B 2021-22, older) [L1364.026].

### Testing ads, offers and landing pages

**Ad creatives**

- Test at least 3 different ad creatives for each product. Do not test fewer than 3 [L0280.013].
- Allocate a minimum of $5 per day per creative. The ideal budget is $5 to $10 per day per creative, so $15 to $30 daily for 3 creatives [L0280.014].
- Judge the landing page by its add-to-cart rate and judge creatives by CTR and CPC, so each asset is optimized on the metric that matches it [L871.028].
- Testing optimization priority (Source C):
  1. Find the creative with a high CTR (1%+ on Facebook, high engagement rate on TikTok). This proves the ad is getting attention.
  2. Once the creative is locked, test offer variations (discount level, bundle structure, bundle price) to find the highest converter.
  Do not optimize both at the same time. Isolate variables [L307.028].

**Offers**

- If testing different offers, maintain consistency first. Do not increase price mid-campaign because sales slowed. Lower the price back to the consistency baseline. Test one variable at a time. Kill offers that do not work. Keep and duplicate what works [L308.030].
- Test campaign-level variants (Source C) [L1217.051]:
  1. Run a normal conversion campaign for about three days.
  2. Send one group of traffic to a collection page where each variant has its own listing.
  3. Send the other group of traffic to a single listing that holds all variants.
  4. Compare the results and keep the setup that performs better.
  5. Use a total test budget of about 20-50 USD.
  [L1217.051]

**Landing pages**

- Build two landing pages for different buyer intents, such as a pre-selected starter kit and a full build-out. Send the campaigns to both pages. After about 10 days, see which page converts. Lean into the winning angle before testing gender-based angles [L1225.045].
- Test the five first-view elements (Source C) [L916.023]:
  1. Create a version of the page with three features or elements in the first view.
  2. Create a second version with five features or elements in the first view.
  3. Run both versions in an A/B test and measure the conversion rate of each version.
  4. Keep the version with the higher conversion rate.
  [L916.023]
- Test a GIF against a static image on the product page. The GIF may not convert better, so test it instead of assuming it works [L1194.026].

### Test ideas list

**Standing list**

- Build a standing list of test ideas covering offers, page structure and product page content. Add ideas as they come up. Start tracking tests from the beginning rather than postponing it (Source B 2021-22, older) [L1364.029].

**Product page**

- Headlines: Run split tests on different headlines for the same product and compare time on page and conversion rate. Other copy elements can be tested the same way. Headlines should not be written once and never revisited (Source B 2021-22, older) [L0416.011].
- Advertorial headlines: A headline test can show which headline grabs the most attention, for example a "10 reasons why" headline on a list-style advertorial followed by a sales page [L0999.068].
- Testable page elements include product descriptions, product titles, product images, opt-in forms, product prices where pricing can be changed, collection page content, blog page layout, homepage layout and entire theme versions (Source B 2021-22, older) [L1364.027].
- Product image emphasis: The three elements with the largest effect on conversion to test are images, headlines and colors. Focus tests on these before smaller changes [L570.008].
- Customer reviews moved directly above the add-to-cart button: a testable concept. A suitable hypothesis is that this raises add-to-cart clicks and conversion rate, because shoppers see social proof at the moment they decide to buy (Source B 2021-22, older) [L1364.048].
- Product page USP: A product page USP test can run for one month. Record the baseline beforehand and run no other test on that page during that month. If the new USP shows no significant gain, or converts worse than the original USP on the same page, the original is judged stronger, and the result is still recorded as an insight (Source B 2021-22, older) [L1364.050].
- Button placement: Testing a button higher on the page is an example of A/B testing one element [L423.007].
- Button color: Not universal; test in your own store [L390.007].
- Product page layouts: Measure conversion rate after each layout change to see whether the new layout works [L1164.047].
- Ebook cover designs: Test several cover designs for the same product to see which converts best, instead of committing to the first cover. Change only the cover between tests [L513.042].
- Trust elements such as star ratings: Test one position at a time, for example lower on the page, directly under the product name, or next to the price. Keep the placement that converts best, because differences from a single element can be measurable [L1218.037].
- Social proof and page improvements: test once steady traffic arrives [L1077.042].

**Homepage and first view**

- Testing ideas for the first audit output: hero positioning, risk reversal and comparison modules. Label each as a test only when traffic supports it, otherwise as a fix [L886.021].
- Five first-view elements: see the procedure in the section on ads, offers and landing pages [L916.023].

**Offers, pricing and pop-ups**

- Discount values: A 5% discount is only a starting point. Edit the expiring coupon section over time and compare results, since 5% is not a proven best value [L1192.020].
- Opt-in pop-up offers: Compare a pop-up offering 15% off the first order with one offering a free set of examples. The winning version is the one that earns more money, which can differ from the one with more sign-ups [L572.011].
- Price changes: Testing a price increase that keeps conversion stable can be worthwhile. Testing a further price cut has little reason if you already have the lowest price in your market [L1364.028].
- Pre-purchase return option (procedure) [L955.054]:
  1. Split incoming shoppers 50/50 and show the pre-purchase return option to only one group.
  2. Keep the other group on a normal cart and checkout with no return option.
  3. Run the test for about two weeks, recording checkout conversion, cart abandonment and AOV for both groups.
  4. If the option group does not lose conversion and AOV rises, test two fee levels such as $2.99 and $3.99 and compare uptake.
  5. Roll out the fee that gives the best uptake without a drop in conversion.
  [L955.054]
- Postage-paid reply cards or envelopes: Test with and without them before you commit. The author found they rarely improve response enough to justify the cost [L1370.094].

**Cart and checkout**

- Do not change a checkout that still produces a good return on ad spend [L963.058] (Source A 2022, older).
- Exit-intent offer: Example of a candidate test to log as an idea. Replace an exit-intent offer of a percent-off discount code with a short product-finder quiz that routes visitors to the product type that suits them. Writing the idea into the changelog before testing it means it can be found again later, so it is not forgotten or retested blindly [L410.013].

**Price testing**

- Use split testing for prices, not live price changes in Shopify [L999.067].

### Analysis and deciding the winner

- Analysis after a test (Source B 2021-22, older) [L1364.011]:
  1. Record the outcomes for the test period using the same metrics as the original baseline.
  2. Ask what changed and what it means.
  3. Ask what the result implies for the rest of the business and for future tests.
  4. Decide the next step: a new test, a refined version of the winner, or a move to another page element.
  [L1364.011]
- Customer feedback that appears during the test is part of the result and can form a new hypothesis, such as the idea being right but the execution being wrong (Source B 2021-22, older) [L1364.012].
- Decisions should wait for more data where possible, because the confidence score rises as the sample grows [L570.002].
- For sales-based tests, the winner is the variant that earns more money, which can differ from the one with more sign-ups [L572.011].
- Expected outcome: changing landing page elements based on testing produced conversion differences of about 0.5 to 1 percent across more than 50 test variations [L1218.018].

### Documentation, changelogs and naming

- Making unrecorded site changes on impulse makes it impossible to tell later whether a change helped, hurt or did nothing. Every change should be planned, recorded before it goes live and measured (Source B 2021-22, older) [L1364.001].
- Document each test before it starts, covering (Source B 2021-22, older) [L1364.009]:
  - what is being tested,
  - the details of the change,
  - how the test will be implemented,
  - the baseline performance,
  - the planned start and end dates,
  - the duration.

  Keep the record in the same place as the results. [L1364.009]
- Store every test record for as long as the business runs, not only until the test ends. Old records prevent repeating a failed test and can later inspire a modified test with new insight (Source B 2021-22, older) [L1364.010].
- Changelog: Change tests every one to two weeks is a typical rhythm for active store owners, so a changelog becomes necessary early on. Log each change with its date and platform so that the many simultaneous tweaks can be separated later [L410.012].
- Test naming: Name every Visually test with its device, scope, element and change, for example "mobile, site-wide, promo bar, text color" or "mobile BDP social proof". Clear names make the test report readable when several experiments run at the same time [L389.026].

### Ongoing cadence

- After a win is achieved and deployed, keep testing. Continue testing different recommendation ranking logic, design variations and customer segments to find further improvements beyond the initial test [L197.024].
- Test conversion changes one at a time and expect a result over a fixed period, such as two weeks. Run each change long enough to measure a clear result before adding the next change, so the cause of any sales movement is identifiable [L410.011].
- Change tests every one to two weeks is a typical rhythm for active store owners [L410.012].
- Plan testing as a long-term effort. A realistic pace is about 1.5 months per test, which allows roughly 8 hypotheses over one year [L423.014].
- Of about 8 hypotheses tested per year, typically around 3 are confirmed as improvements [L390.011].
- Stores with many product pages can run many tests at once, one per page (Source B 2021-22, older) [L1364.021].
- Never run two concept tests on the same page at the same time [L1364.020].
- Do not change traffic sources during a test. Accept natural shifts only [L1364.025, L1364.026].
- Do not run A/B tests with very small traffic. Focus on fixing obvious problems first [L390.012].
- Keep a standing list of test ideas and add to it as they come up [L1364.029].

#### Further details

- A/B testing should be iterative and ongoing: after a win is achieved and deployed, continue testing different recommendation ranking logic, design variations, and customer segments to identify further improvements beyond the initial test. [L0197.024]

- Do not turn off ad campaigns after 1 or 2 days unless performance is extremely poor; analyze performance around day 3, not before, because early data is incomplete. [L0280.015]

- Testing optimization priority: (1) Find creative with high CTR (1%+ on Facebook, high engagement rate on TikTok), this proves ad is getting attention. (2) Once creative is locked, test offer variations (discount level, bundle structure, bundle price) to find highest converter. Don't optimize both simultaneously, isolate variables. [L0307.028]

- If testing different offers, maintain consistency first. Don't increase price mid-campaign because sales slowed-lower the price back to consistency baseline. Test one variable at a time. Kill offers that don't work; keep and duplicate what works. [L0308.030]

- 1. Pick one element to test, such as a bonus offer or the button text. 2. Run version A for one week with enough traffic. 3. Run version B the following week and compare the results. Use this only when enough visitors come in, because time-based testing is less reliable. [L0356.042]

- Page-builder based split testing changes the content on the same URL and shows each variation to about 50 percent of visitors, instead of splitting URLs. Typical test items are offers, bonus items, and button text. [L0356.043]

- In Google Optimize, the weights of the variants determine the share of traffic each receives. With three variants use 33 percent each; with two variants use 50 percent each; any uneven split can be set to favor one variant. [L0387.011]

Set up a split test in Google Optimize:
1. Create the page variants, one per angle or version you want to test.
2. Open Google Optimize and set up a split test (A/B test).
3. Add the variants and set the traffic weights (e.g., 33/33/33 for three variants, 50/50 for two).
4. Copy the generated link and use it in ads, bio, or organic posts.
5. Read the performance data per variant to see which one converts best.
[L0387.012]

- Testing completely different marketing angles in one split test (for example gaming, home decor, and a therapeutic angle for the same product) shows which audience converts best, without needing separate ads for each angle. [L0387.013]

- When the target audience and angle are already known, test one element of the landing page at a time (for example the headline first for about five days, then the product pictures, then the brag bar), because changing several elements at once makes results hard to attribute. Only test multiple different angles together when the audiences and angles are truly different. [L0387.014]

- ABC testing adds a third or further variant of the same element, for example three possible positions for the add-to-cart button, with traffic split across all variants. Multivariate testing combines several elements, such as 3 button sizes and 3 copy options, which means 9 combinations and a much larger sample size. [L0390.009]

- Test conversion changes one at a time and expect a result over a fixed period, such as two weeks. Run changes long enough to measure a clear result before adding the next change, so the cause of any sales movement is identifiable. [L0410.011]

- Change tests every one to two weeks is a typical rhythm for active store owners, so a changelog becomes necessary early on. Log each change with its date and platform so that the many simultaneous tweaks can be separated later. [L0410.012]

- A candidate test worth logging as an idea is replacing an exit-intent offer of a percent-off discount code with a short product-finder quiz that routes visitors to the product type that suits them. Writing the idea into the changelog before testing it means it can be found again later, so it is not forgotten or retested blindly. [L0410.013]

- A/B testing compares two versions of one element to check one specific hypothesis. For example, you could test whether moving the add-to-cart button higher on the product page increases conversions. [L0423.007]

- Split testing compares two distinct solutions against each other to see which performs better. For example, two different brand or product options can be shown to visitors to find the stronger one. [L0423.008]

- A/B/C testing checks one hypothesis against three potential solutions. For example, it can show which of three positions for a call-to-action button converts best. [L0423.009]

- Multivariate testing examines a complex hypothesis that combines several variables, which makes it the most complicated method. The number of combinations equals options multiplied by variants: 3 options for 2 variants gives 9 combinations, and 3 size options with 3 copy options gives 9 combinations for one call-to-action. [L0423.010]

- Each version in a test needs a sufficient sample, with the benchmark being about 10 samples per version. Results from smaller samples are unreliable. [L0423.012]

- Running split tests is technically demanding, so setup errors can easily invalidate results. Verify that each variant is delivered correctly before reading the data. [L0423.013]

- Test several ebook cover designs for the same product to see which one converts best, instead of committing to the first cover. Change only the cover between tests so the result shows which design performs. [L0513.042]

- For an A/B test, aim for at least 1,000 samples per variant before treating the result as meaningful. In a 50/50 split this means 2,000 total recipients or visitors in the funnel, not 500 per variant. [L0570.001]

- A result at 1,000 samples per variant can reverse once more data arrives, because small samples produce unstable winners. The confidence score rises as the sample grows, so decisions should wait for more data where possible. [L0570.002]

- The three elements with the largest effect on conversion to test are images, headlines and colors. Focus tests on these before smaller changes. [L0570.008]

- Declaring a winner after only 1,000 samples per variant risks choosing a version that loses once more data is collected. Wait for a larger sample before acting on a result. [L0570.012]

- Split testing compares two versions of one element, such as an opt-in pop-up offering 15% off the first order versus one offering a free set of examples. The winning version is the one that earns more money, which can differ from the one with more sign-ups. [L0572.011]

- Judge the landing page by its add-to-cart rate and judge creatives by CTR and CPC, so each asset is optimized on the metric that matches it. Use a sample of about 200 landing page visitors before judging a change. [L0871.028]

Test the five first-view elements:
1. Create a version of the page with three features or elements in the first view.
2. Create a second version with five features or elements in the first view.
3. Run both versions in an A/B test and measure the conversion rate of each version.
4. Keep the version with the higher conversion rate.
[L0916.023]

- 1. Split incoming shoppers 50/50 and show the pre-purchase return option to only one group. 2. Keep the other group on a normal cart and checkout with no return option. 3. Run the test for about two weeks, recording checkout conversion, cart abandonment and AOV for both groups. 4. If the option group does not lose conversion and AOV rises, test two fee levels such as $2.99 and $3.99 and compare uptake. 5. Roll out the fee that gives the best uptake without a drop in conversion. [L0955.054]

- Price testing in Shopify by changing the price of the live store is less reliable than split testing, because it compares two periods rather than two groups at the same time. Use split testing for prices. [L0999.067]

## Prioritizing and running the optimization plan

### Entry conditions: when conversion work starts

- Start optimizing as soon as the store makes its first sale, because that proves the checkout works and customers want the product. Before any sale, begin conversion work once the store reaches about 40 unique visitors per day. [L1293.001]
- At 40 unique visitors per day, the store should make at least one sale every other day. If it does not, fix the conversion problem before spending more on traffic. [L1293.002]
- Run these basic checks first: 1) confirm Google Product Listing Ads are running, 2) confirm Amazon Product Listing Ads are running, 3) confirm the store has at least 50 products. [L1293.003]
- Action plan from the lesson, in order: [L1293.034]
  1. Check traffic and confirm at least 40 unique visitors per day. [L1293.034]
  2. If traffic is low, confirm Google Product Listing Ads and Amazon Product Listing Ads are running. [L1293.034]
  3. Set up free bonus gifts for the top three products. [L1293.034]
  4. Brainstorm on paper or log into the shop and make the changes. [L1293.034]

### Order of operations

Work in this sequence. Each step points to the section that holds its full detail.

1. **Confirm entry conditions.** Traffic, basic ad checks and the 50-product minimum from the entry section come first. [L1293.001, L1293.002, L1293.003, L1293.034]
2. **Audit the store as a paid visitor sees it.** Run the cold-visitor review, then the full-funnel audit at about one and a half months in. [L1346.035, L1017.005]
3. **Choose where to focus.** Rank products by traffic and work on the products that already get visitors. [L1446.013, L1446.014, L0208.007, L0941.049]
4. **Build the first product page, then duplicate it.** Build the page structure on one product page first. Once its add-to-cart ratio meets the platform minimum, duplicate that page for the other products. [L1194.032]
5. **Run the conversion setup in order.** Follow the setup sequence. [L0393.009]
6. **Roll out the marketing message in stages.** Start with best-sellers, test, then extend. [L0396.034]
7. **Build the test backlog and run tests continuously.** [L1364.004, L0399.001, L0399.019]
8. **Hold the weekly routine.** [L1293.005, L1088.060, L0390.051]
9. **Change scale or strategy only on evidence.** Use the scaling, cut and maintenance rules. [L0686.012, L0744.053, L1228.008, L0639.046]

### Store audit procedures

#### Cold-visitor review

*Older (2021-22)? No. Source: Source A, undated.*

Review a store the way a cold visitor from a paid ad sees it:

1. Note the product and offer promised by the ad that brought the visitor. [L1346.035]
2. Open the homepage and check within a few seconds whether it shows the same product type, and whether a stranger can tell what the store sells. [L1346.035]
3. Open a product page and check whether the featured image shows the product clearly, whether the description sells it with benefits, features and dimensions, and whether reviews, stock count, delivery estimate and live chat link are visible. [L1346.035]
4. Check the product page tabs or sections for the description, the shipping policy and any price match policy. [L1346.035]
5. Scroll to the footer and check that get-in-touch details, support hours, support email, live chat link, social media links and policy links are present and point to relevant pages. [L1346.035]
6. Check that the social media accounts exist and show recent activity. [L1346.035]
7. Write down every point where you would hesitate to buy, and fix those points first. [L1346.035]

#### Full-funnel audit at about six weeks

1. Once the store is built and ads are running, around one and a half months in, audit the whole store. [L1017.005]
2. Go through every page and every step of the funnel from the ad click to the completed order, including minor details that tutorials may not cover. [L1017.005]
3. Look for small problems that cause drop-off, such as a missing element or unclear step, instead of waiting for the student to report low conversion or many abandoned carts. [L1017.005]

#### Standing assumption

- Assume your store has issues that need constant fixing, since even large companies have teams fixing bugs and reports every day. Regularly review your store with fresh eyes and check trends in your data. [L0390.051]

### Setup and rollout sequences

#### Conversion setup, in order

1. Install an urgency app and a review app on Shopify. [L0393.009]
2. Modify the content page templates. [L0393.009]
3. Create Facebook and Instagram business pages. [L0393.009]
4. Install Klaviyo on Shopify. [L0393.009]
5. Set up automated emails and actions for bounced visits and abandoned carts. [L0393.009]
6. Apply for a financing app. [L0393.009]
7. Complete all remaining conversion action tasks. [L0393.009]

#### Marketing message rollout

*Older (2021-22): Source B.*

1. Start with the best-selling products, not the whole catalogue. [L0396.034]
2. Test the new descriptions against the old ones. [L0396.034]
3. Optionally run conversion rate tests and compare the results. [L0396.034]
4. If the new approach works, implement it across the rest of the products. [L0396.034]

#### Seven store optimizations for a winning product with no sales

Run these in this order when a winning product gets traffic but no sales:

1. Split-test landing page design or theme. [L0773.009]
2. Test prices. [L0773.009]
3. Test lead magnets (free gifts). [L0773.009]
4. Add automatic discounts. [L0773.009]
5. Improve copywriting. [L0773.009]
6. Improve product description and trust signals (images, GIFs, reviews). [L0773.009]
7. Break down ad data by placement, gender, age and country before relaunching. [L0773.009]

#### Store optimization sequence (alternative order)

*Source: Source C. No trigger stated. See the conflicts section.*

1. Test different themes. [L1108.024]
2. Test different bundles. [L1108.024]
3. Test different prices. [L1108.024]
4. Add a clear sense of urgency. [L1108.024]
5. Improve the copywriting on the store. [L1108.024]
6. Once the conversion rate is good, keep scaling the product. [L1108.024]

*Mistake to avoid:* Do not leave the store unchanged from day one while only adjusting ads. Working on the store is part of testing every product. [L1108.025]

### Choosing where to focus

#### Product pages first

- Improving product pages where you already receive traffic increases conversion rate without requiring additional ad spend, directly improving ROAS. This is the highest-leverage starting point for optimization. [L0208.007]
- Improve product page optimization for products that already convert before scaling their traffic. Better pages turn the same clicks into more sales. [L0941.049]
- Optimize the top 20 percent of product pages first, since they usually produce about 80 percent of sales, and then work through the remaining products. [L0966.072]
- Optimized product pages support free Google traffic, Google Ads clicks and conversions, so optimizing the products already getting traffic gives the fastest return. [L0966.073]
- Start conversion work on pages that already get the most traffic, since these produce the fastest gains. Check Google Analytics for your most visited pages before deciding what to change. [L0972.035]

#### Ranking products by traffic

Two cut-offs appear in the sources. Both are kept here, and the sources do not say which applies to which store size.

1. Look at the product analytics and rank all products by the traffic they receive. [L1446.013]
2. Identify the top 100 products that actually get visitors. [L1446.013]
3. Focus optimization on those product pages rather than trying to perfect every page. [L1446.013]
- Focus optimization effort on the top 20% of products that receive traffic, since these are likely to be the initial money makers. Other products can wait until traffic data shows they matter. [L1446.014]

#### 80/20 across pages and channels

- Use the 80-20 principle for SEO optimization: start with the top 20% of pages by traffic. Optimize these first to boost conversions, then slowly expand to other pages. Do not try to optimize the entire catalog on day one. *Older (2022).* [L0249.033]
- Use an 80/20 approach when deciding where to focus: for an established store of about three to four years, paid search and organic search are almost always the largest traffic sources and therefore where conversion work has the most impact. Compare sources in Google Analytics to see which converts and which does not. *Older (2021-22).* [L1456.004]
- Focus on the top 20% of products by traffic and conversion first. (Derived from the two rules above.) [L1446.014, L0966.072]

#### Representative analysis

- Changing website elements at random based on personal opinion wastes effort. Improvements should follow from evidence of what customers need to see to move toward purchase, judged with empathy and in comparison with competitor pages. *Older (2021-22).* [L1456.035]
- When a competitor comparison reveals a problem on one product, the same problem usually exists on other products in the store. Analyse a few representative products in the comparison matrix instead of checking every product separately. *Older (2021-22).* [L1456.039]

#### Traffic versus conversion

- If traffic is high but conversions are low, improve the product page and retarget visitors. If traffic is low, first focus on getting relevant traffic. Conversion levers include social proof and compelling offers, and retargeting drove most of the owner's sales. [L1077.040]
- When traffic already arrives but sales lag, improve the conversion rate before buying more traffic. A store's conversion rate is never finished and should be improved continuously. *Older (2019).* [L0663.034]
- On-site conversion changes produce small, incremental gains and do little if the traffic is poorly matched to the offer or the offer itself is weak. Fix the audience match and the offer before spending time on these details. *Older (2021-22).* [L1522.001]

### Checklists

#### CRO action checklist (full sequence)

*Older (2021-22): Source B. Google Optimize and Neat are named as in the source; check that each tool is still available before use.*

1. Set up a CRO tracking system, such as a Trello board, spreadsheet, Google Sheet, Asana or another project management tool, to log every conversion test. [L0399.001]
2. Connect a CRO testing tool to the e-commerce site (Google Optimize or the Neat A/B testing tool on Shopify), and test with one or both. [L0399.001]
3. Fill the first column of the tracking system with a list of future CRO tests, based on what you think needs testing after reviewing the material. [L0399.001]
4. Review your competition analysis, covering competitors' offers and USPs. [L0399.001]
5. Compare your current offer with competitors and judge where you stand in your market. [L0399.001]
6. Improve the offer toward a strong C.U.R.V. offer where you are behind, and record it in the curve offer builder. [L0399.001]
7. Show the curve offer on the home page, product page and About Us page at minimum. [L0399.001]
8. Review product descriptions against the marketing message parts. [L0399.001]
9. Write a marketing message for one or more current bestsellers, or for high-traffic products with low conversion rates, and share the mechanism with any external copywriter. [L0399.001]
10. Implement the review multiplier call system, share the script with customer service, and schedule post-purchase calls. [L0399.001]
11. Add video review requests to every review request, with image reviews as a second option. [L0399.001]
12. Set up the off-site customer survey and the emails that invite visitors, opt-ins, pre-purchase prospects and existing customers to take it, choosing questions from the template list. [L0399.001]
13. Choose and implement one on-site survey mechanism, such as an online chat widget or Hotjar. [L0399.001]
14. Identify other on-site CRO tests, plan them and start testing. [L0399.001]
15. Review the list of smaller on-site factors and add the promising ones to the test plan. [L0399.001]
16. Keep the surveys and testing running continuously. [L0399.001]

#### On-site factors procedure

*Older (2021-22).*

1. Check whether your store already does each factor. [L1522.002]
2. For each factor you do not yet have or are unhappy with, add it to a conversion test backlog (a Kanban-style board such as Trello works). [L1522.002]
3. Decide when and how each test will run, then implement and measure it. Not every factor has to be implemented. [L1522.002]

#### Checklist of easy wins

- If visitors come to the store but do not buy, work through a list of 18 easy-win conversion tips before making deeper changes. *Older (2018).* [L1274.007]

### Test backlog and test design

- Keep at least one conversion test running at all times and treat testing as a permanent item on the to-do list. Even small tests add up to long-term change, and new test ideas should always be collected. *Older (2021-22).* [L1364.004]
- Conversion rate optimization is an incremental process built from many small, repeatable improvements. Waiting for one rare change that doubles conversion rate is not a plan for growth. *Older (2021-22).* [L1364.002]
- Make many small changes to the store, such as offer placement and wording, and test them one after another rather than waiting for one large redesign. Each small published change can noticeably move results, so check the effect of every change. [L0549.053]
- Store design tests make sense only after the store already makes sales. Before that, use design changes only when the offer and the traffic are both confirmed to be working. [L0388.005]
- Rewrite the marketing message first for products that already sell well. Then prioritize products with high traffic but low conversion rates, since they offer the biggest conversion upside. *Older (2021-22).* [L0399.011]
- Add the smaller on-site factors that may improve conversion to the test plan, and test them after the larger tests are planned. Use the tracking system for this list too. *Older (2021-22).* [L0399.019]
- Many popular conversion tips and hacks change very little on their own. The larger gains usually come from fundamental changes to the store, offer and purchase path, so these should be prioritized over minor tweaks. *Older (2021-22).* [L0598.002]
- Conversion improvements fall into two kinds: quick actions that can be taken immediately, and ongoing processes that take weeks or months to build and pay off. Both should be planned, and the ongoing ones should be treated as continuous work inside the business. *Older (2021-22).* [L0598.003]
- Conversion optimization is a foundational system. These 11 tasks form a foundational conversion system that requires continuous revisiting and refinement beyond initial implementation. [L0040.010]

### Quick wins versus tests

- Split the roadmap into quick wins (fixes to do now, such as making a guarantee, free shipping or free gifts visible) and testing ideas (hero positioning, risk reversal, comparison modules), and tag each with the metric it should move. [L0886.020]
- In an example 30-day roadmap, the first block (days 1 to 3) was labelled a quick win with high impact. The days 1 to 5 block included rewriting the homepage hero to explain the premium tool, its technology and its benefits (less damage, less frizz, less styling time); adding a comparison section against standard hot tools near the homepage and product page; and making the guarantee, free shipping and free gifts visible, which was rated low effort. The metrics to improve were homepage conversion rate, product page engagement, add-to-cart and checkout start. [L0886.035]

### 30-day CRO roadmap

#### Prompt

Use this prompt to turn a store review into a plan:

```
Turn this review into a 30-day CRO plan. Rank each step by expected impact, amount of work, and how confident we are it will succeed. Separate immediate improvements from experiments, and name the metric each change is meant to move.
```

[L0886.004]

#### Roadmap layout

The roadmap has these columns: Timing, Priority, Impact, Confidence, Metric to Improve, with rows of actions. The example timings are below. Cells marked "not stated" are not given in the source.

| Timing | Priority | Impact | Confidence | Metric to Improve |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Days 1-3 | Quick win | High | not stated | not stated |
| Days 1-5 | not stated | not stated | not stated | Homepage CVR, product page engagement, ATC, checkout start |
| Days 4-7 | not stated | not stated | not stated | not stated |
| Week 2 | not stated | not stated | not stated | not stated |
| Week 3 | not stated | not stated | not stated | not stated |
| Week 4 | not stated | not stated | not stated | not stated |

[L0886.019]

Metrics named in the roadmap include CVR (homepage and product page), PDP engagement, ATC (add to cart) and checkout start. [L0886.019]

Example content for the days 1 to 5 block is in the quick wins section. [L0886.035]

### Weekly routine and ongoing maintenance

#### Weekly cycle

- Repeat the full optimization process every week, for example every Monday, instead of doing it once, because a store either grows or slips backwards. [L1293.005]
- Conversion optimization is an ongoing process. After the initial setup, keep checking how ads and pages perform over time and review each optimization area again. *Older (2020).* [L1088.060]
- Monitor ad spend every two weeks (self-auditing), identify ads and products not generating profitable sales, and verify ROAS is stable or improving. *Older (2020).* [L0245.043]

#### Reading trends

- A small, short-term dip in traffic or sales is not always a problem. Check whether it continues into the next weeks before concluding something is broken. *Older (2020).* [L1088.061]
- Slow sales periods are a good time to work on conversion rate optimization, adding products and suppliers, improving content, and expanding sales channels. The aim is to be ready when orders return at a higher rate. *Older (2021).* [L0801.117]

#### Measuring compounded gains

- Improving each funnel step by 10% compounds: 1.1 x 1.1 x 1.1 = 1.331, so three separate 10% gains give about 33% total, not 30%. Funnel steps to optimize include the visit to next-page rate, the add-to-cart rate, the checkout click rate, abandoned-cart recovery, and pre-purchase emails. [L0572.013]

### Dashboard organization

- Organize the dashboard with decision metrics on the left, top-of-funnel metrics in the middle, and bottom-of-funnel metrics on the right. This structure allows you to see everything that drives daily decisions without scrolling, then use the funnel metrics (reach, impressions, link clicks, add to carts, checkouts, purchases) to quickly identify where drop-offs occur in the customer journey. Everything you need to make decisions should fit on one screen. [L0290.009]

### CRO team and agency compensation

- CRO agency success is harder to measure than paid media because conversion improvement is influenced by many factors. Structure compensation as base plus incentive tied to incremental revenue or conversion rate improvement, but beware revenue share percentages that favor the agency. [L0217.036]

### Ad-level and product-level cuts

#### First 24 hours (ad level)

- Within the first 24 hours, evaluate individual ads within the ad set (not the ad set level). Turn off any single ad that has spent $10 or more, shows a cost per unique link click (CPC) of $3.50 or higher, and has generated no add-to-cart events or purchases, because such CPC is not sustainable long-term. [L0229.003]

#### Product break-even (product level)

- A product with no sales in its first two days can still become profitable after the landing page or offer is fixed and the ads have collected more data. Cut a product at the pre-set break-even limit rather than at the first disappointing day, and change one weak funnel stage at a time. [L0639.046]
- Changing the ad budget at product level: monitor, cut, redirect and verify. (See the reallocation procedure below.) [L0245.043]

#### Reallocating budget from losers

*Older (2020): monitoring every two weeks.*

1. Monitor ad spend every two weeks (self-auditing). [L0245.043]
2. Identify ads and products not generating profitable sales. [L0245.043]
3. Cut spend from underperforming products entirely. [L0245.043]
4. Redirect that budget to products that are converting or have historically converted. [L0245.043]
5. Verify return on ad spend (ROAS) is stable or improving. [L0245.043]

#### Recovering a store that almost breaks even

*Older (2022).* To improve a store that is almost breaking even after several months of ads:

1. Review all ad and analytics data to see which ads and products lead to the best results. [L0803.056]
2. Identify losing products because of low margin, supplier issues or high ad cost. [L0803.056]
3. Stop advertising losers, renegotiate with the supplier, or focus only on organic traffic for them. [L0803.056]
4. Reinvest the cut budget into the top 20 percent of products and optimize those product pages for higher conversion. [L0803.056]

#### Launch budgets

- Pouring large budgets into untested campaigns is a mistake. Start with small budgets to see if a campaign is profitable before scaling, and if results are poor, delete and restart the campaign rather than modifying it extensively. *Older (2020).* [L0210.027]

### Scaling and changing strategy

#### Scaling checks

- Cost structure example: £15 cost per £45 sale equals 33% of revenue spent on ads. Use this ratio when calculating whether to double budget during 4-hour scaling checks. [L0308.011]

#### Raising bids

- To justify a higher bid while keeping your target profit per sale, you must first raise the conversion rate. Confirm that the new bid is profitable at your current conversion rate before increasing it. *Older (2021-22).* [L0686.012]

#### Google Shopping ceiling

- Once the profitable Google Shopping limit is reached, raising conversion rate is the only remaining lever inside that channel, and it also has a ceiling. After that, keep the account profitable in maintenance mode and focus on other channels. *Older (2021-22).* [L0744.053]

#### Low conversion on paid traffic

*Older (2021-22).*

1. Check that the traffic targets the right audience. [L1228.008]
2. If the ads look good but conversions are low, reduce ad spend. [L1228.008]
3. Focus effort on the website and raise the conversion rate. [L1228.008]
4. Increase ad spend again only once the website can support the cost of the traffic. [L1228.008]

#### Scaling a product

- Build the page structure on one product page first, and duplicate it only after its add-to-cart ratio meets the platform minimum. [L1194.032]
- Improve product page optimization for products that already convert before scaling their traffic. [L0941.049]

### Where sources disagree

1. **Design changes before or after sales.** Source C says store design tests make sense only after the store makes sales, and before that only when the offer and traffic are confirmed to work. [L0388.005] Source A says to start conversion work at the first sale, or before any sale at about 40 unique visitors per day. [L1293.001] Source C also says not to leave the store unchanged from day one. [L1108.025] Apply the design rule to design tests. Use the pre-sale rule for general conversion work, once traffic reaches about 40 unique visitors per day.

2. **Order of first store tests.** One Source C sequence starts with the landing page or theme, then prices, lead magnets, discounts, copy, product description and trust signals, and ad breakdown. [L0773.009] Another starts with themes, bundles, prices, urgency and copy, then scaling. [L1108.024] The first sequence has a stated trigger (a winning product gets traffic but no sales). [L0773.009] The second has no stated trigger. [L1108.024] Use the first when that trigger applies.

3. **Many small changes versus fundamental changes.** Source B says small tips change little and fundamental changes to the store, offer and purchase path come first. [L0598.002] Source C and Source B both recommend many small tested changes, and an ongoing process. [L0549.053, L1364.002] Use fundamental changes when the offer or purchase path is weak. Once those are planned, run small changes continuously. [L0399.019, L1364.004]

4. **Cut-offs for product focus.** The top 100 trafficked products is one cut-off. [L1446.013] The top 20% of trafficked products is another. [L1446.014] Both are kept above. Use the top 100 as the working list and the 20% rule to rank within it, if the sources' stated numbers are applied to the same store. (Derived; the sources do not say which applies to which store size.)

5. **Ad cut level.** Ad-level cuts happen within the first 24 hours at $10 spend and $3.50 CPC. [L0229.003] Product-level cuts happen at the pre-set break-even limit, not on the first disappointing day. [L0639.046] These apply at different levels and do not conflict.

#### Further details

- Product recommendation and discovery tests require strong product categorization and merchandising to achieve their full potential lift. Before running tests on product recommendations, out-of-stock alternatives, or quick navigation links, audit and optimize your product catalog structure and categories. [L0197.026]

## Platform-specific conversion tactics

### Amazon conversion benchmarks

- Conversion rate is a number to track weekly, not once. Even small changes to images, copy or product features can move it. Once conversion is optimized, stop changing things to avoid regression. [L0227.023]
- The sources set the "exceptional" line differently. L0227.023 puts exceptional at 18% and above and says to stop further changes once a listing reaches it. L0227.024 uses a fuller scale: 5% is poor, 10-18% is good, 20% and above is excellent, 30% and above is exceptional. Use the L0227.024 scale for grading and the 18% point from L0227.023 as the freeze point. [L0227.023, L0227.024]
- A supplement product averaged 38% conversion on Amazon, with some days above 50%. Other platforms typically reach 2-3%. [L0227.024]
- Amazon shoppers are in a buying mindset. Baseline conversion is 15-20%, rising to 33% on some days and above 50% in supplement categories. Dropshipping stores typically convert around 2%. Amazon ads are therefore the best traffic source for FBA products, despite high cost-per-click. [L0228.025]
- Amazon conversion is much higher than on a website, but the sources set different working bars:
  - L1153.032 treats 3% as good on a store, and on Amazon treats a rate under 15% as a bad sign. It cites an 8% rate as a poor week.
  - L0227.024 counts 10-18% as good on Amazon.
  - Use L0227.024 as the general benchmark and L1153.032 as the stricter alarm level. [L1153.032, L0227.024]
- Amazon shoppers already want to buy, which is why conversion is high compared with traffic from Google, Bing or Facebook. Items under $50 need little thought from the buyer and support fast purchases. [L1153.033]

| Context | Value | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Good conversion on a store | 3% | L1153.032 |
| Typical dropshipping store | About 2% | L0228.025 |
| Typical rate on other platforms (vs. Amazon) | 2-3% | L0227.024 |
| Amazon, poor week | 8% | L1153.032 |
| Amazon, bad sign | Under 15% | L1153.032 |
| Amazon, good | 10-18% | L0227.024 |
| Amazon, baseline | 15-20% | L0228.025 |
| TikTok scaling target | Above 1.5% to 2% | L1375.005 |

### Amazon listing optimization

- The product page elements that influence Amazon conversion are the title, images, bullet points and description. Optimize each one to match the problems and benefits buyers find in competitor reviews. [L1153.035]
- Research competitor reviews in this order:
  1. Open the product pages of competitors and read the positive and negative reviews. [L1153.034]
  2. Note the recurring problems buyers mention. [L1153.034]
  3. Use these problems in your title, bullet points and description to show how your product solves them. [L1153.034]
  4. Use the same insights to make small product improvements instead of building a new product. [L1153.034]

### Amazon advertising sequence

Run the sequence in order. Do not run automatic and manual campaigns at the same time.

1. Set up an automatic Amazon ad campaign for one week. Amazon selects keywords and displays the product. [L0228.026]
2. Review which keywords produced conversions and which wasted spend. [L0228.026]
3. Build a manual campaign that targets only the converting keywords. This gives you control over cost-per-click and bid amounts. [L0228.026]
4. Do not run automatic and manual campaigns simultaneously. You will outbid yourself and waste budget competing against your own ads. [L0228.026]

- In the short term, losing money or breaking even on Amazon ads is acceptable while you accumulate sales and conversions to improve organic ranking. Long-term profitability needs a margin high enough that each sale can reinvest in ads and still be profitable. Higher price points make this strategy possible. [L0228.027]
- If Amazon ads are already profitable (for example, spending $10 to make $20), increase the budget. Keep scaling until conversion rates plateau. More sales improve organic ranking, which generates additional unpaid traffic. [L0228.028]

### Dynamic product ads

- Running dynamic product ads and optimizing them for CTR is the target for a 26% lift in conversion rate. [L1031.032]

### Service-business websites

- Use shorter text on a service website. Visitors rarely read long blocks, so services must be communicated quickly and clearly. [L0440.025]
- On mobile layouts, page builders such as Gem Pages or PageFly can create side-scrolling carousels and drop-down (accordion) menus. Use them to compress feature lists and benefit copy while keeping detail accessible. [L0918.013]

### High-ticket and bulky items

- Older (2021-22): For high-ticket products, a phone call call to action can convert better than a buy button. End the copy with an instruction to call today on a visible phone number and offer to discuss purchase options. [L0461.013]
- Older (2018): White glove delivery, meaning professional delivery and setup, can increase conversion rate for large or bulky items. [L1109.044]
- Older (2019): Mobile hesitance is common for high-ticket products. Expect many mobile visitors to convert later on desktop, and measure performance with that in mind. [L1275.070]
- Older (2018): There is no fixed rule that conversion falls as price rises. Traffic quality and how targeted it is matter more than the price point. [L1109.045]
